Police clearance certificates are forged too, with QR codes that lead to a fake website: apply only on pcc.police.gov.bd (Saudi Arabia doc: Work visa: company worker, Step 6: Give the agency your papers for the visa (start before Step 5)) <sup>[N209](#n209)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- Reported Oct 2022: workers who went to the UAE on fake smart cards got neither legal status nor jobs <sup>[N210 (2022)](#n210)</sup>.
- 27 Dec 2023 to 4 Jan 2024: 20 smart-card applications rejected in the Ami Probashi app were later given clearance, according to sources cited by Prothom Alo; BMET formed a six-member inquiry committee on 10 January, which reported on 7 February <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. Of the 20 cards, 8 were in the name of Bangladesh Export Corporation (RL-803), on 2 and 3 January, and 12 in the name of Saad International Limited (RL-1068), on 1 and 3 January <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. Saad International's managing director said his agency applied by the rules as always, had never had a complaint, and knew of no irregularity; Bangladesh Export Corporation could not be reached <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. The inquiry found 19 cards were approved from an Android phone through two IP addresses and one from the computer of the Additional Director General, whose ID and password were saved on it <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. The Additional Director General (Training), then covering for the one on leave, told the inquiry he had not finalised any rejected application and that the app had been hacked before; the app's chief technical officer said no one but a system user could have done it <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. The committee, which included the Ministry, Ami Probashi and the BCC, held no one responsible <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>.
- 31 Dec 2023: Alif Overseas (RL-847) took out smart cards for 24 Saudi-bound workers; in the third week of January BMET officials found no matching data on the server, the Director General was told in writing on 28 January, and BMET did not cancel the cards <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. On 5 February 2024 the Director General wrote to the Dhaka Metropolitan Police's cyber-crime deputy commissioner, saying Alif Overseas had taken the clearances by hacking the system; the deputy commissioner said the letter had not reached him <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. A BMET official said the cyber police had been asked to look at server fraud as a whole, and the BCC had been asked to audit the server <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. The son of Alif Overseas's owner said the agency cannot hack BMET's server, never applied for the cards, and that someone inside BMET may have taken them out in its name <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>. Tasneem Siddiqui of RMMRU (the Refugee and Migratory Movements Research Unit) said workers sent without proper checks can be cheated, and the agencies named should be investigated and punished if guilty <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>.
- FY2023-24: airport desks checked 1,391,988 clearances and stopped 1,098 travellers with no or fake BMET clearance <sup>[70 (2024)](#o70)</sup>.
- July 2026: the CID said that after checks tightened, some recruiters allegedly used fake BMET cards to send workers to Cambodia <sup>[N166](#n166)</sup>.
- August 2026: a Civil Aviation Authority deputy director said that in many cases passengers arrive at the airport with fake BMET cards, fake visas or other fake papers, which 24 agencies at the airport, including immigration and the airlines, check together <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.

BMET's inquiry made eight recommendations, among them OTP and session time-out in the app, BCC testing of its weaknesses, an IT audit, and keeping all of the app's data on BMET's server <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Have the inquiry's recommendations been carried out, and has BMET's server moved to the BCC <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>?

**What to do:** see File a police case <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.

<a id="loan-sharks-and-land-mortgages"></a>

### 2.12 Loan sharks and land mortgages

**How it works:**

1. The family raises the money by selling land, pawning jewellery or borrowing at high interest <sup>[N73](#n73)</sup>.
2. In an OKUP survey, 72% borrowed, 11% mortgaged land and 6% sold family assets to go <sup>[N176](#n176)</sup>.
3. Some dalals lend the money themselves: in a 2023 study of Bangladeshi women working in Jordan, loans from the brokers were the most problematic <sup>[N211 (2023)](#n211)</sup>.
4. What the worker sends home may not even cover the interest <sup>[N212 (2023)](#n212)</sup>. An IOM officer said in May 2026 that people often go at high cost on borrowed money and their income is almost used up repaying the loan, so a person needs to know beforehand what going will cost, what they will earn, how long they will stay and what is left at the end <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>.
5. The debt keeps the worker in a bad job: a study by OKUP and IIED said people who go abroad on borrowed money are forced to accept any work and that it is almost impossible for them to come home without repaying the loan, and that with the distance, the cost of travel and the wish to provide for the family they bear everything in silence; 99% of the workers it surveyed had faced at least one sign of modern slavery (low or withheld wages, physical and mental abuse, restricted movement) and 81% more than five <sup>[N201](#n201), [N149](#n149)</sup>.
6. Disaster pushes families to migrate: OKUP's chairman said in March 2025 that when disasters leave people no other work locally the family decides to migrate and at least one member leaves in search of work, but there is no arrangement to protect these migrants <sup>[N201](#n201)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- a loan arranged by the person selling you the job: in July 2026 the CID arrested an NGO loan officer accused of helping a woman take a Tk 1 lakh loan, keeping the money and her passport, and trafficking her to Cambodia <sup>[N213](#n213)</sup>;
- a bribe asked for a government loan: in July 2026 the state minister said complaints allege migrants are asked for Tk 10,000-20,000 in bribes for Tk 3 lakh loans from Probashi Kallyan Bank (PKB, the state migrant bank), whose loan steps are in Saudi Arabia doc: Work visa: company worker, If you need to borrow <sup>[N214](#n214)</sup>.

**The numbers:**

- The OKUP and IIED study, "Exposed and Exploited: Climate Change, Migration and Modern Slavery in Bangladesh", published 18 February 2025, surveyed 648 households in 33 villages of Mathbaria, Pirojpur, and Gowainghat, Sylhet; sending a member abroad cost Tk 461,220 on average in Pirojpur and Tk 413,667 in Sylhet, and Mathbaria migrants raised 25% of it by selling land and 18% from high-interest loans <sup>[N201](#n201), [N149](#n149)</sup>.
- The same study found climate risk raised the likelihood of these households sending someone abroad by 214% (161% for moving to another Bangladeshi town); most go to the Gulf, and a third work as construction labourers in risky conditions <sup>[N201](#n201)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- 2019 (reported Mar 2025): Abdullah Rubel of Padmapukur union, Shyamnagar, Satkhira, mortgaged his family's shrimp farm and spent Tk 5 lakh to go to Brunei; he found no work, did all the household work in a Bangladeshi's home for food and lodging with no wages, and after nine months borrowed again for a ticket home <sup>[N149](#n149)</sup>. In March 2025 he still had not repaid the loan or recovered the farm <sup>[N149](#n149)</sup>.
- Early 2023 (reported June 2026): a Madaripur man took loans from two banks to pay a broker EUR 16,000 for promised work in Italy <sup>[N170](#n170)</sup>.
- Mar-Sept 2023 (reported May 2024): Md Selim of Narayanganj sold his auto-rickshaw and his wife's jewellery and borrowed to go to Malaysia; he got no promised job, paid his own fare home in September 2023 and was left destitute and in debt <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215)</sup>.
- Reported May 2024: five Sherpur, Bogura, men who paid Abdul Alim for work abroad said some had borrowed and some had sold land and cattle to raise the money <sup>[N184 (2024)](#n184)</sup>.
- June 2024: in a Prothom Alo video, Mohammad Kolim of Barishal, unable to go to Malaysia, said he had paid with a loan at interest, and Belal Hossain of Cox's Bazar said his money came partly from his father, partly from uncles who mortgaged the homestead and partly from selling cattle, and all of it was lost <sup>[N216 (2024)](#n216)</sup>.
- July 2024 (reported Feb 2026): a Bangladeshi sold land and borrowed from relatives to raise more than EUR 10,000 for a Romania transport job that wasn't there on arrival <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>.
- 2024 (reported June 2025): Afaz Uddin said the BDT 24 lakh he paid for a promised Canada job was all his savings plus loans from acquaintances and relatives; his wife said the family was still paying interest <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>.
- Reported Feb 2026: a Prothom Alo editorial said an 18-year-old from Chandanaish, Chattogram, hoping to go to South Africa, was sent on a Tk 9 lakh loan beyond his family's means, and that his journey ended in his death abroad <sup>[N218](#n218)</sup>.
- Reported May 2026: Arif Hossain of Santoshpur, Sandwip, sold his electronics shop for a Saudi visa, was cheated there and came home after 27 days with nothing left <sup>[N100](#n100)</sup>.
- Reported May 2026: Selim Reza wrote that Bangladeshi workers he spoke to in Qatar had sold land, borrowed at high interest or left their families in debt to go, and that many who went on loans cannot send money home without regular work <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What monthly interest do village moneylenders charge migrants in 2026, and is land usually mortgaged by a written deed or informally?

<a id="marriage-family-visa-and-fake-nikahnama-schemes"></a>

### 2.13 Marriage, family-visa and fake-nikahnama schemes

**How it works:**

1. A broker arranges a marriage to a foreigner and promises a rich husband, a big house abroad or money for the family <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>.
2. The woman travels on a family visa, such as China's Q-1 family-reunion visa <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>.
3. Abroad, the woman may be forced into sex work: a Chuadanga mother alleged in a 2024 case that this happened to her daughter in China <sup>[N219 (2024)](#n219)</sup>.

Marriage has also been used to take women to India and Malaysia: women have been drawn into a love affair, married and taken to India with a child, then forced into sex work <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>, and many Rohingya girls found on one smuggling boat bound for Malaysia in April 2025 had been married over the phone, a police sub-inspector said; one, from a Cox's Bazar camp, had been married to a man in Malaysia she had never met (see [Malaysia: boats and land crossings](#malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings)) <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>.

**What dalals say:** "the husband is rich" (স্বামী বিত্তশালী) <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>; in one case the family was told the Chinese groom had become Muslim, worked in a Gazipur factory and would stay in Bangladesh <sup>[N219 (2024)](#n219)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- a foreign groom found through "arrangers": middlemen allegedly dealt in up to Tk 10-20 lakh per woman sent to China <sup>[N198](#n198), [N219 (2024)](#n219)</sup>;
- fake addresses, forged papers and suspect marriage documents <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>;
- a job or study offer that turns into a marriage: in a May 2024 case, a Rangamati woman's sister alleged she was taken to Dhaka with a promise of a nursing course and forced to marry a Chinese man <sup>[N219 (2024)](#n219)</sup>;
- a poor village family: the Airport Armed Police Battalion (APBn, the police unit guarding Dhaka's Shahjalal airport) said in May 2025 that some rings, with local dalals, target women from simple, poor village families to take to China <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- Mar 2024: a Chuadanga mother filed a case at a Dhaka tribunal alleging her daughter, married to a Chinese man in July 2023, was taken to China and forced into sex work; the daughter said Bangladeshi traffickers had sold her to him for Tk 10 lakh; the accused include a Jatrabari marriage registrar (kazi), and the CID was investigating in June 2024 <sup>[N219 (2024)](#n219)</sup>.
- Reported Mar 2025: a woman taken to India after marriage said her six-month-old child was held hostage and dangled from a balcony, with a threat to drop the child if she did not obey <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- 3 to 8 Apr 2025: a Rohingya woman from Balukhali camp (Camp 9), Ukhiya, was married by phone to a man in Malaysia she had never met, her parents agreeing on a broker's word; two days later she was handed to a broker, kept with other Rohingya women in a house in the Kachchhapia hills of Teknaf and put on a trawler for Malaysia that the navy seized on 8 April; her family had spent about Tk 50,000 by then <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>.
- 26 May 2025: a 19-year-old woman being taken through Shahjalal airport told the APBn at the roundabout by the main entrance that two Chinese men were trying to traffic her to China; that night the APBn arrested Hu Junjun (30), Zhang Leiji (54) and a Bangladeshi, Md Nayan Ali (30), in Bashundhara, Dhaka <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>. By the APBn's account, the two Chinese men had come to Bangladesh a year earlier, lived in Bashundhara and built ties with dalals around the country; with Nayan Ali's help the woman was pushed by threats and inducements to marry, a passport was made in her name at a false address, and she married Hu Junjun in March 2025 with a dower (denmohar) of Tk 10 lakh <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>. Zhang Leiji bought her air ticket without telling her, and the men took her by force to the airport <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>. The APBn said a Bashundhara house held more traffickers and women victims; the others fled the raid, and police said they had initial proof the ring had long been trafficking <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>. Her mother filed a case at Airport police station on 28 May under sections 6(1), 6(2), 7, 8 and 10 of the 2012 trafficking act <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>. The APBn's superintendent of police (operations) said this was the second recent detention of Chinese nationals for trafficking at Shahjalal airport <sup>[N222](#n222)</sup>.

At least 4,226 Bangladeshi women took China's Q-1 family visa in 2020-2025: 35 in 2022, 1,016 in 2023, 1,378 in 2024 and 1,721 in 2025, according to data presented at an anti-trafficking-law workshop; the figure is not a count of trafficking victims <sup>[N198](#n198)</sup>.

**What to do:** exploitation through a fraudulent marriage counts as trafficking under the Prevention and Suppression of Human Trafficking and Migrant Smuggling Act 2026 (see [File a trafficking case](#file-a-trafficking-case)) <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Are forged nikahnamas (Muslim marriage contracts) used for Gulf or Italian family visas, and how does a family check one against the kazi's register?

<a id="fake-european-work-permits"></a>

### 2.14 Fake European work permits

**How it works:**

1. A dalal or agency sells a work permit for a European country such as Serbia, North Macedonia or Romania <sup>[N167](#n167), [N223 (2023)](#n223)</sup>.
2. The permit is forged; when the visa is refused, the ring attaches a counterfeit visa sticker, gets a BMET card for it, and sends the person to the airport <sup>[N167](#n167)</sup>.
3. Most are stopped at immigration <sup>[N167](#n167)</sup>.
4. Even real permits can lead to a different job, lower pay or no employer at all <sup>[N195](#n195), [N224 (2024)](#n224)</sup>.

**What dalals say:** that approval and a sticker visa will come within a month <sup>[N167](#n167)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- an agency that controls your online application login: one Serbia applicant found the agency had changed his password <sup>[N167](#n167)</sup>;
- a contract wage higher than what is really paid: in Romania, a legal expert said in 2026 that the contract filed for the visa often differs from the one signed on arrival and shows a much higher salary <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>; in Malta, an Identity Malta source said in 2024 that recruits earn much less than their contracts declare <sup>[N224 (2024)](#n224)</sup>;
- Romania sold as a stepping stone: in 2023 the Bangladesh ambassador to Romania said agencies encourage people who go there to move on to another country and process any file that pays more <sup>[N223 (2023)](#n223)</sup>.

**Check before paying:** BMET's free visa check (see Fake visa, demand letter or job offer) <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>. Since 4 Nov 2026, BMET must not clear workers for Serbia without the ministry's approval <sup>[N13](#n13)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- May 2023: private agencies were reported to charge Tk 6-8 lakh for Romania against BOESL's Tk 2.31 lakh, and the president of the Chamber of Commerce Romania-Bangladesh said he had handled at least 200 cases of workers who didn't get the promised job or pay <sup>[N223 (2023)](#n223)</sup>.
- May 2023: a Sylhet man said a broker took Tk 6 lakh for Poland, left him in Kyrgyzstan and disappeared <sup>[N225 (2023)](#n225)</sup>.
- Late 2023 to Mar 2024: Sajal Baidya, 35, of Jagannathpur, Sunamganj, back from Saudi Arabia for about three years, lost Tk 6 lakh trying to go to Romania through a dalal a few months before March 2024, then engaged another dalal to go to Italy for Tk 13 lakh, a relative said; he died under a train at Kulaura station, Moulvibazar, on 6 March 2024, and the railway police officer in charge said that, from talking to relatives, it appeared he had killed himself <sup>[N226 (2024)](#n226)</sup>.
- July 2024 (reported Feb 2026): a Bangladeshi paid EUR 8,000 commission to a Bangladeshi agency for a Romania transport job; on arrival the company was not there <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>.
- July 2024: The Shift reported that Dhaka agencies were offering Maltese employers workers at EUR 600 a month, below Malta's EUR 925 minimum wage, plus a EUR 1,500 "gift" per worker <sup>[N224 (2024)](#n224)</sup>.
- Reported 28 Oct 2025: the embassies of Serbia and North Macedonia, having found fake work permits in visa applications, had issued no work permits to Bangladeshis for two months, and diplomatic sources said about 90% of the permits Bangladeshi applicants submitted to North Macedonia's embassy were fake, with the same situation in Serbia <sup>[N167](#n167)</sup>.

**What to do:** in Romania a worker has a 90-day window by law to find a new job and get a new work permit, and a migrant caught working illegally is deported under escort <sup>[N195](#n195)</sup>. In Bangladesh, see File a police case <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does a Serbia, Croatia or Malta work permit sell for in Dhaka in 2026?
> - Did Croatia suspend work visas for Bangladeshis, and is it still suspended?

<a id="fake-australia-and-canada-work-visas"></a>

### 2.15 Fake Australia and Canada work visas

<a id="australia"></a>

#### 2.15.1 Australia

**How the real visas work:**

- **The legal routes.** For Bangladeshis they are the study visa, skilled migration visas and employer-nominated work visas; the employer-sponsored temporary one is the Skills in Demand visa (subclass 482), in force since December 2024 <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>. These need an occupation on the approved list, proof of skill, work experience, English and an employer eligible to sponsor, and there is no easy, direct way to bring people from Bangladesh for ordinary work such as fruit picking or clearing fields, according to Kawsar Khan, a registered Australian migration consultant and immigration lawyer in Sydney <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>.
- **Experience.** In 2024 a 482 applicant needed at least two years' experience in the same occupation within the previous five years (for example, as a restaurant manager), and the immigration department had proposed cutting this to one year <sup>[N228 (2024)](#n228)</sup>.
- **The sponsor.** Only an "approved sponsor", approved by Australia's immigration department, can nominate someone, and getting a job through one from Bangladesh is in practice very hard <sup>[N228 (2024)](#n228)</sup>. An employer can apply to sponsor a worker from abroad only when suitable skilled people are not available locally, which is costly and slow <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>.
- **English and skills.** Proof of English is a mandatory condition, and there is no way to get an Australian work visa directly without a language test or skills assessment <sup>[N228 (2024)](#n228), [N229](#n229)</sup>.
- **Who grants it.** The Department of Home Affairs alone can grant a visa, and no person, employer, organisation or lawyer can guarantee one <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>.
- **Farm work.** There is no direct agricultural visa to Australia for Bangladeshis <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>. The main route for foreign farm workers, PALM (the Pacific Australia Labour Mobility scheme), is open only to Fiji, Kiribati, Nauru, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Tuvalu, Vanuatu and Timor-Leste; workers are selected through those countries' government labour-sending offices and stay up to 9 months for seasonal work or one to four years on long-term contracts <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>. PALM's official website says no one has to pay money or give a gift to join, and the employer pays the airfare at the start <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>.
- **The Working Holiday visa.** It goes only to citizens of listed countries, in two types, subclass 417 and 462, and Bangladesh is in neither; India joined the list in 2024 under a trade agreement, by lottery <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>. In September 2026 the minister said second-year Working Holiday visas would go by lottery, with 45,000 places against about 57,000 who qualified the year before, and third-year places would be 5,000 against about 31,000 <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>.
- **Skills still wanted.** Kawsar Khan wrote in September 2026 that the reforms give construction trade skills the same points as a higher university degree and prioritise shortage sectors such as health care, teaching and farm labour, and that construction engineering, nursing, health care, specialised teaching and technical occupations still need skilled migrants <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>.

**How it works:**

1. The ring rides on real news: Australian immigration law changes almost every year, and fraud rings use wrong explanations of the changes and flashy news to take people's money <sup>[N228 (2024)](#n228)</sup>. From July 2026 Australia slowed processing of Working Holiday visas, and on 17 September minister Tony Burke announced that agriculture and fisheries would join the priority list for skilled visa processing and first-year Working Holiday applications would again be processed at normal speed; what Australian media called "faster visas for farms" became "agricultural visas for Bangladeshis" in Bangladesh, though the announcement covered only people already eligible and citizens of listed countries <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>. Social media posts then claimed Australia was giving Bangladeshi farm workers visas in 10 to 15 days with no experience or English certificate <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>.
2. The seller takes money in the name of a sponsor: there are precedents of lakhs of taka taken this way <sup>[N228 (2024)](#n228)</sup>. Law enforcers arrested several rings in Dhaka and elsewhere in 2026 accused of building copies of Australian government websites and giving fake sponsorships and "work visit visas" <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>.
3. The papers are forged: wholly forged appointment letters on the letterhead and logo of a well-known Australian organisation, followed by a demand for an advance, and overseas or internet phone numbers so the call seems to come from Australia <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>.
4. The check is faked too: investigators said in July 2026 that a ring advertising Australia and Canada jobs on social media used fake work permits, visa approval letters and LMIAs (Canada's Labour Market Impact Assessment), and a fake visa-verification website made to look like an Australian government site that, when a passport number was entered, showed that the visa had been approved <sup>[N197](#n197)</sup>.
5. The recruiter poses as an expatriate: according to the CID (the Criminal Investigation Department of the police), a 19-year-old in Taraganj, Rangpur, ran a Facebook page as an Australia-based expatriate named "Khaja Mohammad Ali", posted attractive videos about Australian jobs, work permits and immigration, and contacted people through an imo (messaging app) account with an Australian number <sup>[N231](#n231)</sup>.
6. Money goes in stages under fee names: "processing", "online application", "form filling" and "visa costs" in one case, and a medical fee, visa processing fee and document charge in another, through bKash (mobile money) and then a bank account, with an accomplice moving it through many mobile banking accounts <sup>[N231](#n231), [N197](#n197)</sup>.

**What dalals say:** "recruitment for over a hundred posts with no experience", "guaranteed permanent citizenship in two months from a work visa", "no interview or English skill needed" <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>; that anyone aged 18 to 30 can apply for a Working Holiday visa <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>; a farm visa in 10 to 15 days <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- a fee to join farm work: PALM is free to the worker, while people in Dhaka, Chattogram and Khulna were asking lakhs of taka for an "agricultural visa" <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>;
- a visa offered without proof of English: immigration experts say anyone who claims this should be identified as a fraud ring <sup>[N228 (2024)](#n228)</sup>;
- a seller who cannot show a registered migration professional's number, cannot state the visa subclass and details, or talks of a "visa in ten days": Kawsar Khan wrote that the conversation should end there <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>;
- a verification site on a .com or .net address: Australian government visa portals carry .gov.au, and fraudsters use .com or .net domains with almost the same name <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>;
- the belief that money buys the visa: Bangladeshis living in Australia told Prothom Alo in August 2026 that many in Bangladesh think a visa comes for lakhs of taka but a work visa is impossible without proof of skill and language, and that fraudsters now send fake links that look exactly like government websites <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>;
- the urge to pay a bit more after learning a paper is forged: Kawsar Khan wrote that people often reply "I have spent so much already, let me pay a bit more and see what happens", and call again only when stuck at the airport or abroad after losing their savings and homestead <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>.

**Check before paying:**

1. <a id="australia-item-1"></a>**The agent.** Anyone giving Australian visa advice or services must be registered with OMARA (the Office of the Migration Agents Registration Authority); check the name or registration number free on the online list at www.mara.gov.au before paying anything <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>. The Australian High Commission in Dhaka says to go only to a registered migration agent or a lawyer for visa advice <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>. A registered agent must charge only a fair and reasonable fee, give a written agreement or written information on the cost and scope of the service before starting, and tell you the result as soon as it comes <sup>[N229](#n229)</sup>. OMARA has taken disciplinary action against more than 61 registered advisers since 2021-22, and several licences have been cancelled in Australia over allegations of lending them to unregistered brokers <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>.
2. <a id="australia-item-2"></a>**The offer.** Go to the employer's own website, take the contact details from there and verify the offer with them, or check it by speaking on a government number or through the embassy <sup>[N229](#n229), [N232](#n232)</sup>.
3. <a id="australia-item-3"></a>**The visa.** Use only the government's official portal, immi.homeaffairs.gov.au <sup>[N229](#n229), [N232](#n232)</sup>. A granted visa's approval letter carries a number that can be matched on VEVO, the Australian government's visa-checking service, though in some cases more checking is needed even when the visa shows there <sup>[N227](#n227)</sup>. In one 2026 case the forgery showed when the application number and the grant number were matched <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>.
4. <a id="australia-item-4"></a>**The money.** Send no large sum to a personal bank or mobile banking number, and collect a written receipt for every payment <sup>[N229](#n229), [N232](#n232)</sup>.
5. <a id="australia-item-5"></a>**The medical.** Do the health examination only at the clinic designated by the Australian High Commission in Dhaka <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- Dec 2025: according to the CID, an Oman-based Bangladeshi was put in touch through an acquaintance with "Khaja Mohammad Ali", who by phone, imo, WhatsApp and Messenger promised to take him to Australia and took Tk 1 lakh 46 thousand 400 in stages through bKash <sup>[N231](#n231)</sup>. The accused then gave a bank account number to collect more; the phone numbers went dead and contact stopped <sup>[N231](#n231)</sup>. The victim's nephew filed a case at Jatrabari police station, Dhaka, on 2 June 2026, and the CID arrested Md Roni Islam, 19, and Md Rakibul Hossain, 26, at Kamarpukur Bazar, Saidpur, Nilphamari, on 17 June, seizing six mobile phones and 12 SIM cards; it said Rakibul helped receive and move the money through various mobile banking accounts <sup>[N231](#n231)</sup>.
- Reported Apr 2026: Sujan Mia of Chhatak, who said he had been caught in France and sent back, said that when he later tried for an Australian visa, a broker and an agency took Tk 17 lakh from him and gave him a fake visa <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>.
- Reported July 2026: Md Sayedul Haque, hoping for a job in Australia, paid about Tk 4 lakh 80 thousand in stages through bKash for a medical fee, visa processing fee, document charge and other heads, more was then demanded with no real arrangement to send him, and he filed a case at Pallabi police station, Dhaka, according to Dhaka Metropolitan Police <sup>[N197](#n197)</sup>. On 28 July 2026 the police's Detective Branch arrested Mariful Islam, 21, and Bachcha Bau alias Hasinur, 28, in Nilphamari, seizing a laptop, seven mobile phones, 13 SIM cards and nine forged Bangladeshi passports; an additional deputy commissioner said the ring had cheated more than 72 people the same way <sup>[N197](#n197)</sup>.
- Reported Aug 2026: Harun ur Rashid, a businessman in Sydney, said a ring made a contract of about Tk 16 lakh with a relative of his to bring him on an "agricultural visa" with the lure of permanent citizenship in three years, though the relative lacked even the minimum English or occupational qualification for an ordinary work visa; the forgery showed when the application number and grant number were matched <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>.

<a id="canada"></a>

#### 2.15.2 Canada

**How the real work permit works:**

1. A genuine job offer from a Canadian organisation <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
2. The employer applies to the Canadian government for an LMIA (Labour Market Impact Assessment, proof that no citizen or permanent resident was found for the post) and gets it approved <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. Not every job needs one: permits are also given without an LMIA under international agreements, special programmes and for certain occupations <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
3. The applicant submits the job offer, passport, proof of education and professional qualification, proof of language skill and, where required, a health examination <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
4. After biometrics, security checks and assessment, the immigration authority decides <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
5. The work permit itself is issued on arrival, on the border officer's final approval <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

Canada has nothing like Saudi Arabia's kafil (sponsor) system through which a sponsor can arrange a visa: only the federal government issues Canadian visas, and even provincial governments cannot issue one <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. A work permit is temporary permission, never a right to live there permanently; permanent residence can be applied for later only by meeting qualification, work experience, language and programme conditions <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. A Prothom Alo explainer of August 2026 said about 6% of Canada's population was unemployed and lakhs of international students already there were looking for jobs, and that without a good IELTS (English) or TEF or TCF (French) score a Canadian job visa "is not for you" <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

**How it works:** according to the same explainer, Canada fraudsters typically make fake job offer letters with well-known organisations' logos, show copies of forged LMIAs or work permits, send fake visa approval letters, build copies of government websites, use the photos or identities of people living in Canada on social media, and press for a quick decision and an advance payment <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

**What dalals say:** "100 per cent guaranteed visa" (১০০ শতাংশ গ্যারান্টি ভিসা), "a job without an interview" (ইন্টারভিউ ছাড়াই চাকরি), "Canadian permanent residence (PR) in two or three months", "a high-paid job with no qualifications", "a special government quota" (সরকারি বিশেষ কোটা) and "no language skill needed" <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- any guarantee: the Canadian government gives no one advance assurance of a visa, every application is checked separately, and no person, agency or representative can guarantee approval <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>;
- "a work permit means guaranteed PR": this is misleading <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>;
- a recruiter who refuses a video call: in the case below, the victim's wife asked many times and he never agreed <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>.

**Check before paying:**

1. <a id="canada-item-1"></a>**The employer.** Check it exists by matching its address, phone number and website <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
2. <a id="canada-item-2"></a>**The offer letter.** Check the pay, workplace, duties and contract terms are realistic, and that the email came from the organisation's own domain, not a free email service <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
3. <a id="canada-item-3"></a>**The website.** If told to verify the visa on a website, check that it really is a government website <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
4. <a id="canada-item-4"></a>**The money.** Send no large sums to a personal bank account or mobile banking number, keep receipts for every payment, and read and understand any document before signing <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>. In doubt, consult a Canada-authorised immigration consultant or lawyer <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- Aug to Dec 2024: Afaz Uddin Molla, 58, a worker in Kuwait for 30 years, saw a Facebook advert offering "a chance to go to Canada for little money" and rang the number; the man who answered called himself Asadul Islam, said he was in Canada, and promised to take him from Kuwait to Canada quickly, and said the salary there would be Tk 4 to 5 lakh <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>. According to the case statement, he took Tk 24 lakh for the visa through several bKash numbers, kept saying the visa would come very soon, and then switched off his phone <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>. When Prothom Alo rang the number in June 2025, the man who answered said he was not Asadul but Hanif, and cut the call <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>.

<a id="what-to-do"></a>

#### 2.15.3 What to do

- In both the Canada case and the Australia "Khaja Mohammad Ali" case above, a relative in Bangladesh filed the case at Jatrabari police station, Dhaka, while the victim was abroad: the wife on 22 May 2025, after which the court ordered the CID to investigate, and the nephew on 2 June 2026 (see [File a police case](#file-a-police-case)) <sup>[N217](#n217), [N231](#n231)</sup>.
- The chief public prosecutor of the Dhaka Metropolitan Sessions Judge Court said in June 2025 that the CID should trace the bKash numbers named in such a case to find every member of the ring, that no one should pay on the strength of flashy social media adverts, and that people should go abroad through licensed institutions that regularly send people <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>.

<a id="online-job-adverts-facebook-and-whatsapp-scams-and-fake-government-sites"></a>

### 2.16 Online job adverts, Facebook and WhatsApp scams, and fake government sites

**How it works:**

1. A Facebook page or WhatsApp group posts jobs with foreign flags, company logos and photos of "successful" migrants <sup>[N73](#n73)</sup>. At a Prothom Alo roundtable on 22 July 2026, a concept paper presented by Shariful Islam Hasan, associate director of BRAC's Migration Programme, said traffickers now lure people by spreading fake job adverts and posts about successfully reaching abroad on social media <sup>[N234](#n234)</sup>. He said social media is a major tool of traffickers and that research says 82% of Bangladesh's young people want to leave the country if they get the chance <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
2. Contact moves to WhatsApp, payment goes by mobile money <sup>[N73](#n73)</sup>. Dr Selim Reza, associate professor and coordinator of the Centre for Migration Studies at North South University, wrote in Prothom Alo in September 2026 that online brokering has created new ways to take money under names such as "registration", "visa processing" and "document verification" <sup>[N73](#n73)</sup>.
3. When the page closes or the number changes, the worker has nowhere to go <sup>[N73](#n73)</sup>.
4. The tools are getting better: the director of Dhaka University's Institute of Information Technology said in July 2026 that traffickers use fake job adverts, videos made with artificial intelligence, cryptocurrency, identity-hiding technology and secure messaging apps, and that even mainstream media are sometimes fooled by the artificial videos <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
5. Fake government sites back up the story: a ring forged apostille certificates (the Ministry of Foreign Affairs' certificate that makes a Bangladeshi document valid abroad) on fake domains resembling the government's, including apostillemygovbd.news and apostille-mygovbd.com, according to a2i, the government's digital-services programme <sup>[N235](#n235)</sup>. For a fake Australian visa-verification site, see Fake Australia and Canada work visas <sup>[N197](#n197)</sup>.
6. The advert can be the first step to worse: Selim Reza also wrote that the recent warnings and data of IOM (the International Organization for Migration) and data say fake online job adverts are not only a way to take money but sometimes the first door to trafficking and to being used in forced criminality <sup>[N73](#n73)</sup>.

**What dalals say:** "free visa" (ফ্রি ভিসা), "guaranteed job" (নিশ্চিত চাকরি), "Tk 1 lakh a month salary" (মাসে এক লাখ টাকা বেতন) <sup>[N73](#n73)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- an overseas job advert without government or BMET approval: publishing one is an offence under section 32 of the OEM Act, with up to 1 year in prison and a fine of at least Tk 50,000 <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. On 30 July 2026 the state minister for expatriates' welfare said advertising on social media to send people abroad without the Ministry's permission is plainly against the law, that the government has ordered tough action against flashy, fake adverts for Malaysia and other countries, and urged BMET to run mobile courts against them <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>;
- computer operator, typist, call-centre or data-entry jobs in Cambodia, the typical bait for scam compounds <sup>[N236](#n236), [N237](#n237)</sup>;
- a travel agency advertising jobs: in a June 2026 Jordan case, the CID found the advertiser was a travel agency with no recruiting licence <sup>[N238](#n238)</sup>;
- an offer in the name of a government body: in Aug 2025 BOESL warned that a ring was using its name to promise Malaysia jobs; BOESL has no agent and takes no fee until final selection, then only by bank pay order <sup>[145](#o145)</sup>;
- an apostille site other than https://www.mygov.bd/: a2i asks people not to open suspicious links or give personal information on any other site for apostille <sup>[N235](#n235)</sup>;
- a "visa processing centre" website whose details don't add up: the Bangladesh Embassy in Abu Dhabi found that uaevisaonline.com's registrant and technical contacts were in the United Kingdom, its registrar's number in the United States, its company number Indian, and its Dubai head-office building number did not exist <sup>[N239](#n239)</sup>;
- a visa bond or fee paid before the interview through a third-party site: the US Embassy in Dhaka warned in January 2026, "Do not pay the bond before the interview. Paying a bond in advance does not guarantee a visa, and third-party websites may be fraudulent", and said no money paid before the interview is refundable <sup>[N240](#n240)</sup>.

**Check before paying:** check the agency's licence and the visa as in Fake visa, demand letter or job offer <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>. The Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare says the recruiting agency's or employer's details in an online advert can be checked on BMET's website or through the Probash-Bondhu call centre (see [Who can I call for help while abroad?](#who-can-i-call-for-help-while-abroad)) <sup>[N241](#n241)</sup>. The CID asks people not to pay anything on an offer of a job abroad without checking the institution, the recruiter and the visa process <sup>[N231](#n231)</sup>. For Cambodia jobs, see Cambodia doc: Scam compounds and trafficking <sup>[N236](#n236)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- Jan 2023: the Daily Star found 30 Facebook groups and pages offering Cambodia jobs <sup>[N242 (2023)](#n242)</sup>.
- 2025 (reported Sept 2026): a Cumilla graduate recruited through a WhatsApp group advert for data entry in Phnom Penh paid about Tk 450,000, and his passport was taken within 48 hours of landing <sup>[N237](#n237)</sup>.
- Aug to Dec 2025: after the Ministry of Foreign Affairs wrote to Bangladesh Police in August 2025 asking for fake apostille domains to be shut and the forgers prosecuted, two of them, apostile.mygov-bd.com and apostiller-bd.com, were closed; a2i said in December 2025 that work to identify the forgers and close the others was continuing <sup>[N235](#n235)</sup>.
- 22 Sept 2025: the Bangladesh Embassy in Abu Dhabi said UAE authorities had confirmed no visa ban on Bangladeshis had been announced, and that news reports of a ban had cited an article on uaevisaonline.com, a so-called visa processing centre whose information was not correct; the embassy said most of the site's past customers complained of being cheated <sup>[N239](#n239)</sup>.
- Aug 2026: a ring sold fake police clearance certificates with QR codes leading to a fake website <sup>[N209](#n209)</sup>.
- For Australia and Canada jobs sold through Facebook, imo and WhatsApp, see Fake Australia and Canada work visas <sup>[N217](#n217), [N231](#n231), [N197](#n197)</sup>.

**What to do:** see File a police case <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.

<a id="your-visa-is-stuck-pay-more"></a>

### 2.17 "Your visa is stuck, pay more"

**How it works:**

1. After the first payment, the dalal reports a problem: the visa is stuck, approval needs more money, or the worker must pay to leave <sup>[N167](#n167), [N200](#n200)</sup>.
2. Sometimes the worker is already abroad and held, and the family is told to pay <sup>[N243](#n243)</sup>. A prosecutor and special investigating officer at the International Crimes Tribunal said in July 2026 that in many cases a trafficking victim is taken abroad, tortured and a ransom demanded, and that with quick, coordinated action it is possible to bring the victim back and arrest the trafficker <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

**What dalals say:** in a 2025 case, hostages in Nepal were forced at gunpoint to tell families "we have reached Canada; the company has arranged 15 days' training" (আমরা কানাডায় পৌঁছে গিয়েছি। কোম্পানির পক্ষ থেকে ১৫ দিনের ট্রেনিংয়ের ব্যবস্থা করা হয়েছে।) <sup>[N243](#n243)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- "pay after you arrive" offers routed through Nepal, where Bangladeshis get a visa on arrival <sup>[N243](#n243)</sup>;
- photos of a visa and ticket in the passport sent to the family as proof of the trip <sup>[N243](#n243)</sup>;
- a fresh demand after arrival: in a 2025 Saudi case the worker said he was asked for another Tk 1.5 lakh for a driving licence the company was to get him; the accused denies it <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>.

**Check before paying:** the destination's own visa check, or BMET's (see Fake visa, demand letter or job offer) <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- May 2025 (reported Sept 2025): the family of Shimul Sheikh, 35, of Bichhali village, Narail Sadar, allege that two neighbours, Khairul Islam (33) and Shariful Islam (28), promised him a good job and an iqama (work permit) in Saudi Arabia for Tk 5 lakh, raised by selling a motorcycle, mortgaging land and borrowing at interest <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>. He said he was told he would drive a company vehicle for Tk 70,000-80,000 a month with a licence from the company, then was asked for another Tk 1.5 lakh for the licence; according to the family no work was arranged and, because they could not pay, no iqama was made <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>. His mother said he was then held, a caller from an unknown number said he would be in danger unless money was paid, and he escaped; in September 2025 he said he was on the run, hungry, and had been chased by police the day before <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>. His father said a village arbitration failed because the accused would not accept it; his mother filed a case in a Narail court, which ordered the PBI (Police Bureau of Investigation) in Jashore to investigate, and a PBI sub-inspector said the investigation was continuing <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>. Shariful Islam denied the allegations, saying Shimul got the driving job as agreed but could not drive, was placed with another company, ran away from it and works with one of Shariful's cousins, that the iqama was made, and that the fresh demand and threats are false; Khairul Islam was not found at his house <sup>[N244](#n244)</sup>.
- Oct 2025: according to BRAC, three Sylhet men promised Canada jobs, to be paid for after arrival, were taken to Nepal on 13 Oct and held at gunpoint; families were told to pay a local broker Tk 5 lakh and then Tk 12 lakh more each; after a trafficking case at Sylhet's Kotwali police station, a joint police-CID raid arrested the local broker, and the traffickers released the men near Kathmandu airport <sup>[N243](#n243)</sup>.
- Reported July 2026: in Cambodia a worker was told to pay USD 1,500 to quit, and the broker held workers' passports and demanded USD 500 more from each <sup>[N200](#n200)</sup>.
- Reported July 2026: a Cumilla father said he paid Tk 20 lakh for his son's trip to Italy via Libya, where the son was held for ransom <sup>[N245](#n245)</sup>.

**What to do:** call 999, then see [File a trafficking case](#file-a-trafficking-case); holding someone and demanding ransom counts as exploitation under the trafficking law <sup>[144](#o144), [126](#o126)</sup>.

<a id="hundi-and-money-mules"></a>

### 2.18 Hundi and money mules

Hundi is the informal, illegal money-transfer system that bypasses banks <sup>[N246 (2022)](#n246)</sup>.

**How it works:** hundi agents take money abroad and pay the family at home through mobile money accounts such as bKash and Nagad, often at a better rate than banks <sup>[N247 (2024)](#n247)</sup>. The CID's acting additional inspector general said in July 2026 that identifying technology-based money transactions is among the biggest challenges in trafficking cases, and that tracing cryptocurrency, hundi and online transfers needs modern software, training and international cooperation <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. Winrock International's Bangladesh country representative, who directs its Ashshash project, said at the same roundtable that the channels used to move money, including bKash, Nagad, hundi and cryptocurrency, must be effectively monitored and held accountable, and the flow of trafficking money brought under strict watch <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- a rate better than the bank's (in Dec 2024, about Tk 115 a dollar against about Tk 108), with cash delivered to the doorstep <sup>[N247 (2024)](#n247)</sup>;
- a recruiter who tells you to open a new bank account in your own name, or to receive money into your account or move it to another: MOHRE (the UAE's Ministry of Human Resources and Emiratisation) named both in November 2026 among five signs of a fake UAE job offer <sup>[N183](#n183)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- Sept 2022: the CID ran operations against alleged MFS (mobile financial services) agents running digital hundi <sup>[N246 (2022)](#n246)</sup>.
- Mar 2024: the CID arrested five people accused of laundering Tk 4 billion abroad through digital hundi using MFS <sup>[N248 (2024)](#n248)</sup>.

**At home:** Cumilla's additional deputy commissioner said in April 2026 that embezzlement of money sent from abroad and family disputes are prominent among migrants' complaints to the Deputy Commissioner's office, and that a person abroad cannot protect his family and property <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Are migrants or their families asked to lend their bank or bKash accounts to move other people's money, and has anyone been charged for it?

<a id="refund-refusals-after-a-failed-departure"></a>

### 2.19 Refund refusals after a failed departure

**How it works:**

1. The worker doesn't go, or comes back without the job <sup>[N249 (2024)](#n249)</sup>.
2. The agency or dalal delays, offers a fresh visa "soon", or gives cheques that bounce <sup>[N178 (2022)](#n178)</sup>.
3. A destination can close after people have paid: the Maldives stopped issuing new work visas to Bangladeshis from April 2024 because Bangladesh had filled the cap of 100,000 workers from any one country set by a Maldivian law, according to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry and the Bangladesh High Commission, which announced the stop on 22 May 2024 <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>.
4. Many victims bargain with the broker instead of going to law: the CID said so of Cambodia returnees in July 2026 <sup>[N166](#n166)</sup>. A CID acting additional inspector general said in July 2026 that many people tortured abroad compromise after coming home or don't want to give evidence, which obstructs trials <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
5. Some turn to local politicians: the administrator of Cumilla City Corporation said in 2026 that politicians like him receive many complaints that agencies took people's money and didn't send them, and have to pressure the middlemen to keep their promise or return the money <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>.

**Red flags:** a promise of a new visa instead of the money back <sup>[N178 (2022)](#n178)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- 20 May 2024, Sherpur, Bogura: Lailul Haque said that after he came back from Malaysia he went to the dalal Abdul Alim several times for his money, that Alim gave assurances but didn't pay, then moved away from Sherpur and stopped answering his phone; local people said Alim was staying in Tarash, Sirajganj <sup>[N184 (2024)](#n184)</sup>. That night five young men who had first gone to Alim at least a year and a half earlier filed a written complaint at Sherpur police station; the officer-in-charge said the necessary legal action would be taken, and the report gives no case or arrest <sup>[N184 (2024)](#n184)</sup>.
- 22 May 2024, the Maldives: 6,436 people had taken BMET clearance to go since unskilled workers began to be sent in January 2024, and a large share of them were stuck without visas, a number no one could confirm <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>.
  - Monir Hossain of Chattogram said he paid Tk 2.6 lakh through an acquaintance, with Tk 2 lakh more due when the visa came; the visa promised for March had not come, he could not tell whether it ever would, and he was paying monthly interest on a Tk 1 lakh loan <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>.
  - Sheikh Alamin of Chandpur said he paid Tk 35,000 to a dalal he knew, the market closed again only three months after reopening, many who had deposited money were left uncertain, and the dalal was still giving assurances <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>.
- 31 May 2024, Malaysia: agencies failed to send about 17,777 workers with BMET clearance by Malaysia's deadline that day, and after a ministry probe the agencies were directed to refund them by 18 July 2024 <sup>[N249 (2024)](#n249)</sup>. In July 2024 the state minister said BAIRA reported 70% refunded but he could not yet say how many had been paid, and in Jan 2025 the ministry secretary said 81% of agencies had refunded <sup>[N249 (2024)](#n249), [N250](#n250)</sup>.
  - Orders of the National Human Rights Commission (NHRC), announced on 3 June 2024, cite an allegation that in the last 10 days of May about Tk 150 crore was taken from about 30,000 passengers in the name of air tickets, an extra Tk 50,000 each, and call for an investigation and legal action <sup>[N251 (2024)](#n251)</sup>.
  - A Prothom Alo column of 4 June 2024, citing newspaper and television reports, said workers bought plane tickets at three to four times the price with money raised by selling or mortgaging land and livestock and borrowing at interest, and still could not go <sup>[N216 (2024)](#n216)</sup>.
- April to May 2025: Abdul Gani said that after a fake ticket and visa the dalal Shafiqul Islam was to return his money but fled the area from 10 April, and that with no refund and under pressure from his dadon (high-interest informal) loan he filed a written complaint with RAB (the Rapid Action Battalion, an elite police force); another complainant said he sold everything and borrowed to pay, was not sent abroad, and was threatened when he asked for his money <sup>[N179](#n179)</sup>.
- 18 Aug 2026: the Expatriates' Welfare and Overseas Employment Minister, Ariful Haque Chowdhury, said action was being taken against unauthorised and fake recruiting agencies and recruitment was being brought online; he gave no number or names <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.
- 15 Nov 2026: the minister told Parliament 56 agencies' licences had recently been cancelled for irregularities and fraud <sup>[N196](#n196)</sup>.

**What to do:** in a 2025 study by OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant workers' organisation) only 39% of the complainants' BMET cases were resolved, typically with Tk 20,000 to just over Tk 1 lakh, though workers had paid three to six times the government cost <sup>[N252](#n252)</sup>. A civil suit can claim the full loss (see [Sue for compensation](#sue-for-compensation)) <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. WARBE Development Foundation, a migrants' rights organisation, said in April 2026 that mediation had recovered a significant amount of money in the past three years (see [Local committees and mediation](#local-committees-and-mediation)) <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>.

<a id="trafficking-disguised-as-a-job-especially-for-women"></a>

### 2.20 Trafficking disguised as a job, especially for women

**How it works:**

1. A woman is offered a well-paid job: housekeeping, a restaurant, customer care <sup>[N253 (2020)](#n253), [N191](#n191), [N213](#n213)</sup>.
2. She is often sent on a visit visa <sup>[N191](#n191)</sup>.
3. On arrival her passport is taken, and she may be sold or forced into sex work <sup>[N191](#n191), [N253 (2020)](#n253)</sup>.

Trafficking used to be done by force and is now done mainly in the name of migration, through job advertisements, according to Shariful Hasan, associate director of BRAC, in July 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- a recruiter she already owes money to <sup>[N213](#n213)</sup>;
- a job with no BMET clearance or on a visit visa <sup>[140](#o140), [N191](#n191)</sup>;
- any job for a woman in Malaysia: the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare said in February 2025 that the agreement between Bangladesh and Malaysia leaves no scope for women workers to be employed there <sup>[N241](#n241)</sup>.

**Cases:**

- Reported Oct 2020: a Jhenaidah woman, lured by a fellow villager with a housekeeping job in Jordan at JD 250 (Jordanian dinars) a month, said she paid two brokers Tk 50,000, was sold in Amman and was held in a house from which women were sent out for sex work <sup>[N253 (2020)](#n253)</sup>.
- 15 Sept 2020: a RAB mobile court team arrested two owners of Fatema Overseas, Road 10, Banani, on a charge of trafficking a young woman to Saudi Arabia <sup>[N177 (2020)](#n177)</sup>.
- July 2022: a 34-year-old Narayanganj woman went to Saudi Arabia on a promise of housework at Tk 30,000 a month and was physically and mentally abused; her husband said the people who sent her demanded Tk 3 lakh 20 thousand to bring her back, and that she was put out of the house after threatening suicide and came home <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>. He filed a case at Hatirjheel police station on 24 November 2022 naming three men; in January 2024 the investigation was unfinished, and the investigating officer said institutions he had written to had not replied <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>.
- 21 Nov 2024: a Satkhira woman went to Saudi Arabia to work as a cleaner, was put to domestic work in a house, said she was sexually abused there and came back in January 2025 <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>. She filed a case in a Satkhira court on 16 February 2025; Monirul Islam, owner of Lamim Visa Care in Satkhira, was arrested, while another accused, Md Rafiqul Islam, owner of M/s Siam Trade International in Dhaka, said she came back because she was already ill <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- 29 May 2025: the Anti-Corruption Commission (ACC) raided BMET's Dhaka office, and on 3 July 2025 filed a case against four BMET officials and the owners and officials of five agencies for trafficking women abroad on forged papers; it alleges applications were filed with another woman's passport number in place of the real applicant's and cleared without a check against BMET's database <sup>[N255](#n255)</sup>. In a letter to BMET on 23 July 2026 the ACC said it had filed a case in June 2025 against five agencies over this, and that its investigation had found allegations against 52 more agencies <sup>[N160](#n160)</sup>.
- 14 May 2026: a Keraniganj woman made to work in a Dubai dance bar for three years was rescued at Dhaka airport as the ring tried to take her back; investigators found at least 14 more women held in Dubai <sup>[N191](#n191)</sup>.
- July 2026: the CID arrested an NGO loan officer accused of trafficking a woman to Cambodia; she came home on 1 July with help from the BRAC Migration Welfare Centre <sup>[N213](#n213)</sup>.
- Told July 2026: Naznin Akter, separated from her husband and struggling to support two children, went to Malaysia to get free of debt after an acquaintance offered her restaurant work at about BDT 60 thousand a month; she paid more than BDT 2.5 lakh, raised by selling jewellery and borrowing <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. In Malaysia her family was pressed for money, and she was kept in a hotel, then held in a closed room for six days and tortured for asking where she was; she got home on 21 November through her brothers' efforts <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Did the ACC file its case over forged clearances for women in June 2025 or on 3 July 2025, and is it one case or two?

**The numbers:**

- The CID's trafficking unit had 122 cases under investigation in 2025, against 114 in 2022, 86 in 2023 and 100 in 2024 <sup>[N191](#n191)</sup>.
- BRAC's Migration Programme said in January 2024 that women domestic workers keep coming home from Saudi Arabia after abuse by employers, that some who were sexually abused come back pregnant, and that with no safe working environment women domestic workers are forced to return almost every month <sup>[N28 (2024)](#n28)</sup>.
- A 2025 study by Badabon Sangha, a women migrant workers' rights organisation, in seven districts found 27.6% of women migrated with no source of income; its programme manager said many took high-interest loans or sold assets, and abroad face low pay, losing control of their passports, long hours and abuse <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>.
- Women's migration fell by about 41% in 2025 against 2022 because of various kinds of fraud, according to Professor Tasneem Siddiqui of RMMRU in July 2026 <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>.

**Action against agencies:** a Prothom Alo editorial of January 2024 said there is almost no example of action against the accused agencies after women come back empty-handed and abused <sup>[N139 (2024)](#n139)</sup>. Traffickers do not get exemplary punishment, according to BRAC's Shariful Hasan in July 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.

**What to do:** see File a trafficking case <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>.

<a id="when-the-visa-the-agency-and-the-clearance-are-all-real"></a>

### 2.21 When the visa, the agency and the clearance are all real

In the cases below the worker went abroad, or tried to, through a recruiting agency or a broker, in several with BMET clearance <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215), [N256](#n256), [N257](#n257), [N258](#n258), [N251 (2024)](#n251)</sup>. Volunteers of several organisations working on migration told Prothom Alo in November 2026 that a visa or job offer through a recruiting agency is not by itself a guarantee of legal and safe migration <sup>[N182](#n182)</sup>.

<a id="how-it-works"></a>

#### 2.21.1 How it works

1. <a id="how-it-works-item-1"></a>**Malaysia, 2022 to 2024.** Workers went through recruiting agencies picked for the syndicate, and many who reached Malaysia got no job <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215)</sup>. UN experts said in a statement published on the website of the UN human rights office (OHCHR) on 19 April 2024 that Bangladeshi workers promised jobs did not get the work after entering Malaysia, and that many were forced to stay on after their visas expired, at risk of arrest, detention, ill-treatment and deportation <sup>[N259 (2024)](#n259)</sup>. They said criminal networks in Bangladesh and Malaysia recruited workers to companies that did not exist, and the workers fell into debt paying the networks for it <sup>[N259 (2024)](#n259)</sup>. A survey of 357 migrants in Malaysia by Verite and four other organisations found workers paid BDT 544,000 on average, against a government-fixed cost of BDT 79,000 <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215)</sup>. Selim of Narayanganj was put on construction piling work instead of the job promised, was injured, got no treatment from the company and bought his own ticket home <sup>[N215 (2024)](#n215)</sup>.
2. <a id="how-it-works-item-2"></a>**The agency on the papers is not the one that sent the worker.** Prothom Alo reported in May 2024 that government records showed a worker going to Malaysia in the name of a syndicate agency even when that agency did not send him; in several written undertakings the paper collected, the agency actually sending the worker, as "sub-agent" or representative, promised to take responsibility if the worker got no job, although its name appeared in no government record of the sending <sup>[N194 (2024)](#n194)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in September 2026 that agencies of three Awami League-era MPs, of one MP's family member, of Awami League leaders and of a city corporation councillor did business inside the 2022-24 Malaysia syndicate, and that these agencies themselves said publicly they had sent no workers: other agencies sent workers in their names <sup>[N160](#n160)</sup>.
3. <a id="how-it-works-item-3"></a>**Saudi Arabia.** Ali Haider Chowdhury, former secretary general of BAIRA (the recruiting agencies' association), claimed in September 2026 that 70% of workers going to Saudi Arabia go for a short term and that many find no work after arriving; Prothom Alo added that there are complaints of workers not getting work according to their contracts there, and that there is not work at the rate workers are going <sup>[N18](#n18)</sup>.
4. <a id="how-it-works-item-4"></a>**Russia, a different employer.** According to a BRAC Migration Programme press release, 35 Bangladeshi workers dismissed from their jobs in Russia returned to Dhaka on 18 January 2026, and the workers told BRAC a recruiting agency had sent them in July 2025 with BMET clearance <sup>[N256](#n256)</sup>. They complained that their clearance named the Russian firm Mars International Limited Trade Development but they were placed with a different firm on arrival, and were made to do building construction although the contract was for factory work <sup>[N256](#n256)</sup>. Six of them said going had cost each BDT 700,000 on average <sup>[N256](#n256)</sup>.
5. <a id="how-it-works-item-5"></a>**Russia, the army.** Research by BRAC's Migration Programme in November 2025 found, as Prothom Alo summarises it, that the deception begins in Bangladesh: the men left with valid visas, job contracts and the required papers, and were sent to the war after being taken to Russia <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. According to a written complaint that the families of Ali Hasan Sohel of Goalanda, Rajbari, and three other men filed with the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry on 19 May 2026, the men were sent to Russia on 7 May 2026 with BMET clearance after contact with Jabel-e-Noor International Limited, a recruiting agency in Malibagh, Dhaka; Sohel's father said the family had been told he was going to a company job for two years <sup>[N257](#n257)</sup>. The families allege that after arrival a group of 30 was kept in a hotel for three days and then handed over to the army <sup>[N257](#n257)</sup>. Rajon Hossain of Jhenaidah, one of the 30, said that on 12 May they were made to sign a contract after being told it was for a bank card, SIM card and work permit, that they could not understand it because it was written in Russian, and that they later learned it was a contract to join the Russian army <sup>[N261](#n261)</sup>. According to the Special Response Battalion (SRB), a police unit, the 30 went through three firms, Jabale Noor, RS International and TS Group <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
   - **The embassy had refused.** According to a letter the labour wing of the Bangladesh Embassy in Russia sent to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry on 25 May 2026, Jabale Noor sought permission on 5 May to send 70 workers and the embassy did not attest it, and RS International was at first told it could bring 10 workers but that was suspended when suspicion arose <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. The letter says the two agencies, unable to get attestation, sent the workers in the name of TS Overseas, and asks the Ministry to check how workers were sent without the embassy's permission <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Prothom Alo found that TS Overseas had taken clearance in the names of 266 workers for Russia, and that the names of the other two agencies could not be found in BMET's database <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
   - Prothom Alo reported that the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry cancelled the licences of Jabale Noor, TS Overseas and RS International (see [Russia: recruited into the war](#russia-recruited-into-the-war)) <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
6. <a id="how-it-works-item-6"></a>**Russia, the government's own agency.** A responsible official of BOESL, the government recruiting agency, which took the second-highest number of Russia clearances at 771, told Prothom Alo in October 2026 that it had been sending workers to three Russian companies, that after one worker ran off with a vehicle and another became involved in the war two of the companies stopped taking workers, that 14 more people were still due to go to the third on its old demand letter, and that BOESL had received no new demand letter for a year <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
7. <a id="how-it-works-item-7"></a>**Cambodia.** Md Shafiqul Islam, sent to Cambodia in October 2025 on the promise of a computer data-entry job, said he made a BDT 450,000 agreement with the broker Masud on BDT 200 stamp paper on 1 September 2025 <sup>[N258](#n258)</sup>. He said he attended three days of BMET training at Probashi Kalyan Bhaban in Dhaka and received BMET clearance (the smart card) <sup>[N258](#n258)</sup>. He said that on 27 October 2025 he was taken to a walled complex near the Thai border, which he described as a scam centre, and that his phone and passport were taken there <sup>[N258](#n258)</sup>. Tofayel Ahmed, another returnee from a scam centre in Cambodia, told a roundtable at the Prothom Alo office in Dhaka on 18 August 2026 that he went to Cambodia with a BMET smart card and was sold to a scam centre there <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>. Dipta Rakshit, Bangladesh country representative of Winrock International, an international development organisation, and director of its Ashshash project, told a roundtable at the Prothom Alo office on 22 July 2026 that information from people who returned from Cambodia shows 80 percent went abroad through the legal migration process <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation, an organisation led by trafficking survivors, filed an application with the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry on 18 October 2026 and said in a press release that many Bangladeshis who went to Cambodia with valid passports, visas and government clearance were forced to work in online scam compounds; Prothom Alo reported more than 1,800 workers stranded there (see Cambodia, Myanmar and Laos: scam compounds) <sup>[N262](#n262)</sup>.
8. <a id="how-it-works-item-8"></a>**Attested demand letters and clearance.** The state minister for expatriates' welfare told Parliament in June 2026 that nominal companies abroad bring in 100 people where there is work for 10 <sup>[N263](#n263)</sup>. He said that, because the Bangladesh embassy in the destination country attests the demand letters sent from abroad, stricter surveillance, monitoring and verification are needed at embassy level <sup>[N263](#n263)</sup>. Migration expert Asif Munir told Prothom Alo in September 2026 that responsibility for approving clearance lies with the government, and asked why workers are sent to countries whose appointment letters cannot be verified and how clearance is given for countries like Russia and Cambodia <sup>[N18](#n18)</sup>. Md Al-Amin Noyon, president of Anirban Survivors' Voice, told the July 2026 roundtable that in the past year and a half 56,000 Bangladeshis went through BMET to 28 countries where Bangladesh has no embassy or consulate, and that there is no information on how they are or whether they are safe <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. He said it is also BMET's responsibility to monitor whether workers reached the workplace and whether their pay and housing are right, and that the state could identify, if it wished, how many went on tourist visas and did not return <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. Shakirul Islam, chairperson of OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant workers' organisation), told the same roundtable that because workers go abroad on BMET's clearance, the state must also take responsibility for that process <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

<a id="red-flags"></a>

#### 2.21.2 Red flags

- an employer on arrival that is not the one named on the BMET clearance <sup>[N256](#n256)</sup>;
- an agency other than the one named on the clearance taking the money, with a private written undertaking instead of the government record <sup>[N194 (2024)](#n194)</sup>;
- a paper to sign in a language the worker cannot read <sup>[N261](#n261), [N264](#n264)</sup>; in one case it was described as being for a bank card, SIM card and work permit <sup>[N261](#n261)</sup>.

<a id="check-before-paying"></a>

#### 2.21.3 Check before paying

Migration volunteers said in November 2026 that these all need to be verified: the real job opportunities in the destination country, the employer's identity, the job contract, the pay and allowances, the visa type and Bangladesh government approval <sup>[N182](#n182)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How can a worker check, before paying, whether the Bangladesh embassy's labour wing attested the demand letter behind his visa, and which agency it was attested for?

<a id="cases"></a>

#### 2.21.4 Cases

- On 31 May 2024, the last day for approved workers to enter Malaysia, thousands of people crowded Dhaka's Hazrat Shahjalal International Airport without tickets in a last attempt to go, according to the National Human Rights Commission <sup>[N251 (2024)](#n251)</sup>. Several thousand were turned back from the airport on 1 June 2024; they said they had paid the company BDT 500,000 to 600,000 and still could not go <sup>[N265 (2024)](#n265)</sup>. Many said the agencies they had paid could no longer be reached by phone, and many who went to the agencies' offices found them shut <sup>[N265 (2024)](#n265)</sup>. Tanvir Mia (22) of Akhaura, Brahmanbaria, who could not fly to Malaysia by the deadline, fell from the door of the Upakul Express train into the Meghna at the Bhairab rail bridge on his way home from the airport and went missing, Prothom Alo reported <sup>[N216 (2024)](#n216)</sup>.
- The wife of Bazlur Rahman Sardar (30) of Jashore said in February 2025 that he had gone back to Malaysia two months earlier after an earlier two-year stay, that while he was working there police detained more than a hundred people together, and that because nobody appeared for the recruiting agency he had gone through, a red seal was put in his passport and he was to be sent home within about a week; Prothom Alo reported he had been in a Malaysian jail for a month and a half after a fault was found in his papers <sup>[N266](#n266)</sup>.
- Md Shafiqul Islam said the Bangladeshi community in Cambodia, approached by his younger brother, paid USD 3,200 to get him released, and that after he came home the broker Masud spoke of compensation, then could not be traced and his office was closed <sup>[N258](#n258)</sup>.
- Sohel's father said the agreed BDT 700,000 was paid in full with borrowed money, and Sohel's wife said not one taka of it has come back <sup>[N257](#n257)</sup>.

<a id="action-against-agencies-and-officials"></a>

#### 2.21.5 Action against agencies and officials

- **April 2024, UN experts:** they said high-level officials of the Bangladesh and Malaysian governments were involved with the networks, that little action had been taken against the firms involved in either country, and that victims who spoke out faced reprisals and harassment <sup>[N259 (2024)](#n259)</sup>.
- **June 2024, National Human Rights Commission:** taking up the Malaysia situation on its own motion, it ordered on 3 June 2024 that the allegations of extra money taken by fraud, of workers sent to name-only companies and of workers finding no work in Malaysia be investigated and action taken, called for identifying the firms and people responsible for workers with visas and clearance failing to go in time, and gave the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry secretary until 10 July 2024 to report <sup>[N251 (2024)](#n251)</sup>.
- **The Paltan syndicate case:** filed on 3 September (the report gives no year) at Paltan police station, Dhaka, by Altab Khan, owner of a firm named Afia Overseas, on charges of embezzlement through a syndicate and human trafficking, it names 103 accused, among them Masud Uddin Chowdhury, former expatriates' welfare minister Imran Ahmed, former secretary Ahmed Munirus Salehin, Benjir Ahmed, a former member of parliament and owner of Ahmed International, and Md Ruhul Amin Swapan, owner of Catharsis International <sup>[N267](#n267)</sup>.
  - The case alleges that, although there are more than two thousand recruiting agents, the accused formed a syndicate and discriminated among them, took from the complainant by intimidation and force an extra Tk 1.5 lakh per person for 841 people, Tk 12 crore 56 lakh 1 thousand in all, and collected about Tk 24 thousand crore from other businessmen and embezzled it <sup>[N267](#n267)</sup>.
  - It alleges that Salehin, while in government service, used his son as a member of the syndicate under his own ministry, and that Imran Ahmed helped the ring by irregularly approving the launch of an app named "Probashi" for his wife's elder brother's son <sup>[N267](#n267)</sup>.
  - On 23 March 2026 Dhaka's detective police (DB) arrested Lt Gen (retd) Masud Uddin Chowdhury, a former member of parliament for Feni-3 and managing director of M International Limited, in Baridhara; on 24 March a Dhaka metropolitan magistrate granted five days' remand, and police said there are 11 cases against him in Feni and Dhaka <sup>[N267](#n267)</sup>. The public prosecutor, Omar Faruq Faruqi, told the court that Masud Uddin Chowdhury had taken Tk 24 thousand crore in the name of sending people abroad and that remand was needed to learn who shared the money and where it was laundered; the investigating officer sought the recovery of the money and the arrest of absconding accused <sup>[N267](#n267)</sup>.
  - He was remanded a second time on 29 March 2026 and a third time, for three days, on 4 April; prosecutors told the court the syndicate traded in commissions from more than two thousand recruiting agencies, which got no work unless they paid, and that every member except him was absconding <sup>[N268](#n268)</sup>. His lawyer, Touhidul Islam, told the court on 4 April that after 11 days of remand the investigating officer's application recorded no progress, that recovering money cannot be a ground for remand, and that his client is over 72 and has had brain surgery and two heart stents <sup>[N268](#n268)</sup>.
- **July 2026, licences withdrawn:** on 19 July 2026 the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry withdrew the licences of 49 recruiting agencies, which Prothom Alo described as linked to the Malaysia syndicate, because charge sheets against them under sections 406, 420, 385, 386, 427 and 34 of the Penal Code 1860 had been filed and accepted in court <sup>[N269](#n269), [N18](#n18)</sup>. Expatriates' Welfare Minister Ariful Haque Chowdhury said that month that the licences of three agencies that sent workers to Russia had also been cancelled, and that BMET had been told to be more careful before giving clearance for Cambodia <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.

<a id="what-to-do-1"></a>

#### 2.21.6 What to do

Taking money on a false job promise is an offence under section 31 of the OEM Act (see [File a police case](#file-a-police-case)) <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>; complain to BMET too (see [Complain to BMET](#complain-to-bmet)) <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.

<a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first"></a>

### 2.22 A flight to a different country first

In the cases below the person was first flown, or in one case about to be flown, to a country other than the promised destination; in several they were held there and more money was demanded <sup>[N270](#n270), [N271](#n271), [N272](#n272), [N232](#n232)</sup>.

**How it works:**

1. <a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first-item-1"></a>**"Visa processing" abroad.** According to victims' relatives at a press conference in Bhairab, Kishoreganj, on 8 January 2025, the owner of a travel agency with an office in Gulshan, Dhaka, that had promised to send them to Europe took seven people to India and Nepal on 12 March 2024 for "visa processing", and they were sent home from India on 28 June after complications caused by visa forgery <sup>[N273](#n273)</sup>. A relative said the owner took BDT 80 lakh from the seven, and the victims later found the Gulshan office padlocked <sup>[N273](#n273)</sup>.
