2. <a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first-item-2"></a>**Nepal.** A Cumilla man promised passage to the United States said he was sent to Nepal on 7 December 2023 and locked in a godown in Kathmandu, where his phone, passport and other papers were taken <sup>[N270](#n270)</sup>. He said he was forced at gunpoint to send his father a WhatsApp voice recording saying he was in Mexico, after which his father paid the remaining BDT 43 lakh of the contract <sup>[N270](#n270)</sup>. The Cumilla man is Tarek Aziz of Khanatua village, Manoharganj, who alleged at a press conference on 1 March 2025 that Ashek Elahi, a former president of the Cumilla South district unit of the student organisation Chhatra Shibir, took him to Nepal on the promise of taking him to the United States, held him hostage there and took BDT 45 lakh from his family; Ashek Elahi denied it at his own press conference on 3 March 2025, said the allegation was baseless and that he was himself a victim of the same fraud <sup>[N270](#n270), [N274](#n274)</sup>. By Ashek Elahi's account, a man named Jobayer Hossain of Chhatarpaiya, Senbag, Noakhali, an acquaintance of Tarek Aziz's father, was the one who sent people to America; Ashek, Tarek and several others went to Nepal with Jobayer on 7 December 2023, where Jobayer held several of them hostage and took their phones, passports and other papers <sup>[N274](#n274)</sup>. Ashek Elahi said that, in fear for their lives, the hostages told their families and his father paid Tk 40 lakh to Jobayer in cash and through a bank, after which what he called Jobayer's Nepali mafia ring let them go <sup>[N274](#n274)</sup>. Tarek Aziz told Prothom Alo on 3 March 2025 that his family knows nobody named Jobayer from Noakhali, that their contract was with Ashek Elahi, that Ashek Elahi took their money and that he has all the proof <sup>[N274](#n274)</sup>. A young man from Jashore promised a labourer's job in Australia was taken to Kathmandu instead (on 14 September 2025, according to his father) and kept in a hotel, where his passport and dollars worth BDT 1 lakh were taken and a further BDT 1.5 lakh was demanded <sup>[N271](#n271)</sup>. He was still missing in April 2026 <sup>[N271](#n271)</sup>.
3. <a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first-item-3"></a>**Australia through a visa-free country.** Kawsar Khan wrote in Prothom Alo in August 2026 that Australia fraud rings sometimes use the Maritime Crew (subclass 988) visa, and then send the victim first to a country where Bangladeshis usually need no visa, such as Nepal, the Maldives, Fiji or Indonesia; he wrote that an offer to route a person through another country instead of a direct Dhaka to Australia ticket is a sure sign of danger <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>. Tabarak Khan, a resident of Botany, Sydney, said his nephew fell into an agency's trap: the government checking system showed a visa, but from its type he was sure it was fake; the nephew ignored repeated warnings, and the fraud was caught at the last moment when the ticket to Nepal was being bought <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>.
4. <a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first-item-4"></a>**Egypt.** A Madaripur man was promised a work permit for Morocco and onward travel to Spain or Italy for BDT 17 lakh <sup>[N275](#n275)</sup>. At the airport in Egypt the group's passports were taken for 23 hours and returned with Libyan visas <sup>[N275](#n275)</sup>. By his account, one of the brokers told them not to worry: they would be taken from Libya through Algeria to Morocco <sup>[N275](#n275)</sup>. He came home after 104 days, after ransom was paid <sup>[N275](#n275)</sup>. One of two Bangladeshi women trafficked to Egypt said she was told she would go to Italy legally: the process would be completed at the Italian embassy in Egypt <sup>[N272](#n272)</sup>. She said she was held there for about a year and a half, that her passport and phone were taken, and that she and another Bangladeshi woman were kept locked in a room <sup>[N272](#n272)</sup>.
5. <a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first-item-5"></a>**Several stops.** A Noakhali man who contracted to reach Italy for BDT 1,200,000 handed his passport to an office in Ashkona, Dhaka, and was sent through New Delhi, Colombo, Sharjah and Cairo before being driven into Libya <sup>[N276](#n276)</sup>. According to his complaint, after he had stayed about 16 days in Colombo more money was taken from his family in stages on various pretexts, and in Cairo a ring member held him in a house for about five days, after which a further USD 6,000 was demanded and taken <sup>[N276](#n276)</sup>.
6. <a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first-item-6"></a>**A country nobody mentioned.** Men promised jobs in Cuba were taken through Kolkata, Delhi, Singapore and Fiji to Vanuatu and locked in an isolated house <sup>[N277 (2024)](#n277)</sup>. Vanuatu police rescued 103 more Bangladeshis, and on 12 November 2024 the Barishal Human Trafficking Tribunal sentenced two brothers to life imprisonment in the case <sup>[N277 (2024)](#n277)</sup>. Mohammad Akram Hossain said a labour-supply firm promised him and his brother-in-law jobs in Cyprus, but in September 2024 their group of 10 was first taken to Saudi Arabia on Hajj visas, kept there a few weeks, flown to Russia and taken by bus from St Petersburg to an army camp <sup>[N278](#n278)</sup>. According to his family, Jafar Sardar (35) of Bara Meghla village, Jashore Sadar, left home on 17 August 2024 bound for Cyprus through a Dhaka agency named "Dream Home Travels", which had submitted his papers to the Cyprus embassy; when no visa came in two years it took him to Saudi Arabia with talk of a job in Russia, and two months later he was taken to Russia through Dubai, Libya or Turkey <sup>[N266](#n266)</sup>. His family said they paid Dream Home Travels BDT 8 lakh in all, raised from a BDT 4 lakh NGO loan, the sale of his mother's and wife's jewellery and money borrowed at interest <sup>[N266](#n266)</sup>.
7. <a id="a-flight-to-a-different-country-first-item-7"></a>**A forged visa and a real flight.** According to the CID, a ring gave two people tickets for Nepal- and Italy-bound flights, boarding passes and fake Italian Schengen visas; they flew on 26 May 2026, Italian immigration at Rome's Leonardo da Vinci airport found the visas to be fake, and after two days in custody they were sent back on 28 May <sup>[N279](#n279)</sup>. The CID said each had a contract of BDT 30 lakh, of which BDT 20 lakh was taken in advance, and it arrested a Biman Bangladesh Airlines ground-service junior officer in the case <sup>[N279](#n279)</sup>.

An analysis reported by Prothom Alo on 31 March 2026 found that people trafficked from Bangladesh to Italy are taken through Nepal, India or Sri Lanka to Dubai, then through Egypt or Tunisia to Libya, where they are kept, and from there across the Mediterranean (see [Libya to Italy by boat](#libya-to-italy-by-boat)) <sup>[N280](#n280)</sup>. According to the SRB, which arrested one ring's alleged leader in Dhaka in September 2026, several victims were assured they would reach Italy through Serbia and Belarus; the ring took BDT 20 to 25 lakh per person, in some cases up to BDT 40 lakh, and sent people abroad under the names of unlicensed agencies, among them "Arzu Immigration Limited" and "Nafis Air Immigration Limited" <sup>[N281](#n281)</sup>.

**Red flags:**

- a ticket to Nepal, India, Sri Lanka or Egypt when the job promised is in another country <sup>[N273](#n273), [N270](#n270), [N271](#n271), [N276](#n276)</sup>;
- for a job in Australia, a route through Nepal, the Maldives, Fiji or Indonesia instead of a direct ticket <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>;
- being told the visa or the embassy paperwork will be completed in the transit country <sup>[N273](#n273), [N272](#n272)</sup>;
- a visa for a country that was never discussed appearing in the passport <sup>[N275](#n275)</sup>.

**What to do:** taking money on a false job promise is an offence under section 31 of the OEM Act (see [File a police case](#file-a-police-case)) <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>; luring someone abroad for work knowing they may be exploited counts as trafficking (see [File a trafficking case](#file-a-trafficking-case)) <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>. By Ashek Elahi's account, the group held in Nepal came home after filing a complaint at the Bangladesh mission in Nepal, and Tarek Aziz, as complainant, accused Jobayer of human trafficking to the Nepal police and to the mission in a complaint that bears Tarek Aziz's signature <sup>[N274](#n274)</sup>. Ashek Elahi said in March 2025 that a case under the human trafficking act had been filed against him and his family, that he and all the other accused in Tarek Aziz's case were on bail, that Jobayer Hossain was not named as an accused, that he and several other victims had complained against Jobayer at Motijheel police station, and that Jobayer and a man named Masud of Kishoreganj were still absconding <sup>[N274](#n274)</sup>.

<a id="proof-that-is-not-proof"></a>

### 2.23 Proof that is not proof

Each item below is something that made a recruiter's offer look real, in a reported case in which people were cheated, or in a 2026 newspaper warning or UN finding <sup>[N258](#n258), [N282](#n282), [N283 (2024)](#n283), [N284](#n284), [N233](#n233), [N232](#n232), [N285](#n285)</sup>.

- **A stamp-paper agreement.** Md Shafiqul Islam said his BDT 450,000 agreement with his broker was on BDT 200 stamp paper, and that he was then taken to a complex in Cambodia which he described as a scam centre <sup>[N258](#n258)</sup>. The Cumilla man held in Nepal claimed that in arbitration after his return the accused signed a stamp paper promising to pay BDT 45 lakh plus BDT 1 lakh compensation and then paid nothing; the accused said his signature was taken by force <sup>[N270](#n270)</sup>. In another dispute, Ashek Elahi, accused in a trafficking case, alleged in March 2025 that on 14 December 2023 a group attacked his house and forced his mother to sign four blank bank cheques and 20 blank non-judicial stamp papers, that on 17 March 2024 Tarek Aziz's father took his uncle's signature on five more, that his family filed a court case to recover them, and that they were then used to file the case against him <sup>[N274](#n274)</sup>.
- **Cheques.** The complainant in a Sylhet UK-visa case said that, after mediation by local people, the accused gave six cheques of BDT 2 lakh each on 21 July 2024, that they were dishonoured, and that on 10 December the bank again said the account did not have enough money <sup>[N282](#n282)</sup>. The complainants' lawyer told a Dhaka court on 15 July 2025 that the cheques the chairman of BSB Global Network gave in the name of refunds were repeatedly rejected by the bank <sup>[N286](#n286)</sup>. According to the case against VisaGuide, a firm that promised to send people abroad on student visas, victims were given cheques and receipts after paying, but no one was sent in the agreed time and the money was not returned <sup>[N287](#n287)</sup>.
- **"Pay the balance after the visa."** Kawsar Khan wrote in a Prothom Alo column in August 2026 that "visa first, money later" (আগে ভিসা, পরে টাকা) is the biggest bait of Australia visa fraudsters <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>. According to a case petition reported on 13 November 2024, Abul Hossain of Hijla, Barishal, made a BDT 350,000 deal with his neighbour for a well-paid job in Dubai, paid BDT 200,000 in 2021, and was to pay the balance after getting the visa <sup>[N283 (2024)](#n283)</sup>. He was sent to Dubai in February 2021 on a seven-day tourist visa, was held there by a local broker ring that demanded BDT 100,000, and was later sent back by Dubai police for entering illegally <sup>[N283 (2024)](#n283)</sup>. When he asked for his money back, the accused demanded another BDT 400,000 to send him to Kyrgyzstan <sup>[N283 (2024)](#n283)</sup>. According to a case statement reported on 21 July 2026, a woman whose husband lives in Italy was taken to a travel agency in Gulshan 1, Dhaka, where the accused made a financial agreement with her that said the full money was to be paid after the visa was issued <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. She had met one of the accused when she went to collect her passport from the Italian embassy for renewal; he presented himself as someone who could get passports renewed quickly and get Italy visas <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>. The CID said that after an Italy visa was issued and she collected her passport, immigration officers at Dhaka airport told her another woman had already gone to Italy on 24 April using the visa in her passport; the CID seized 56 passports and 153 seals from the ring <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **A visa that shows online.** Kawsar Khan wrote in August 2026 that fraud rings usually pick Australian visa categories such as Working Holiday (subclass 417) or eVisitor (subclass 651), which Bangladeshis are not eligible for: they enter an eligible country in the online application to get a quick digital visa, then use the correction facility to add the applicant's Bangladeshi passport number <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>. The government's checking system then shows the visa as granted, but it is impossible to travel on it; people he warns answer "but the visa is showing online!" <sup>[N232](#n232)</sup>.
- **A foreign phone number.** A Prothom Alo explainer of August 2026 said fraudsters call on WhatsApp or imo from numbers with Canada's country code (+1), and that anyone outside Canada can make a call from a Canadian number online <sup>[N233](#n233)</sup>.
- **An office.** According to complaints filed with police in Jashore and Natore in September 2025, a man took BDT 58 lakh from four Jashore men to send them to Canada on worker visas, wound up his Jashore office and opened a new one named Euro Visa Help Center BD in Natore Sadar; one of the four claims he handed over BDT 15 lakh from selling his mother's land and an NGO loan <sup>[N289](#n289)</sup>. According to the case against VisaGuide, victims went to its office on 5 July 2026 and found it had been moved away <sup>[N287](#n287)</sup>. The Uttara office of RS International, the agency through which four Patgram men were sent to Moscow, was found locked on 7 June 2026 <sup>[N290](#n290)</sup>. Police and relatives said Rashed Khan of Charjadabpur, Shariatpur Sadar, arrested in a Shariatpur case reported in February 2025, works at sending manpower to various countries and has a firm named Lamisa Enterprise; the wife of one man said her husband left home with him on 3 February 2024 to go to Italy <sup>[N291](#n291)</sup>.
- **An appointment letter, an interview and a medical test.** Victims of S Alam Overseas, whose office was on the second floor of a house in Sector 12, Uttara, Dhaka, said in November 2026 that the agency gave them fake appointment letters from an Azerbaijan company named Colin Construction and visas, had foreigners interview them online, and sent everyone who submitted a passport for a medical test at a centre in Banani <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>. The 23 victims Prothom Alo spoke to said they each paid between BDT 80,000 and BDT 350,000, taken in four stages, plus BDT 5,000 for the medical test <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>. On 23 October the receptionist phoned to say the company had closed; about 200 people were cheated, according to the victims <sup>[N284](#n284)</sup>. Dhaka detective police said on 31 March 2024 that a father and son they had arrested told victims they held valid licences for four to five recruiting agencies when they had none, and had victims take a medical test after they paid <sup>[N292 (2024)](#n292)</sup>. According to the father of the Jashore man held in Nepal, the brokers gave him fake Australian government visa-grant papers when demanding the remaining BDT 12 lakh, and after taking the money took his son to Uttara, Dhaka, for a medical test and fingerprints <sup>[N271](#n271)</sup>.
- **Papers showing government approval.** A UN commission found that Russia job offers seemed credible to recruits because of the recruiters' assurances, their own earlier work abroad, others who said they had got the same work, and documents showing the recruiting firm's government approval; in some cases an acquaintance had told them about the job (see [Russia: recruited into the war](#russia-recruited-into-the-war)) <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.
- **A visa in hand.** Police in Chattogram said on 22 November 2026 that an arrested ring member admitted the ring offered to send people to Serbia for BDT 600,000, took the money, and gave a visa to win their trust; the visa was forged, which immigration police at the airport caught <sup>[N293](#n293)</sup>. Police said that when the forged visa was caught, ring members claimed the victim had some other problem <sup>[N293](#n293)</sup>. A man from Osmaninagar, Sylhet, alleged on 11 February 2025 that the accused agreed in early 2022 to arrange six UK work permit visas for BDT 10 lakh each, some months later sent photos of the visas on WhatsApp, and then sent the passport back by courier with no visa in it <sup>[N282](#n282)</sup>.
- **People he has already sent.** A Madaripur broker was known locally for having sent more than 300 young men to Italy through Libya <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>. Locals said a Libya-based broker from Adityapur, Madaripur, had sent over 50 young men from the village to Italy in one year <sup>[N295](#n295)</sup>. Local people in Bhairab, Kishoreganj, said in July 2024 that several rings sending people to Italy by sea through Libya had been active there for 10 years and many people did reach Italy through them; the most active, Rasel Mia's ring, then took several crore taka from 24 people, none of whom reached Italy <sup>[N296 (2024)](#n296)</sup>. The sister-in-law of a Madaripur man whom relatives of two dead migrants accuse of trafficking said many people in the area came to him after his two sons went to Italy <sup>[N297](#n297)</sup>. Relatives allege he took BDT 30 lakh from the two families and said they would cross the Mediterranean by ship, but they were put on a small engine boat that left Libya on 14 October 2025 with fifty people, and the two died of cold at sea on 21 October; one man's brother said the family learned of the death 21 days after it, and his father said the broker had told them the men were alive <sup>[N297](#n297)</sup>. The complainant in the Sylhet UK-visa case said the accused had sent his own two sisters to England <sup>[N282](#n282)</sup>.
- **A friend.** Abdullah Al Zobayer, 24, of Mirsarai, Chattogram, said in June 2024 that he trusted a friend who was the younger brother of a Libya-based broker and a member of the ring, was taken to Libya on the promise of Italy, and that the ring collected BDT 4 lakh from his family <sup>[N298 (2024)](#n298)</sup>.
- **Local standing.** In Kalia union, Sakhipur, Tangail, three villagers said in May 2024 that they each gave BDT 100,000 directly to the union parishad chairman, and a fourth said he gave BDT 72,000 through a union parishad member; six months on, they said, the chairman had given neither a visa nor a refund <sup>[N299 (2024)](#n299)</sup>. Two Madaripur brothers who drowned off Tunisia had been sent by a man who is the local union parishad chairman, according to the family; he denies it <sup>[N300 (2024)](#n300)</sup>. The brokers kept telling the family the brothers were alive and avoided letting them speak by phone; a relative in Italy told the parents about four months later <sup>[N300 (2024)](#n300)</sup>. Families of four Patgram men say local people took BDT 900,000 from each for good jobs in Russia; a sister of one alleged that two local Jamaat-e-Islami office-holders and an employee of one of them took the money <sup>[N290](#n290)</sup>. The CID, citing a woman's case, said in July 2026 that she met a man who worked at the NGO she had borrowed from, that he helped her take a BDT 100,000 loan from the NGO towards the cost of going abroad, and that she was sent to Cambodia in August 2025, where, according to her case, ring members forcibly took her passport and money <sup>[N213](#n213)</sup>. The complainants' lawyer in the BSB Global Network case showed the court photos of the firm's chairman with the former prime minister and ministers <sup>[N286](#n286)</sup>.

**What to do:** taking money on a false job promise is an offence under section 31 of the OEM Act (see [File a police case](#file-a-police-case)) <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. A person harmed can also file a civil suit for compensation (see [Sue for compensation](#sue-for-compensation)) <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.

<a id="recruited-while-abroad-or-just-back"></a>

### 2.24 Recruited while abroad or just back

- **In the Gulf.** Abdullah Al Zobayer, who came back from captivity in Libya in 2024, said the ring that took him has many members and also targets Bangladeshi expatriates in the Middle East, taking them to Libya with talk of good jobs or passage to Europe and holding them hostage to collect money from their families <sup>[N298 (2024)](#n298)</sup>. Afaz Uddin Molla, a worker in Kuwait, said he answered a Facebook advert in August 2024 offering a cheap route to Canada and was promised he would be taken there from Kuwait; according to the case statement, the man calling himself Asadul took BDT 24 lakh from him by December 2024 <sup>[N217](#n217)</sup>. According to his family and local sources, Elias Howlader of Kalkini, Madaripur, went to Qatar about three years before March 2026, decided from there to go to Italy illegally after being lured by a Bangladeshi broker, and reached Libya from Qatar in early August 2025; his father said the family learned of it only after he had gone <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>. According to the family, brokers took BDT 20 lakh from them in instalments while he was held and tortured in a Libyan detention camp, where he died on 23 March 2026 <sup>[N294](#n294)</sup>. In a case over a promised job in Australia reported in June 2026, the victim was a Bangladeshi working in Oman whom a person he already knew approached in December 2025 with an offer of work in Australia, according to CID (the police's Criminal Investigation Department) citing the case <sup>[N231](#n231)</sup>.
- **In Libya.** One of a group of 113 returnees told the ambassador he had lived in Libya for 14 years before deciding, in old age, to try the "game", the boat crossing to Italy (see [Libya to Italy by boat](#libya-to-italy-by-boat)) <sup>[N301](#n301)</sup>.
- **In Malaysia.** Shariful Islam Hasan, associate director of BRAC's Migration Programme and Youth Platform, told a roundtable at the Prothom Alo office in Dhaka on 22 July 2026 that many people in Malaysia are being cheated with assurances that they will be sent to Australia <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. Kuddus Bepari (33) of Majumdarkandi, Rajoir, Madaripur, who had worked in Malaysia for 7 years and came home in August 2024 to see his newborn child, was frustrated because the Malaysian labour market was not going well, and the local broker Monir Sheikh, learning of it, lured him into a BDT 16 lakh "body contract" to reach Italy through Libya, according to police, family and local sources (see [Deaths](#deaths)) <sup>[N302](#n302)</sup>.
- **On the flight home.** According to CID sources, a man from Senbag, Noakhali, who had worked in Saudi Arabia for about three years met a fellow passenger on the flight home, and through him was introduced to a member of a trafficking ring (see [A flight to a different country first](#a-flight-to-a-different-country-first)) <sup>[N276](#n276)</sup>.
- **Back home.** Mannan Soyal, who returned from a scam centre in Cambodia, told the July 2026 roundtable that he went to Saudi Arabia in 2022, stayed three years and decided to go abroad again after coming home, and that a relative from his area then sent him to Cambodia for BDT 5 lakh with talk of a restaurant job (see Cambodia, Myanmar and Laos: scam compounds) <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **In Russia, after the job ends.** Ali Hossain of Mymensingh said in November 2025 that his son Afzal Hossain Meraj, who went to Russia on 10 August 2024 through the travel agency Friendship and Co-operative Recruitment, worked four months at the company he was recruited for, was laid off, and then went to fight through a ring; he was wounded in a mine blast and spent 25 days in hospital <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. An unnamed young man told Prothom Alo that the firm he worked for in Russia put 15 to 20 workers out after two to three months, and that when four or five of them went to the airport in late February 2025 to fly home, a Russian broker took them to a hotel with talk of work <sup>[N304](#n304)</sup>. He said the broker took their passports and all their papers "for visa renewal", three or four Russians had them sign a contract, and they were then taken to a forest to cut trees, where guns and ammunition arrived some days later <sup>[N304](#n304)</sup>. Another man, from Trishal, Mymensingh, met a Russian broker while jobless in Moscow for about a month and a half after losing his job <sup>[N304](#n304)</sup>. According to his family, after the construction firm employing Shahriar Sumon of Trishal, Mymensingh, stopped work six months in, a broker in Moscow first took him away with a promise of vineyard work and then, promising to send him to Italy, took BDT 5 lakh from him and a Bangladeshi companion; they were made to sign papers in Russian whose meaning they did not understand, and later learned they had been enrolled in the Russian army <sup>[N264](#n264)</sup>. According to the wife of Kamrul Hasan of Brahmanbaria, after he went to Moscow looking for work and was cheated by one broker, another took BDT 5 lakh from him with a promise to send him to Italy, and he was then handed over to the Russian army (see [Russia: recruited into the war](#russia-recruited-into-the-war)) <sup>[N305](#n305)</sup>.
- **In Russian custody.** The UN's Independent International Commission of Inquiry on Ukraine says foreigners already in Russia were also taken into the armed forces by deception or coercion: some were in police custody or in temporary detention centres for foreign nationals and some were serving prison sentences, and several were told that signing a military contract would spare them prosecution, a long sentence, solitary confinement or torture, with threats of torture or a longer sentence if they did not sign <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

<a id="study-abroad-firms-that-take-the-fee"></a>

### 2.25 Study-abroad firms that take the fee

- **BSB Global Network.** Complainants told Prothom Alo in July 2025 that they paid the firm BDT 17 lakh each (a lawyer couple) and BDT 13 lakh (another complainant) for Canadian visas for higher education, and that it arranged no visas and did not return the money <sup>[N306](#n306)</sup>. According to the CID's preliminary inquiry, from 2018 to 2024 its chairman, Khairul Bashar, with his wife Khandaker Selima Rowshan and son Arosh Ibne Bashar, built an organised ring that took large sums from students for admission to foreign universities, scholarships and visa processing, drawing them in with fake visa processing, made-up claims of representation and flashy adverts <sup>[N307](#n307)</sup>. The CID found that for many students the firm had made no application to any foreign university <sup>[N306](#n306), [N308](#n308)</sup>. The CID said the family kept the money in various bank accounts and withdrew it to buy property, run businesses and transfer money illegally <sup>[N307](#n307)</sup>.
  - **The cases.** The CID filed a money-laundering case against Khairul Bashar at Gulshan police station on 4 May 2025, arrested him in Dhanmondi, Dhaka, on 14 July 2025, and questioned him on 10 days' remand <sup>[N307](#n307), [N306](#n306)</sup>. On 13 August 2025 a Dhaka court gave 13 more days' remand in five separate fraud cases <sup>[N307](#n307)</sup>.
  - **The victims.** The CID said in August 2025 that 448 victims had so far reported being cheated, that the real number could be higher, that several had filed fraud and forgery cases at different police stations, and that many students who did go abroad were cheated in various ways there <sup>[N307](#n307)</sup>.
- **VisaGuide.** According to the case against it, victims were given cheques and receipts after paying, no one was sent abroad in the agreed time, and on 5 July 2026 victims found its office had been moved away (see [Proof that is not proof](#proof-that-is-not-proof)) <sup>[N287](#n287)</sup>.

<a id="people-who-offer-to-get-your-money-back"></a>

### 2.26 People who offer to get your money back

- **Money owed abroad.** According to case documents, Rahima Begum of Dhaka, whose husband had paid his Saudi sponsor BDT 35 lakh to take 17 people to Saudi Arabia and never got it back, contacted advertisers she saw on Facebook and YouTube on 8 March 2022 that promised recovery of owed money, and they took BDT 10 lakh 20 thousand from her in stages before cutting contact <sup>[N309](#n309)</sup>. They promised to recover the money within 72 hours by supernatural means <sup>[N309](#n309)</sup>. Police charged 23 people on 13 July 2026 <sup>[N309](#n309)</sup>.
- **A relative held hostage.** According to the CID, after the family of a man held in Libya paid BDT 5 lakh in August 2025, a man named Sohel contacted the hostage's wife, said he had all the information and would go to Libya himself if necessary to rescue him, and told her to come to the Kadamtali area of Dhaka; when she went, his phone was switched off <sup>[N310](#n310)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-complain-and-get-help"></a>

### 2.27 Where to complain and get help

<a id="complain-to-bmet"></a>

#### 2.27.1 Complain to BMET

1. <a id="complain-to-bmet-item-1"></a>**Online:** since 30 July 2026, the worker or a family member can file on OEP's Online Complaint Management Module (OEP is the Overseas Employment Platform, BMET's online system), upload evidence, follow progress and get a notification at each stage <sup>[N214](#n214), [N81](#n81)</sup>. The module, built by BMET and RMMRU (the Refugee and Migratory Movements Research Unit), is to bring every District Employment and Manpower Office (DEMO) and BMET's central complaint handling into one system <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>.
2. <a id="complain-to-bmet-item-2"></a>**On paper:** BMET's complaint form (Form Ka) asks for your name, NID (national ID card), passport and BMET smart card numbers, addresses and the agency's or broker's details; hand it in at BMET, 89/2 Kakrail, Dhaka-1000, or your DEMO <sup>[141](#o141), [132](#o132)</sup>.
3. <a id="complain-to-bmet-item-3"></a>**What happens:** free; an inquiry committee holds a hearing, BMET recommends and the ministry decides, within 30 working days <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.
4. <a id="complain-to-bmet-item-4"></a>**If nothing happens:** BMET's complaint officer, the Director (Employment), diremi@bmet.gov.bd, 01711111544 (3 months); then the ministry Secretary, secretary@probashi.gov.bd (1 month); then grs.gov.bd (3 months) <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.

Under section 41 of the OEM Act the inquiry must finish within 30 working days, and a proven complaint may be settled by order or arbitration within 3 months of the inquiry's end <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. If an agency refuses to pay an arbitration award, BMET may deduct it from the agency's security deposit, according to a 2026 study by RMMRU <sup>[N311](#n311)</sup>.

**In practice:** many complaints stay unresolved for years <sup>[N169 (2024)](#n169)</sup>. BMET's Director General said in July 2026 that in 2025 BMET settled 500 complaints by the manual process and obtained Tk 10 crore in compensation for migrants <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>. In Cox's Bazar, the DEMO's assistant director said in June 2026 that about 30 complaints lodged with BMET and his office had been settled and reports sent on them; he gave no period <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.

<a id="complain-to-the-ministry"></a>

#### 2.27.2 Complain to the Ministry

- **Enforcement-2 branch** of the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare: application with proof, your full address and the agency's RL (recruiting licence) number and address; free; action in 3-5 working days, settlement in 30; 02-41030255, sasenforcement@probashi.gov.bd <sup>[62](#o62)</sup>.
- **Mobile courts** under the OEM Act give immediate remedies; free; 7 working days <sup>[49](#o49)</sup>.
- **Hotline:** 16135 (toll free), +880 9610102030 from abroad <sup>[132](#o132)</sup>.
- **Coming:** the expatriates' welfare minister said on 26 October 2026 that an open email service to settle any complaint is being launched and publicised, and that work is under way on technology at BMET to check visa validity quickly <sup>[N312](#n312)</sup>.

The Ministry's secretary said in July 2026 that most complaints it gets from migrants turn out to be true, and that the most common are money taken on a promise of sending someone abroad, a migrant's property at home being seized, and a person going missing abroad <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>. The state minister said in May 2026 that complaints of irregularities and excess cost do come, that there are syndicate, test and recruitment problems in Malaysia, Korea, Saudi Arabia and other countries, that insecurity for women migrants is more frequent in some Middle Eastern countries, that the scope for legal remedy used to be limited, and that agreements with some countries, which he did not name, have now created scope to file cases and recover compensation <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Which countries' agreements now let a worker file a case and recover compensation there, and how does a worker start one?
> - What is the address of the Ministry's open complaint email announced in October 2026, and is it live <sup>[N312](#n312)</sup>?

<a id="file-a-police-case"></a>

#### 2.27.3 File a police case

- **Section 31 of the OEM Act:** taking or trying to take money on a false promise of a job abroad, holding a migrant's passport or papers without lawful reason, or luring someone with false promises of higher pay: up to 5 years' prison and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. It applies to any person, not only agencies <sup>[127 (2013)](#o127)</sup>.
- **Section 33:** obtaining a demand letter, visa or work permit by illegal means and buying or selling it in Bangladesh: 2 to 7 years' rigorous imprisonment and at least Tk 3 lakh <sup>[138](#o138), [26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Section 35:** an unregistered sub-agent: 1 year or a fine of Tk 50,000 to 2 lakh <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Section 34:** sending someone out other than through an official exit point: up to 10 years and at least Tk 5 lakh <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **How it is handled:** sections 31, 33 and 34 are cognisable (police can register a case and arrest without a warrant), non-bailable and can't be settled privately; sections 32, 33A, 35 and 35A can be tried by a mobile court <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. Cases are tried by a first-class judicial or metropolitan magistrate <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Emergency:** 999 <sup>[144](#o144)</sup>.

<a id="file-a-trafficking-case"></a>

#### 2.27.4 File a trafficking case

- **The law:** the Prevention and Suppression of Human Trafficking and Migrant Smuggling Act 2026 replaced the 2012 trafficking act <sup>[126](#o126), [146](#o146)</sup>.
- **What counts:** recruiting, moving or holding someone by deceit or by exploiting their vulnerability, for sexual, labour or other exploitation inside or outside Bangladesh, is trafficking (section 5) <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>. So is luring or helping someone to go abroad for work knowing they may be exploited <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>. Exploitation includes a fraudulent marriage, forcing someone into online scamming, and holding someone for ransom <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>.
- **Penalty:** at least 5 years' rigorous imprisonment up to life and at least Tk 50,000; for an organised group, death, life or at least 7 years and at least Tk 5 lakh <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>. Migrant smuggling, getting someone illegally into a country where they are not a citizen or resident, carries 3 to 10 years and at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>.
- **Where:** a complaint can go to a police station or directly to the Tribunal, and police must protect the complainant and keep their identity secret (section 28) <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>. Until Human Trafficking and Migrant Smuggling Suppression Tribunals are set up, the government can appoint the district's Women and Children Repression Prevention Tribunal judge, or the district and sessions judge, to hear these cases <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>.
- **Who files:** cheated workers returning from various countries file trafficking cases, and in some cases so do the families of migrants killed abroad, Prothom Alo reported in July 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
- **Compensation:** on conviction the Tribunal can order the trafficker to pay the victim (section 46) <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>.
- **In practice:** in the roughly 4,400 to 4,500 trafficking cases of 2020 to 2024, 94 to 95% of the accused were acquitted, according to Home Ministry data, and in January to May 2026 about 500 new cases were filed while 17 were disposed of (see [What happens to trafficking cases](#what-happens-to-trafficking-cases)) <sup>[N24](#n24), [N313](#n313)</sup>.

<a id="sue-for-compensation"></a>

#### 2.27.5 Sue for compensation

Anyone harmed by a breach of the OEM Act or the employment contract can file a civil suit for compensation alongside a criminal case (OEM Act section 28) <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. A trafficking or smuggling victim has the same right under section 56 of the 2026 trafficking act <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>. In practice workers often can't keep a case going for lack of money and legal aid <sup>[N311](#n311)</sup>.

<a id="local-committees-and-mediation"></a>

#### 2.27.6 Local committees and mediation

- **Mediation:** Jesia Khatun of WARBE Development Foundation, a migrants' rights organisation, said in April 2026 that a significant amount of money had been recovered through mediation in the past three years; she gave no amount <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>.
- **Grievance redress committees:** Iqbal Mahmud of The Asia Foundation said at the same time that committees formed through municipalities, upazila (sub-district) and union parishads (the lowest tier of local government) are working at district and local level, but on a limited scale <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>.
- **Calls to activate committees:** at an April 2026 roundtable in Cumilla, the principal of the Cumilla Technical Training Centre recommended activating union-level complaint settlement committees, making sub-agent registration compulsory, expanding airport help desks, opening one-stop service centres and making payment through banks compulsory, and WARBE's Cumilla officer called for migration information services and a "migration corner" in each union <sup>[N109](#n109)</sup>. At the same roundtable a union parishad member from Amratoli, Cumilla Sadar, named getting no help with complaints among the main problems, alongside torture, excessive workload and withheld wages, and WARBE's officer listed dalal fraud, excess cost, unpaid wages and missing papers abroad, going without skills and little rehabilitation on return <sup>[N109](#n109)</sup>. In Cox's Bazar in June 2026 the regional head of the NGO YPSA said counter-trafficking committees exist at district, upazila and union level and need to be made active <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.
- **District committees:** the Cox's Bazar DEMO's assistant director said in June 2026 that a District Migration Coordination Committee and a District Vigilance Task Force Committee are active against unsafe migration there, that upazilas have been told to form the same committees, and that a pilot programme helps people in Chakaria decide before going abroad <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How does a cheated worker bring a complaint to the union or upazila grievance committee, and which ones actually meet?
> - Does WARBE Development Foundation mediate refunds for walk-in complainants, and at which offices?

<a id="free-legal-help"></a>

#### 2.27.7 Free legal help

- **The right:** migrants and people cheated in the name of migration have a right to reasonable legal aid (OEM Act section 27) <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Government legal aid helpline:** 16699 <sup>[139](#o139)</sup>.
- **OKUP:** free, confidential legal advice and help filing a case, 01842773300 <sup>[N86](#n86)</sup>.
- **BNWLA** (the Bangladesh National Women Lawyers' Association): an advocate said in June 2026 that it was conducting the cases of about 100 returnees from abroad in the Cox's Bazar court, and that about 465 human trafficking cases were pending <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Which CID or police anti-trafficking unit phone number takes complaints directly from families, and does it answer?
> - Does BRAC's Migration Welfare Centre take walk-in fraud cases, and at which addresses?
> - What number does BNWLA give returnees who need a lawyer, and does it take cases outside Cox's Bazar?

<a id="departure-bottlenecks-offloading-airport-checks-bribes-and-deportation"></a>

## 3 Departure bottlenecks: offloading, airport checks, bribes and deportation

<a id="in-one-minute-1"></a>

### 3.1 In one minute

Before a worker boards, five offices each hold a stamp he cannot travel without: the passport office, the police (for the police clearance), an approved medical centre (for the Gulf, one approved by the Gulf Health Council), the destination's embassy, and BMET (the Bureau of Manpower, Employment and Training, which issues the emigration clearance or "smart card") <sup>[31](#o31), [32](#o32), [N314](#n314), [N315 (2019)](#n315), [10](#o10)</sup>. At each one, surveys, news reports and an anti-corruption case show people paying on top of the official fee: TIB (Transparency International Bangladesh) found three in four households that used the passport office in November 2024 to October 2025 paid a bribe or unauthorised money, medical centres have been reported to threaten fit workers with an "unfit" result and to sell guaranteed "fit" reports, and in July 2025 the Anti-Corruption Commission filed a case against BMET officials it accuses of clearing women on fake passports <sup>[N316](#n316), [N317 (2017)](#n317), [N314](#n314), [N255](#n255)</sup>. The last gate is immigration at the airport, run by the police's Special Branch (SB), which turned back 81,500 travellers from 2021 to June 2026, often over mismatched visas, clearance discrepancies, forged papers or answers that did not add up <sup>[147](#o147), [N318](#n318)</sup>. Some were stopped with valid papers, most visibly three adult women flying to Nepal on 8 August 2026 who, their lawyer says, were asked why they were unmarried; on 29 September 2026 the High Court asked the government to explain why stopping them should not be declared illegal <sup>[N319](#n319)</sup>. A Home Ministry official has admitted that some complaints of people paying police to get through, or being unjustly stopped, are true <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. Even past Dhaka, visitors can be refused on arrival, as hundreds of Bangladeshis were at Kuala Lumpur in 2025 <sup>[N320](#n320), [N321](#n321)</sup>. Workers who come back deported or on an outpass are met by the government's airport welfare desk, though in 2020 some deportees were jailed on arrival, and destinations such as Kuwait and Malaysia bar deportees from returning for years or for life <sup>[76](#o76), [N322 (2020)](#n322), [N323](#n323), [N324 (2017)](#n324)</sup>.

<a id="passport-and-police-clearance"></a>

### 3.2 Passport and police clearance

<a id="the-passport-office"></a>

#### 3.2.1 The passport office

**The rule.** An e-passport (48 pages, 5 years) costs Tk 4,025 regular, Tk 6,325 express or Tk 8,625 super express, VAT included <sup>[31](#o31)</sup>. Regular delivery is within 15 working days of biometric enrolment, express within 7 and super express within 2 <sup>[31](#o31)</sup>. The application must match the NID (national identity card) or online birth certificate exactly, and anyone over 20 must use the NID <sup>[50](#o50)</sup>. The deputy director of the Sylhet divisional passport and visa office said in April 2026 that the regular fee is Tk 4,025 for five years and Tk 5,750 for ten years, that super express costs Tk 10,350, and that with super express someone who gives biometrics in the morning can get the passport in Dhaka by the afternoon <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>. He said anyone 18 or over applies with the NID and anyone younger with a birth registration, pays the fee at a bank and gives fingerprints <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>. The Department of Immigration and Passports' additional director general said in August 2026 that super express passports are being delivered within 24 hours or two days <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.

> **Need to know**

**One person, one passport.** Since e-passports began in 2020, no passport is issued without online verification of the NID and birth registration data, and the gaps that once let some people hold more than one passport are now easily detected, the Cox's Bazar regional passport office said in June 2026 <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>. Because an e-passport takes all 10 fingerprints, the same person can no longer get a passport under a different identity, and there is no scope to issue one without valid information and papers, even to someone who went abroad by an illegal route, the department's additional director general said in August 2026 <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>. He said the department has cases of people who took passports by repeatedly changing their name, date of birth and other details, which is reducing foreign embassies' and immigration authorities' acceptance of Bangladeshi passports <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.

**What happens.** In TIB's National Household Survey 2025 (November 2024 to October 2025), passport services were the most corrupt sector: 84.4% of households using them met corruption and 76.6% paid bribes or unauthorised money <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. The average bribe was Tk 4,532, and 39.7% reported harassment by brokers ("dalals") <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. Most irregularities happened while preparing and submitting the application (76.8%), and the biometric stage carried the highest average bribe, Tk 6,680 <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. People reissuing an old MRP (machine-readable passport) paid the most, Tk 12,444 on average <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. Barishal (93.3%) and Sylhet (91.7%) divisions were the worst <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. TIB says that despite online services many people still depend on brokers, which keeps bribery alive <sup>[N325](#n325)</sup>.

**How the broker trade works.** At the Mymensingh regional passport office, a file sent through a dalal is called a "channel file" (চ্যানেল ফাইল); three dalals told Prothom Alo in 2024 that every shop outside the office does them for Tk 2,500 above the government fee, of which Tk 1,200 goes to the passport office and the rest to the dalals <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>. By their account, a fake "nationality and character certificate" in the name of Mymensingh City Corporation is attached to the file with a code on it; the clerk taking applications knows from the code whose dalal sent it and accepts it without scrutiny, the office keeps a daily count of coded forms, and an office employee collects the money by that count every Wednesday <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>. The dalals sit in at least 30 computer, photocopy and online-application shops in front of the Mymensingh office in Maskanda, having moved off the street because of occasional raids, and by their account they take at least Tk 5 lakh a day <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>. In Gazipur in 2025, 25 to 30 shops on both sides of the road in front of the office fill in forms and deposit the bank fee themselves <sup>[N87](#n87)</sup>. Applicants there said applying alone meets harassment on various pretexts while through a dalal everything is done at once for Tk 1,000 to 1,500; a reporter saw people wait about an hour for document checks and two to two and a half hours for fingerprints and photo, while those who paid the dalal went straight to the fingerprint and photo room <sup>[N87](#n87)</sup>.

**Cases:**
- **24 April to 16 May 2024, Mymensingh.** Azharul Islam of Tarakanda waited an hour and a half in line and was refused over an error in his papers, failed on two days with new errors found each time, and refused to use a dalal; on 28 April he asked the office's deputy assistant director why his application was not taken when all papers were in order, the officer accepted it quickly, and he got his passport on 16 May <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>.
- **4 May 2024, Mymensingh.** A reporter saw Hashem Ali of Phulpur's application accepted without scrutiny, though his nationality and character certificate had blank ward and neighbourhood fields and an unidentified signature, and carried a handwritten code of three capital letters and the number 53 beside the serial number, a code the report says the dalal gives <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>. The same day Sabbir Ahmed of Churkhai, told his papers had an "error" and to come back the next day, went with two friends to a shop outside to find a dalal <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>.
- **June 2024, Mymensingh.** The secretary of SUJAN (Citizens for Good Governance) in Mymensingh said nothing gets done at the office without a dalal, and that his nephew failed for a month to submit an application and got a passport only through one <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>. The office's deputy director said applicants are told to beware of dalals outside, that he did not know of codes on fake City Corporation certificates or of staff taking money, and that he would look into it, as did the deputy assistant director <sup>[N326 (2024)](#n326)</sup>.
- **20 November 2024, Rajshahi.** People under the banner "Victims: the ordinary people of Rajshahi" formed a human chain in front of the divisional passport and visa office in Shalbagan against dalal files being processed first, and gave the deputy director a month to end it <sup>[N327 (2024)](#n327)</sup>. They said dalal files go straight upstairs to deputy director Rozy Khandaker's room for her signature and are entered on the same floor, then come quickly down for the photograph, while people without a dalal stand for hours in the ground-floor line <sup>[N327 (2024)](#n327)</sup>. They said officers look only for mistakes in files without a dalal and check nothing in dalal files, and that the ground-floor clerk is often away from his desk <sup>[N327 (2024)](#n327)</sup>. She denied all the allegations, asked them for proof, and told journalists she could not speak without the Director General's permission <sup>[N327 (2024)](#n327)</sup>. An intelligence report cited by Prothom Alo describes a "G-25" syndicate of 24 passport officers said to collect at least Tk 25 lakh in bribes a day; the ACC (Anti-Corruption Commission) questioned its members in 2021 and filed cases against some, and the Rajshahi protesters said she is known as a member <sup>[N327 (2024)](#n327)</sup>.
- **May 2025, Gazipur.** Ariful Islam of Kapasia joined the fingerprint and photo line at 9 am and finished at 1:30 pm, while those who paid a dalal finished within 10 to 15 minutes of arriving <sup>[N87](#n87)</sup>. Riad Mia of Tongi waited until 1:30 pm on a Wednesday without submitting his wife's file, came back on Thursday and finished after more than two hours, having refused a man who asked Tk 1,000 to get it done quickly <sup>[N87](#n87)</sup>. The office's deputy director said pressure of applicants had risen so it could take time, though not two to three hours, and that she has not let dalals inside since she arrived <sup>[N87](#n87)</sup>.
- **13 July 2025, Faridpur.** Two local dalals, Siam Ahmed and Rashed Khan, took Tk 30,000 from a Rohingya couple on a promise of passports, did no passport work, and beat the couple's son-in-law when he protested, according to local sources; he asked the office's Ansar guards for help, and police detained the couple, the son-in-law and the two dalals <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>. The Kotwali police station's officer in charge said legal action was being taken against the dalals, and the three Rohingya would be sent to a camp if they lived in one <sup>[N328](#n328)</sup>.

**Police verification.** TIB counts the abolition of police verification for passports among recent reforms <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. In a 2017 study, TIB had found that three in four new-passport applicants paid bribes or irregular money during police verification, and its executive director said there is "a trap of harassment" at every step from application to delivery <sup>[N329](#n329)</sup>. In the 2025 survey (November 2024 to October 2025), passport verification was the police service with the most corruption (80.7%) and bribery (77.5%) <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Is police verification still done for any new passport in 2026, and if so, for whom?
> - Which office takes a complaint about a passport-office broker or bribe: the Department of Immigration and Passports, its GRS (grievance redress) officer, or only the ACC (Anti-Corruption Commission)?

<a id="police-clearance"></a>

#### 3.2.2 Police clearance

**The rule.** The police clearance certificate (PCC) is applied for online at pcc.police.gov.bd and verified by police at your present address <sup>[32](#o32)</sup>. The fee was raised from Tk 500 to Tk 1,500 by a Security Services Division circular of 4 December 2024 <sup>[148](#o148)</sup>. You track it by texting "PCC S \<reference number\>" to 26969; the PCC helpdesk is 01320001824 or 01320001825, help.pcc@police.gov.bd, Sunday to Thursday 9 am to 5 pm <sup>[32](#o32)</sup>.

**What happens.** TIB's 2017 study found police took Tk 500 to 1,000 per visa for a clearance <sup>[N76 (2017)](#n76)</sup>. In TIB's 2025 survey, 66.1% of households dealing with the Special Branch paid unauthorised money, though police clearance had the lowest average bribe of any police service, Tk 1,279 <sup>[N316](#n316)</sup>. Cumilla's additional superintendent of police said in April 2026 that despite some complaints, people with no case against them get clearance within the set time, and that a delay is resolved quickly by contacting the office concerned <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>. Brokers also sell forged certificates: police said a ring, whose arrested members were mainly brokers, took Tk 1,23,000 from a man in Banshkhali, Chattogram, in 2026 on a promise of a quick clearance and gave him a forged one; his brother said the man had a criminal case and so had not applied to the police directly, and that the family paid Tk 80,000 in advance and the rest on delivery (see [Forged documents and impersonation](#forged-documents-and-impersonation)) <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>. Chattogram's Superintendent of Police asked people not to get a clearance through any broker or middleman but to follow the government process, and called forging officers' signatures and using a fake website and QR code a very serious crime <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How long does a PCC take in 2026, and what is the usual delay at the address check?
> - What is the street price for a "fast" PCC through a broker in 2026?

<a id="the-gulf-medical-wafid-formerly-gamca"></a>

### 3.3 The Gulf medical (Wafid, formerly GAMCA)

<a id="the-rule-and-the-fee"></a>

#### 3.3.1 The rule and the fee

Workers going to the Gulf states must pass a medical at a centre approved by the Gulf Health Council (GHC), booked through its Wafid system; the approved centres were previously known as GAMCA (GCC-approved medical centres, the GCC being the Gulf Cooperation Council) <sup>[N314](#n314)</sup>. The agency buys a USD 10 referral token from Wafid, and the whole medical should cost no more than Tk 8,500 to 9,000 <sup>[N314](#n314)</sup>. Wafid says the result appears within 26 days, and a certificate is valid two months for issuing the visa <sup>[59](#o59), [61 (2021)](#o61)</sup>. Centres are licensed first by Bangladesh's health ministry, then approved by the GHC <sup>[N330](#n330)</sup>. Qatar uses its own centres: only two are approved, in Dhaka and Sylhet, and the fee is at least USD 137 <sup>[N330](#n330)</sup>.

<a id="the-unfit-then-pay-scheme-and-the-slip-racket"></a>

#### 3.3.2 The "unfit, then pay" scheme and the slip racket

- **Unfit, then pay.** TIB reported in 2017 that some centres threaten fit workers with an "unfit" result to charge more, or make them repeat tests <sup>[N317 (2017)](#n317)</sup>. A worker told TBS (The Business Standard) in 2025 that, found unfit in Chattogram for Tk 8,500, he was declared fit at a Panthapath centre in Dhaka for Tk 50,000, then re-tested in Kuwait for 5 dirhams <sup>[N330](#n330)</sup>.
- **"Fit" packages.** A Dhaka agency offered one worker a Tk 10,000 "standard" medical that could end "unfit", or a Tk 20,000 package that guaranteed "fit" <sup>[N314](#n314)</sup>. Syndicate centres reportedly charge Tk 25,000 to 30,000 for a guaranteed "fit" <sup>[N314](#n314)</sup>. A former BAIRA (recruiting agencies' association) leader said only 2-5% of workers pay the set fee, and the rest pay at least Tk 17,000 to 20,000 <sup>[N330](#n330)</sup>.
- **Why people pay.** An "unfit" result leaves the passport unusable for a Gulf medical until it expires, and workers fear a lifetime blacklist, so many fix a "contract" with a broker in advance <sup>[N314](#n314), [N330](#n330)</sup>.
- **Who controls the slips.** Industry insiders say about 20 of more than 200 approved centres get nearly 90% of referrals because syndicate-linked centres pre-book slots <sup>[N314](#n314)</sup>. Approved centres grew from 26 in 2017 to 260, against 167 in India <sup>[N330](#n330)</sup>.
- **Reports without tests.** Some centres issue certificates without testing, or upload reports from a passport photocopy, and some tests done in Dhaka are filed under district centres' names <sup>[N317 (2017)](#n317), [N330](#n330), [N314](#n314)</sup>.
- **What it costs later.** A worker who paid Tk 25,000 for a "fit" certificate failed the re-test in Saudi Arabia and was deported <sup>[N314](#n314)</sup>.

In July 2025 the ministry's monitoring wing said it had heard of extra charges but received no formal complaints, and had contacted the Gulf Cooperation Council; by September 2025 the ministry had written to the GHC asking it to act against irregular centres <sup>[N314](#n314), [N330](#n330)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Has the GHC suspended or delisted any Bangladeshi medical centre since 2024, and where is the list published?
> - How does a worker appeal an "unfit" result: a re-test at another centre, or an appeal to the GHC through Wafid?
> - How long does an "unfit" record block a passport in Wafid?

<a id="malaysias-medical"></a>

#### 3.3.3 Malaysia's medical

The ministry fixed the Malaysia-bound medical at Tk 8,000 on 31 October 2026, and no centre may take more <sup>[149](#o149)</sup>. No fee is due for the MiGRAMS slip (the booking from Malaysia's recruitment system), no centre may work under a particular agency, and a worker may test at any approved centre <sup>[149](#o149)</sup>. If a worker changes agency after the slip, the first agency must send the medical report on without delay <sup>[149](#o149)</sup>. BAIRA said that over the previous three years a syndicate of about 100 agencies had charged Tk 11,000 against a Tk 6,500 fee, taking Tk 355 crore extra <sup>[N330](#n330)</sup>.

**Complain:** email the ministry at secretary@probashi.gov.bd if a centre asks more than Tk 8,000 or harasses you <sup>[149](#o149)</sup>.

<a id="attestation-and-visa-stamping"></a>

### 3.4 Attestation and visa stamping

Every document going abroad needs attestation by Bangladesh's Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MOFA), after the issuing body and, for Arabic translations, a notary, the Islamic Foundation and the Law Ministry <sup>[55](#o55)</sup>. MOFA also runs online apostille (attestation of public documents and certificates) on mygov.bd, the MyGov portal of the a2i (Aspire to Innovate) programme, so documents can be attested from home; a2i said on 13 December 2025 that about 17 lakh applications had been settled in the previous 11 months <sup>[N235](#n235)</sup>. For Saudi Arabia, the employer sends visa numbers online, the agency submits the passports, and since 31 October 2022 passports, stamping and fingerprints go through Tasheer, a visa service centre at Jamuna Future Park, not the embassy <sup>[N315 (2019)](#n315), [N331 (2022)](#n331)</sup>. Stamping can take one week to two months after fingerprints in some cases <sup>[N332](#n332)</sup>. In 2022 an agency could submit at most 30 passports a week, with no cap for women workers <sup>[N333 (2022)](#n333)</sup>.

**What happens.**
- In 2017 TIB reported delays, harassment and illegal charges by some host-country embassies at stamping, and that a file with any error is returned <sup>[N317 (2017)](#n317)</sup>.
- In 2022 agencies said they paid USD 220-250 per Saudi visa application through middlemen, which raised workers' costs by at least Tk 20,000; the embassy said only a small fee for Enjaz (the online Saudi visa application) applies and brokers may misuse its name <sup>[N333 (2022)](#n333)</sup>.
- TIB has reported that some officials at Bangladeshi missions abroad attest demand letters without proper checks or for bribes <sup>[N34](#n34)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What are Tasheer's fee and waiting time for a Saudi work visa in 2026?
> - Where is the bottleneck for Kuwait visas in 2026: the Kuwait embassy, the medical or BMET?
> - How does a worker check that his visa has really been stamped and is not fake before BMET clearance?

<a id="bmet-clearance-and-the-smart-card-1"></a>

### 3.5 BMET clearance and the smart card

<a id="the-rule-and-the-fee-1"></a>

#### 3.5.1 The rule and the fee

The emigration clearance, printed as a smart card, is BMET's permission to leave for work <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>. If an agency recruited you, it applies; if you found the visa yourself, you apply at BMET's emigration branch in Dhaka or the Chattogram district employment and manpower office (DEMO) <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>. Either way it takes 3 working days <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>. Fees (the welfare, insurance and smart card fees by bank pay order, the tax by treasury challan) <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>:
- welfare fee to the Wage Earners' Welfare Board: Tk 5,500 from 1 October 2026 <sup>[33](#o33)</sup>;
- insurance premium: Tk 1,000, once <sup>[34](#o34)</sup>;
- clearance (smart card) fee: Tk 250 <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>;
- source income tax: Tk 400 for an unattested single visa, Tk 250 attested, Tk 500 for a Saudi visa; Tk 1,200 (skilled) or Tk 800 (unskilled) on a group visa <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.

For women going as domestic workers, BMET needs proof of age 25 to 45 and a guardian's no-objection letter <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.

<a id="bribes-brokers-and-fake-clearances"></a>

#### 3.5.2 Bribes, brokers and fake clearances

- TIB's 2017 study found Tk 100-200 taken unofficially per clearance at BMET, and Tk 5,000-15,000 for low- and semi-skilled Malaysia-bound workers <sup>[N76 (2017)](#n76), [N317 (2017)](#n317)</sup>.
- TIB reported in 2017 that some workers buy the pre-departure training certificate through brokers without attending <sup>[N76 (2017)](#n76)</sup>.
- In 2016 only 301 workers used BMET's one-stop service to get their own clearance <sup>[N317 (2017)](#n317)</sup>.
- According to sources cited by Prothom Alo, 20 smart card applications rejected in the Ami Probashi app (the government's migrant-worker app) between 27 December 2023 and 4 January 2024 were later given clearance; separately, on 31 December 2023 the agency Alif Overseas (RL-847) took out smart cards for 24 Saudi-bound workers for which BMET officials later found no matching data on the server (see [Fake BMET cards and fake clearance](#fake-bmet-cards-and-fake-clearance)) <sup>[N208 (2024)](#n208)</sup>.
- On 3 July 2025 the ACC (Anti-Corruption Commission) filed a case against four BMET officials and the owners or officials of five agencies; it says its 29 May 2025 raid on BMET found clearances for Gulf domestic work approved on another woman's passport number without a check against BMET's database, and issued to four women under 25 <sup>[N255](#n255)</sup>. The accused are BMET deputy director (emigration) Md Sazzad Hossain Sarkar, assistant director (emigration) Md Hossain Ullah Akanda, manpower survey officer Md Nizamuddin Patwari and office assistant Md Azad Hossain, with Md Anwar Hossain of H A International, Md Salauddin of K H Overseas, Md Jamal Hossain of Makkah Overseas, Md Anwar Hossain Bhuiyan of Tasnim Overseas and Ekramul Haque of S M Manpower <sup>[N255](#n255)</sup>. The ACC alleges the agencies colluded to use fake passports for women sent to the Middle East as domestic workers, that clearing four women under 25 broke a ministry circular, and that the officials gained financially; it said it was investigating further and would take legal action when the investigation ends <sup>[N255](#n255)</sup>.
- In 2026 the state minister for expatriates' welfare said some BMET officials clear fake documents, that BMET can check visas for only four or five countries, and that a fake-visa clearance case for Serbia was under inquiry <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. BMET's charter lists online visa checks for Saudi Arabia, Qatar, Bahrain, Singapore and the UAE <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.
- In August 2026 a Civil Aviation Authority deputy director said passengers often arrive at the airport with fake BMET cards, fake visas or other fake papers, and that 24 agencies working at the airport, including immigration and the airlines, check these together <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.

**Check it is real** at portal.wewb.gov.bd/bmet-wewbm-check <sup>[69](#o69)</sup>. The welfare desks at Dhaka, Chattogram and Sylhet airports check smart cards before departure <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.

**Complain:**
1. BMET's Director (Emigration), +8802222222415, 01711-135901, diremi@bmet.gov.bd; then the Additional Director General (Employment), 02-48321949, adgemp@bmet.gov.bd <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.
2. Against an agency: a written complaint with proof to BMET's Director General, free, handled within 30 working days <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.
3. If BMET fails: its grievance ladder, then the ministry's Secretary (secretary@probashi.gov.bd), then the Cabinet Division's GRS at grs.gov.bd <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>.
4. For a bribe: the ACC (see [Where to complain](#where-to-complain)) <sup>[150](#o150)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does a DEMO charge, officially and in practice, for registration and fingerprints in 2026?
> - How many fake smart cards have the airport desks caught since 2024?

<a id="departure-day-at-the-airport"></a>

### 3.6 Departure day at the airport

<a id="who-checks-what"></a>

#### 3.6.1 Who checks what

1. <a id="who-checks-what-item-1"></a>**Papers to carry.** BMET's checklist: valid passport, work visa, medical certificate, demand letter, the contract attested by the destination's embassy, the agency's name and phone, the BMET smart card and the air ticket <sup>[34](#o34)</sup>. Keep one set of copies at home and three with you <sup>[34](#o34)</sup>.
2. <a id="who-checks-what-item-2"></a>**Airline check-in.** Air Arabia requires passengers from Bangladesh holding a copy of a UAE visa or e-visa to have an "OK to Board" message on the booking, done at least 24 hours before departure for AED 15 to 20 <sup>[151](#o151)</sup>.
3. <a id="who-checks-what-item-3"></a>**The welfare desk.** The Probashi Kallyan (expatriate welfare) desk sits directly opposite immigration at Dhaka; a worker with a BMET card who gets its seal clears immigration faster, according to the airport <sup>[152](#o152)</sup>. The desks check smart cards and help fill boarding cards <sup>[10](#o10), [76](#o76)</sup>.
4. <a id="who-checks-what-item-4"></a>**SB screening.** In 2021, before the immigration counter, SB officers at a separate desk held the passports of first-time Middle East travellers and interviewed them <sup>[N117 (2021)](#n117)</sup>.
5. <a id="who-checks-what-item-5"></a>**Immigration counter.** Counters are open 24 hours; the officer stamps the passport and may ask about your travel plans <sup>[153](#o153)</sup>.

TIB found in 2017 that some workers are handed their job contract only on reaching the airport <sup>[N317 (2017)](#n317)</sup>. A UNDP Bangladesh gender expert said in May 2026 that women workers are often seen standing confused at the airport with no idea what to do, and that the same goes for men <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>.

Besides SB immigration, the air force, the police, the Airport Armed Police Battalion (APBn, at the airport since 2013) and an anti-human-trafficking investigation team work at the airport, APBn's DIG (Deputy Inspector General) for operations said in August 2026 <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>. A Home Ministry joint secretary said at a July 2026 roundtable that the Passenger Name Record and Advance Passenger Information systems, which give the authorities airline passenger data, are being expanded at airports to detect fake travel documents <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. At Sylhet's Osmani airport, APBn said in April 2026 that an active group of traffickers and smugglers works the airport area and tries to mislead passengers with inducements, and that rings use first-time travellers as easy targets to carry drugs or smuggled goods (see [What should I carry?](#what-should-i-carry)) <sup>[N334](#n334), [N92](#n92)</sup>. Its unit there should have more than 300 members but has about 100, with intelligence and plain-clothes members deployed to stop anyone luring passengers <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Which other airlines flying from Dhaka require OK to Board in 2026, and for which destinations?
> - Is the welfare desk's seal still required, or optional, for workers at Dhaka in 2026?

<a id="offloading-who-decides-and-on-what-law"></a>

#### 3.6.2 Offloading: who decides and on what law

Immigration at Bangladesh's airports is run by the Special Branch of Bangladesh Police under the Ministry of Home Affairs, headed by an Additional Inspector General of Police (Additional IGP) <sup>[147](#o147), [154](#o154)</sup>. "Offloading" is immigration's refusal to let a checked-in passenger leave; in cases reported in 2021 it was marked with an "offload" stamp or note in the passport <sup>[N117 (2021)](#n117)</sup>. SB lists the laws it applies, including the Passport Order 1973 and the Emigration Ordinance 1982 and Rules 2002 <sup>[155](#o155)</sup>. The Passport Order bars leaving without a valid passport or travel document, and lets a passport be impounded if a court has barred its holder from leaving <sup>[155](#o155)</sup>. The Ordinance, as SB quotes it, makes emigration for work lawful for a citizen with a work permit, employment visa or appointment letter, or selected through a recognised agent, and punishes departing otherwise with up to one year's simple imprisonment or a Tk 5,000 fine <sup>[155](#o155)</sup>. In the three women's case below, their lawyers argued that an adult woman's marital status, or travelling without a parent or guardian, cannot by itself be a lawful ground to stop her <sup>[N335](#n335)</sup>.

<a id="who-gets-stopped"></a>

#### 3.6.3 Who gets stopped

Reported reasons include mismatched visas, clearance discrepancies, forged documents and conflicting answers at the interview <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. Cases show who else gets stopped:
- **Women travelling alone.** Women report officers asking whether their husband knows, and phoning husbands or parents to check <sup>[N336](#n336), [N337](#n337)</sup>. A rights activist says officers are most suspicious of women going to Thailand or Nepal <sup>[N336](#n336)</sup>.
- **Nepal.** Police officers told Prothom Alo that Nepal has been flagged as a trafficking route and, because it gives visas on arrival, Nepal travellers' papers get special scrutiny <sup>[N336](#n336)</sup>. Salma Ali, chairperson of ATSEC South Asia and adviser to the Bangladesh National Women Lawyers' Association, said in July 2026 that a suspicious passenger can be spotted in minutes, and that a young woman bound for Nepal had recently been suspected and was, in her words, rescued and brought back <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Tourist-visa travellers going for work.** APBn's DIG (Operations) said in August 2026 that officers can often tell when someone on a tourist visa is really going for work, and then verify and, if needed, report or try to stop the person; passengers are also checked against a database of cases <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.
- **Spouse visas to closed markets.** A woman with BMET clearance going to her "husband" in Oman, where worker and housemaid visas are shut, had no registered nikahnama; the same spouse-visa approval had been used for five women <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>.
- **Students and first-time visitors.** A student with a valid Thai visa was told his trip would be cancelled until he named his university <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. A first-time Malaysia traveller with a valid visa and BMET clearance was stopped and a GD (police general diary) started <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>.
- **Risky destinations.** BMET was told to check visas strictly for Serbia, Laos, Cambodia, Vietnam and Libya <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. Immigration police link visa fraud by Bangladeshis to at least 23 countries, including Thailand, India, Malaysia and Saudi Arabia <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers"></a>

#### 3.6.4 The numbers

Special Branch figures, as reported by Bonik Barta <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>:
- 2021: 11,528 turned back;
- 2022: 18,756, the highest;
- 2023: 9,872;
- 2024: 18,009;
- 2025: 15,516, of 5,708,132 who travelled;
- January to June 2026: 7,819, of 2,622,711.

> **Need to know**
> - How many of those turned back were women, and how many were going on work visas?
> - Does SB publish offloading figures by airport (Dhaka, Chattogram, Sylhet)?

<a id="documented-cases"></a>

#### 3.6.5 Documented cases

- **8 August 2026, Dhaka, Nepal.** Three adult women with passports, visas or entry permits, return tickets and hotel bookings, one also carrying a dollar endorsement, were taken to an office after getting boarding passes <sup>[N319](#n319), [N337](#n337)</sup>. They say officers asked why they were unmarried and whether their families consented, phoned their parents, and told one of them that unmarried women may not travel abroad alone <sup>[N319](#n319), [N337](#n337)</sup>. They say they were escorted out through arrivals with no written document and one woman's passport was kept, and their lawyer says they lost money on tickets and bookings <sup>[N337](#n337), [N319](#n319)</sup>. Police officers said the women showed no invitation letter for the seminar they mentioned <sup>[N336](#n336)</sup>.
- **November 2025, Dhaka, Nepal.** A 52-year-old woman travelling with a family she did not belong to was held until she argued her passport back <sup>[N336](#n336)</sup>.
- **Reported 2026, Dhaka, Thailand and Malaysia.** The student and first-time traveller above were let go only after naming a university or after a senior official's video call <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>.
- **Reported 2026, Dhaka, Oman.** The spouse-visa woman above <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Are there documented 2025-26 cases of women offloaded on trips to India or Thailand, with dates?

<a id="the-airport-contract"></a>

#### 3.6.6 The "airport contract"

In 2021 Dhaka Post found travel agencies charging Tk 30,000 for an "airport contract" for visit-visa trips to Dubai, saying the money went to immigration police <sup>[N117 (2021)](#n117)</sup>. Travellers given an "offload" stamp were told to pay more, such as Tk 45,000 to immigration police through the agency, and one who paid a broker Tk 1.65 lakh was waved through when an officer said "cut his offload" <sup>[N117 (2021)](#n117)</sup>. In 2026 a Home Ministry additional secretary said some complaints of people paying police to get through, or being unjustly stopped, are true <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. In a case the US Embassy in Dhaka filed in February 2024, an accused broker, Ashraful Alam Bhuiyan, admitted that an immigration officer at Shahjalal airport helps him with trafficking for money (see [Forged documents and impersonation](#forged-documents-and-impersonation)) <sup>[N338 (2024)](#n338), [N339 (2024)](#n339)</sup>.

<a id="your-rights"></a>

#### 3.6.7 Your rights

- The Constitution guarantees equality, non-discrimination by sex, protection of law, personal liberty and freedom of movement (Articles 27, 28, 31, 32 and 36); the three women's writ rests on these <sup>[N335](#n335)</sup>.
- On 29 September 2026 the High Court issued a rule asking why stopping them should not be declared unlawful, ordered the Additional IGP (immigration) to investigate and resolve their complaint within seven days and report to the court, and told immigration not to obstruct their future travel, subject to law <sup>[N319](#n319), [N335](#n335)</sup>.
- The court also asked why the government should not issue guidelines so adult women are not stopped for being unmarried or travelling without guardians <sup>[N335](#n335)</sup>.
- Withholding a would-be emigrant's travel documents for money is an offence under the Emigration Ordinance <sup>[155](#o155)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Must an officer give a written reason or receipt when he offloads someone or keeps a passport?
> - Has the High Court ruled on the rule issued on 29 September 2026?

<a id="papers-officers-have-asked-for"></a>

#### 3.6.8 Papers officers have asked for

- A hotel booking and an invitation letter for the stated purpose (the Nepal case) <sup>[N336](#n336)</sup>.
- A registered nikahnama for a spouse visa <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>.
- For workers, the BMET card; the airport says getting the welfare desk's seal speeds immigration <sup>[152](#o152)</sup>.

<a id="if-you-are-offloaded"></a>

#### 3.6.9 If you are offloaded

The three women were given no written document when they were sent out <sup>[N337](#n337)</sup>. The routes they used, and SB's own, are:

1. <a id="if-you-are-offloaded-item-1"></a>**Complain to SB immigration** through the online form at immi.specialbranch.gov.bd/Your_Complain; for emergencies, SS Immigration +88-01713373071, or the OC (officer in charge) of Immigration at Dhaka airport +880-2-8914226, 01320005902 <sup>[156](#o156), [157](#o157)</sup>. SB also runs an Offload Management System (OFMS) online "for settling offloads" <sup>[158](#o158)</sup>.
2. <a id="if-you-are-offloaded-item-2"></a>**Write to the Additional IGP (immigration)** asking for an investigation, preservation of CCTV footage, action and compensation, as the three women did in late August 2026 <sup>[N319](#n319)</sup>.
3. <a id="if-you-are-offloaded-item-3"></a>**If there is no reply,** a lawyer's legal notice, then a writ petition to the High Court <sup>[N319](#n319)</sup>.
4. <a id="if-you-are-offloaded-item-4"></a>**For discrimination or abuse,** complain free to the National Human Rights Commission (see [Where to complain](#where-to-complain)) <sup>[159 (2018)](#o159)</sup>.
5. <a id="if-you-are-offloaded-item-5"></a>**For a bribe demand,** the ACC (see [Where to complain](#where-to-complain)) <sup>[150](#o150)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does the OFMS do for a passenger: lift an offload record, or only record it?
> - Does an offload stay on the passport record and affect the next attempt?
