Dr Delwar Arif, an associate professor at Canisius University in New York, wrote in a Prothom Alo column on 27 March 2026 that a ring took crores of taka from thousands of people in the name of sending them to the United States, through well-furnished offices in upmarket areas of Dhaka and eye-catching advertisements <sup>[N402](#n402)</sup>. According to the column, the advertisements say "Do you want to settle in America?" and "Get a green card in America by investing only Tk 50 to 60 lakh"; the writer says they take Tk 50 to 60 lakh a head only to bring a person across the southern or northern border of the United States, and that the dalals and traffickers do not care what happens after arrival or whether the person is caught <sup>[N402](#n402)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-3"></a>

#### 4.5.2 The path

- **Through Suriname.** According to a US Embassy case statement of February 2024, one of the accused, Mofizur Rahman, traffics people from Bangladesh to Dubai and, with other dalals, to the United States, and sent his own brother from Bangladesh first through India to Suriname in South America, from where the brother went to the United States across the Mexico border <sup>[N338 (2024)](#n338)</sup>.
- **Through Paraguay.** According to the same statement, another accused, Ashraful Alam Bhuiyan, said in his visa interview that he helps traffic people to the United States through Paraguay in South America <sup>[N338 (2024)](#n338)</sup>.
- **Eleven countries in nine months.** Hossain, a rice farmer from Feni whose land had flooded repeatedly and who said ruling-party members were threatening him over his opposition politics, sold his land and borrowed about USD 33,000 from relatives and friends, raising USD 41,000 in all for travel and the dalal's costs, according to an investigation by Columbia Journalism Investigations (CJI) and Documented republished by Prothom Alo in October 2025 <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>. He left Bangladesh in February 2023 and crossed 11 countries by air and land over nine months before giving himself up to border officers in the US state of Arizona, who held him for three days in a room with 15 people and then released him <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-2"></a>

#### 4.5.3 What happens

- **Asylum.** A legal firm in Manhattan filed Hossain's asylum application in April 2024 on the ground that he supports a Bangladeshi opposition party; the effect of climate-driven floods on his income and livelihood is not an accepted ground for asylum, and the investigation says the United States has no specific provision protecting people who flee climate disasters <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.
- **Work while waiting.** While waiting for his hearing in the immigration court, Hossain got a US work permit after seven months; he worked first as a kitchen helper in a local restaurant, then delivering food by bike in Brooklyn, and in 2025 worked full time, six days a week, at a Bengali sweet shop and cafe in Kensington, Brooklyn, sharing a nearby basement apartment with two roommates <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>. The investigation says Bangladeshi migrants arriving in New York first settle around the Bangladeshi grocery shops and restaurants of Kensington, and that an estimated 1 lakh Bangladeshis live in New York City <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.
- **Detention.** Officers of US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) detained two Bangladeshis from Noakhali who were working in Buffalo, New York, and Hossain keeps copies of his asylum application and court hearing notices to hand and his lawyer's number on speed dial in case ICE comes to his home <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.
- **Family left behind.** Hossain had hoped that once granted asylum he could apply to bring his wife and daughter; he said that had he known how hard it now is, he would not have come to the United States <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.
- **Fake lawyers in the US.** Dr Delwar Arif wrote that, according to reports aired by ABC's Channel 7, MSN and Bangla channels in the United States, a Bangladeshi in New York who posed as an immigration lawyer without being one, and his firm, took millions of dollars from Bangladeshi and other legal and illegal immigration-seekers in the United States, USD 60 thousand from one person alone <sup>[N402](#n402)</sup>. He writes that in nearly two decades in the United States he has heard of many cases of people being cheated through Bangladeshi immigration lawyers, that many highly educated Bangladeshis are among the victims, and that US embassies could protect would-be migrants through public awareness campaigns and joint work with local law enforcement <sup>[N402](#n402)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-3"></a>

#### 4.5.4 The numbers

- **Growth:** according to CJI and Documented's analysis of US Customs and Border Protection records, border crossings from Bangladesh rose 150% from 2019 to 2024 <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.
- **Home districts:** in the same analysis Noakhali is the top district of origin, with more than 30% of the Bangladeshis detained at the border, and Feni is among the top 10 Bangladeshi towns from which people reached the US-Mexico border <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.
- **2024 detentions:** among people detained in 2024 whose records the investigation examined, about 100 were born in Bangladesh, against more than 520 born in Guatemala and about 350 in Senegal <sup>[N403](#n403)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-2"></a>

#### 4.5.5 The law

- **Bangladesh:** a US Embassy official filed a case at Gulshan police station, Dhaka, in February 2024 against five Bangladeshis on charges of involvement in human trafficking; the case says two of them, Mofizur Rahman (54) and Ashraful Alam Bhuiyan (33), went for US visa interviews on 11 February and admitted during the interviews that they are involved in trafficking people from Bangladesh to various countries and work mainly as dalals <sup>[N338 (2024)](#n338)</sup>. The case names two associates, Mohammad Zaman and Bhasani, and says a man named Mohammad Nur Alam heads the ring; the police Counter Terrorism and Transnational Crime (CTTC) unit was given the investigation, and its deputy commissioner said in February 2024 that the process of formally taking over the case file from Gulshan police station had begun <sup>[N338 (2024)](#n338)</sup>.

<a id="the-visitor-visa-bond"></a>

#### 4.5.6 The visitor visa bond

- **The rule.** From 21 January 2026, Bangladeshi citizens approved for a US business or tourist visa (B1/B2) must post a bond of up to USD 15,000 (about Tk 18 lakh 35 thousand at Tk 122.31 a dollar), the US Embassy in Dhaka said in a notice on its verified Facebook page on 19 January 2026; the condition does not apply to holders of valid B1/B2 visas issued before that date <sup>[N240](#n240)</sup>. The US State Department's travel website said on 6 January 2026 that citizens of 38 countries including Bangladesh must post the bond, and the embassy's notice said the money is returned if the visa's conditions are properly followed <sup>[N240](#n240)</sup>.
- **How it is paid.** An applicant found eligible after the visa interview is given payment instructions by the consular officer with a direct link to pay.gov, and must pay the bond within 30 days, the embassy said on 26 January 2026 <sup>[N404](#n404)</sup>.
- **When it is lost.** The visa issued under the bond pilot programme is single-entry and valid for at most three months, the holder must enter the United States through certain designated ports of entry, and the bond is cancelled automatically and the money returned after the holder comes home if all the visa's conditions are met, among them not working in the United States and returning within the set time, the embassy said <sup>[N404](#n404)</sup>.

<a id="visit-and-tourist-visas-sold-as-jobs"></a>

### 4.6 Visit and tourist visas sold as jobs

<a id="how-it-is-sold-4"></a>

#### 4.6.1 How it is sold

The Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare warned in February 2025 that brokers lure people with pay and jobs and send them on tourist or visit visas to the Middle East, Russia, Greece, Libya, Lebanon, Egypt, Cambodia, Malaysia and elsewhere <sup>[140](#o140)</sup>. Brokers tell people they have a work visa: to Saudi Arabia, allegedly, a three-month tourist visa sold with a promised 15-month iqama (residence permit); to Cambodia, a one or two-month tourist visa sold as a two-year work visa <sup>[N181 (2024)](#n181), [N165](#n165)</sup>. Women are sent to Dubai on tourist or visit visas for promised jobs in dance bars, beauty parlours, restaurants or homes <sup>[N350](#n350)</sup>. Some victims went with BMET clearance and still landed on a visit visa <sup>[N405](#n405)</sup>. Ali Akbar Khan, acting additional inspector general of police at the CID (the Criminal Investigation Department of the police), said at a Prothom Alo roundtable in July 2026 that many people go abroad on tourist visas and are then cheated, and that some go from being victims to becoming traffickers themselves <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. For Canada, a Prothom Alo report from Canada in April 2024 said each person who came on a visit visa had spent Tk 5 to 15 lakh on it, because the visa is document-based and dishonest rings made the financial and other documents for them <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>. The Maldives gives Bangladeshi tourists a visa at the airport, and dalals use this to cheat workers <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-country-by-country"></a>

#### 4.6.2 The path, country by country

- **UAE (Dubai):**
  - The UAE market closed to Bangladeshis in 2012 after complaints about workers; about 25,000 a year still went in 2014 and 2015, 1,000 in 2020, and sending resumed in 2021 <sup>[N407 (2024)](#n407)</sup>.
  - After the Covid pandemic the UAE began taking workers on travel visas, with the employer applying after arrival to change it to an employment visa; agency owners said in 2024 that a ring used this to cheat workers, many of whom found no work and lived on the streets and in parks <sup>[N407 (2024)](#n407)</sup>. Shakirul Islam, chair of OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant workers' organisation), said workers who went on travel visas had to pay brokers again to get an employment visa <sup>[N407 (2024)](#n407)</sup>. Tipu Sultan, joint secretary general of BAIRA (the Bangladesh Association of International Recruiting Agencies), said many found no work, which damaged Bangladesh's image and reduced interest in its workers <sup>[N407 (2024)](#n407)</sup>.
  - BAIRA's secretary general said in June 2024 that the UAE does not give visas for hiring in the unskilled sector and wants workers in professional categories, where Bangladeshi workers lack certificates, although the visa type can be changed after arriving <sup>[N407 (2024)](#n407)</sup>. An employer found connected with a worker who has become illegal is fined AED 50,000 (UAE dirhams), one reason UAE employers became unwilling to take Bangladeshis <sup>[N407 (2024)](#n407)</sup>.
  - The UAE restricted both work and visit visas for Bangladeshis in July 2024, and turning a visit visa into a work visa has been closed to Bangladeshis for years <sup>[N7](#n7), [N408](#n408)</sup>. Dubai is the usual transit point to Libya and has also been used for Kyrgyzstan and Russia <sup>[N361](#n361), [N409 (2024)](#n409), [N410](#n410)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Can a Bangladeshi on a UAE visit visa have it changed to an employment visa inside the UAE in 2026? Agency leaders said in June 2024 that it could be, a January 2026 report says it has been closed for several years, and the Bangladesh Embassy said in September 2025 that no UAE visa ban had been announced.

- **Saudi Arabia:** a visit visa can't be turned into an iqama; offers to convert one for a fee are fraud <sup>[N153 (2022)](#n153)</sup>. Saudi Arabia stopped new business, tourist and family visit visas for Bangladeshis from April to June 2025 after people used them to do Hajj <sup>[N411](#n411), [N412](#n412)</sup>. An immigration official said traffickers sometimes use a valid visa for one country, such as a tourist or Umrah (pilgrimage) visa, before sending people elsewhere <sup>[N350](#n350)</sup>.
- **Oman:** since 31 October 2023 a visit visa can't be converted to a work visa in Oman; the person must leave and come back on a work visa <sup>[N10 (2023)](#n10)</sup>.
- **Kuwait:** every visit visa holder is barred from working and must leave at the end of the visit <sup>[182](#o182)</sup>. Only some visit visas can be converted, for KD 150 (Kuwaiti dinars) <sup>[183](#o183)</sup>.
- **Qatar:** a visit visa can't be converted inside Qatar to a work visa <sup>[123](#o123)</sup>. In 2014, 23 Bangladeshis brought on tourist visas with job promises had no work for three months, overstayed and were arrested <sup>[N413 (2014)](#n413)</sup>.
- **Malaysia:** in October 2026 the CID arrested a Paltan travel agency owner accused of taking two people to Malaysia on visit visas with a promise of work permits, holding them and demanding Tk 5 lakh from their families <sup>[N414](#n414)</sup>. In September 2026 Malaysian immigration detained 147 Bangladeshis on Sepang building sites where employers had misused social visit passes <sup>[N415](#n415)</sup>. A woman taken to Malaysia on a visit visa on 15 November 2025 for a promised restaurant job said her passport, ticket and papers were taken on arrival and she was told she would get no work unless she paid the balance, though she had agreed to pay the last Tk 5,000 after starting work (see [A visit or tourist visa sold as a job](#a-visit-or-tourist-visa-sold-as-a-job)) <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Maldives:** workers land through Kuala Lumpur, Chennai or Malé with a forged 12-month work visa that is really a three-month tourist visa <sup>[N71 (2023)](#n71)</sup>. Some workers went in on the airport tourist visa without the embassy's attestation, and in 2024 they were being identified <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>. The High Commission says a tourist can never get a work visa there, and trying to work on one can mean jail and a fine <sup>[184](#o184)</sup>.
- **Canada:** in 2024 Canada announced that people who found an LMIA-approved job (one for which no one already allowed to work in Canada could be found; LMIA is the Labour Market Impact Assessment) could come and work temporarily, and many Bangladeshis who came on visit visas without understanding this found no such job and claimed political asylum <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-4"></a>

#### 4.6.3 The numbers

- **UAE:** BMET data show about 8,500 workers a month went to the UAE in 2022, 8,200 a month in 2023 and 6,500 a month in the first four months of 2024 <sup>[N407 (2024)](#n407)</sup>.
- **Maldives:** the market closed in 2019, when 4,411 workers went; after the pandemic began the Maldives caught workers without valid papers and sent them home, 16,000 between March and December 2021, then regularised about 40,000 irregular Bangladeshis before the market reopened in December 2023 <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>. In May 2024 there were about 100,000 legal Bangladeshi workers there and possibly at least 30,000 more irregular ones <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-3"></a>

#### 4.6.4 What happens

- **No legal work:** the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare says a tourist or visit visa gives no legal scope to work, so people sent on one suffer financial and mental harm and are put at risk, and deaths are occurring on illegal routes <sup>[N241](#n241)</sup>.
- **Debt bondage and sex work:** women taken to Dubai have passports and phones taken, are confined, and are given invented debts of USD 10,000 to 15,000 that some are forced to work off in sex work <sup>[N350](#n350)</sup>.
- **Ransom:** the Malaysia visit-visa case above ended in a ransom demand <sup>[N414](#n414)</sup>.
- **Overstay and deportation:** visit visas expire, the person becomes illegal and is arrested (Qatar 2014, Kuwait, Maldives) <sup>[N413 (2014)](#n413), [182](#o182), [184](#o184)</sup>.
- **Airport collusion:** allegations reported in November 2026 say brokers pay some airport staff Tk 30,000 to 50,000 per passenger, called a "body contract", to pass immigration with incomplete papers <sup>[N350](#n350)</sup>.
- **Canada, as reported in April 2024** (see also Asylum claims built on a false story) <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>:
  - Travellers were being turned back at airports before entering Canada, documents were checked again on landing, and those with fake invitation letters and fake documents were sent home at once <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.
  - Visit-visa applicants had shown a good job or business, large bank balances and ties at home, then told a different story to claim asylum, so many were expected to lose their cases and be removed <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.
  - An asylum claimant must prove his life is under threat at home and must hire an immigration lawyer, who charges at least CAD 7,000 to 10,000; a claimant who loses is removed <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.
  - Claimants got roughly 700 dollars a month from the government until they found work and had to hand over their passports, so could not go home; thousands could find no work for lack of English, relevant experience and education <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.
  - Many were living on food banks, which had cut what they give as crowds grew, and were bringing large sums from home through hundi to fight their cases <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.
  - A ring inside Canada was sending people on to the United States across unguarded border points near Montreal, and various sources said many were held in US jails <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-3"></a>

#### 4.6.5 The law

- **Bangladesh:** the Ministry's rule is never go to work on a tourist or visit visa on a broker's promise, and never go without BMET clearance <sup>[140](#o140)</sup>. The same notice says to confirm with BMET that the job and the recruiting agency are genuine, and not to go abroad for work through anyone other than a valid recruiting agency <sup>[N241](#n241)</sup>. Taking money on a false promise of a job abroad is punished by up to 5 years in prison and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Destinations:** Kuwait bars every visit visa holder from working, and in the Maldives trying to work on a tourist visa can mean jail and a fine (see The path, country by country) <sup>[182](#o182), [184](#o184)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-2"></a>

#### 4.6.6 Where to get help

The Probash-Bondhu call centre answers workers and families 24 hours (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[101](#o101)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does a Dubai or Saudi tourist visa "with job" sell for in Dhaka in 2026?

<a id="free-visas-and-visa-trading-in-the-gulf"></a>

### 4.7 Free visas and visa trading in the Gulf

<a id="how-it-is-sold-5"></a>

#### 4.7.1 How it is sold

A "free visa" (also called "azad visa", meaning a free or independent visa) is a visa the worker buys that is not tied to a real job <sup>[N358](#n358)</sup>. Under kafala, the Gulf sponsorship system, the visa is issued for one employer, and the sponsor lets the worker work elsewhere in return for a fee <sup>[N416 (2016)](#n416)</sup>. Brokers and some agencies tell workers they will easily find work and earn a lot once there, and charge Tk 4 to 7 lakh for Qatar <sup>[N35](#n35), [N174](#n174)</sup>. Dr Selim Reza of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies wrote in May 2026 that in practice a "free visa" means sending a worker without any specific job, a form of visa trading from which brokers, recruiting agencies and some dishonest businessmen profit <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>. OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant rights group) found free-visa migrants to the Gulf paid about Tk 5 lakh, against the government-set average of Tk 1.16 lakh <sup>[N31](#n31)</sup>. Of those who knowingly bought one, 54% went through illegal broker networks, 31% through distant relatives, 8% through family and 7% directly through recruiting agencies <sup>[N31](#n31)</sup>. In Kuwait an organised ring sells visas for lakhs of taka under the "free visa" name <sup>[N417](#n417)</sup>.

Qatar's own request is for skilled workers: at the seventh Bangladesh-Qatar joint committee meeting on 18 May 2026 Qatar showed interest in technical workers, especially electricians, plumbers, welders and AC technicians, and its labour minister said Qatar wants skilled workers directly from five specific TTCs (Technical Training Centres) in Bangladesh <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Which five TTCs did Qatar name, and how does a trainee there get onto that direct channel?

<a id="what-happens-4"></a>

#### 4.7.2 What happens

- **No job:** 43% of free-visa migrants found no work on arrival <sup>[N418](#n418)</sup>. The visa is tied to a sponsor with no job, and most end up in low-paid work or broken contracts <sup>[N417](#n417)</sup>. Workers who spend Tk 4 to 7 lakh on a Qatar "free visa" find a company's name on the papers but no work behind it, Selim Reza wrote in May 2026 <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>. A Bangladeshi worker in Riyadh wrote in August 2025 that dalal rings bring workers through various agencies on false promises but cannot give them work, so many hunt for work themselves; he had seen cheated workers sleeping on the street and one preparing to kill himself under the weight of debt <sup>[N124](#n124)</sup>.
- **Paying again:** 21% had to pay about Tk 1.5 lakh more after arrival to get an iqama, and 14% could not pay, got no permit and stayed illegally <sup>[N31](#n31)</sup>. In Qatar, sponsors sometimes demand regular payments to keep sponsoring <sup>[N419](#n419)</sup>.
- **Absconding cases:** brokers in Kuwait file false "absconding" (runaway) reports to free up visa quotas for resale, and some sponsors in Oman report free-visa workers as absconding so they are not punished if the worker is caught elsewhere <sup>[N420](#n420), [N416 (2016)](#n416)</sup>.
- **No rights:** in Qatar a free-visa worker has no legal help if unpaid <sup>[66 (2021)](#o66)</sup>. Free-visa workers there hunt for illegal day labour and many face harassment, arrest or deportation <sup>[N35](#n35), [N174](#n174)</sup>.
- **Qatar in 2026:** Selim Reza wrote in May 2026 that countless Bangladeshi workers wait for temporary work in and around Doha, some get no work for weeks, and many go unpaid for months <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>. He wrote that thousands are without work or pay, a large share jobless after being cheated through visa trading and broker rings, some undocumented and many without food or medical care <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>. After the 2022 football World Cup, he wrote, employment fell in much of Qatar's construction sector as companies wound up projects and laid off workers, so there are fewer openings than before <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>.
- **Saudi Arabia:** Amnesty International says migrant workers there have been cheated by recruitment brokers, forced to work extra hours and had wages stolen, in some cases probably amounting to trafficking for labour exploitation; IOM (the International Organization for Migration) lists irregular migration, hidden work such as domestic or informal work, undue employer control, recruitment corruption and dependence on third-party brokers among the risks migrants face there <sup>[N116](#n116)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-4"></a>

#### 4.7.3 The law

- **Bangladesh:** BMET says there is no such thing as a free visa <sup>[34](#o34)</sup>. Buying or selling a visa or work permit inside Bangladesh, or getting one illegally, is punished by 2 to 7 years' rigorous imprisonment <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. These offences are non-bailable <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>. Selim Reza wrote in May 2026 that it must be ensured no worker can go abroad without a contract for a specific job, and that sending workers on a "free visa" or uncertain employment must stop <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>.
- **Qatar:** visa trading, and working on a free visa, carry up to 3 years in prison and a fine of up to QR 50,000 (Qatari riyals) <sup>[80 (2015)](#o80), [66 (2021)](#o66)</sup>.
- **Oman:** an Omani caught granting free visas is fined 1,000 rials, but the practice is widespread <sup>[N51 (2021)](#n51)</sup>.
- **Kuwait:** the Bangladesh ambassador says there is no free visa in Kuwait law <sup>[N421](#n421)</sup>.
- **Maldives:** the High Commission says there is no free visa; working for another company brings arrest and deportation <sup>[164 (2024)](#o164), [N422](#n422)</sup>. Its notice of 22 May 2024 said there is no scope to work outside the approved employer and that a number of unskilled workers had been sent home for breaking this law <sup>[N172 (2024)](#n172)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-5"></a>

#### 4.7.4 The numbers

- **OKUP 2025:** of 1,084 migrants studied, 51% travelled on so-called free visas <sup>[N175](#n175)</sup>.
- **Kuwait:** visa trading and airfares push costs to 5 to 6 times the government rate of Tk 1.06 lakh <sup>[N423](#n423)</sup>.
- **Qatar:** about 473,000 Bangladeshis work there, about 30% on development projects, Qatar's labour minister said on his visit to Dhaka from 17 May 2026 <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>; 107,598 workers went from Bangladesh to Qatar in 2023, by the overseas employment ministry's data <sup>[N174](#n174)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-3"></a>

#### 4.7.5 Where to get help

BMET asks anyone handed such a visa to have it and the other papers checked at BMET in Dhaka or the District Employment and Manpower Office; after it goes wrong, anyone cheated can complain to the government under the 2013 Act (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[34](#o34), [26](#o26)</sup>.

<a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings"></a>

### 4.8 Malaysia: boats and land crossings

<a id="how-it-is-sold-6"></a>

#### 4.8.1 How it is sold

About 17,777 workers with BMET clearance failed to reach Malaysia before the 31 May 2024 deadline <sup>[N249 (2024)](#n249)</sup>.

**By land:** a Bangladeshi smuggling ring broken up in Malaysia in July 2026 is believed to have charged up to RM15,000 (Malaysian ringgit) per person and made about RM3.2 million since January 2026 <sup>[N354](#n354)</sup>. A group caught in Thailand in April 2026 had paid brokers Tk 7 lakh each <sup>[N353](#n353)</sup>.

**By sea, to Rohingya in the camps:** brokers lure Rohingya refugees from the Cox's Bazar camps onto trawlers with good jobs in Malaysia, and girls with a promise of marriage there, taking Tk 20,000 to 30,000 a head before boarding, police and local representatives said in October 2024 <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>. In April 2025 police said the people on one seized trawler had been promised good jobs in Malaysia or marriage to a suitable man, many girls had been married over the phone, and most had paid Tk 30,000 to 50,000 <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>. Researchers of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies wrote in January 2024 that Rohingya men already in Malaysia persuade parents in the camps to marry them their young daughters without dowry on the promise that the wife can then go safely to Malaysia, and use Facebook, Imo and WhatsApp to draw young women; some who set out die at sea, some are sold as slaves in Myanmar, and some who reach Malaysia are sold there and cut off from their families <sup>[N425 (2024)](#n425)</sup>. The same researchers wrote that traffickers use the family and friends of Rohingya already in Malaysia to persuade others in the camps to go, with promises of help after arrival <sup>[N425 (2024)](#n425)</sup>.

**By sea, to Bangladeshis:** a camp-in-charge of the Refugee Relief and Repatriation Commission (RRRC, the government office that runs the Rohingya camps) said in June 2026 that Bangladeshis as well as Rohingya take the sea route, especially teenage girls taken to Malaysia and Indonesia with the lure of a "better husband" <sup>[N426](#n426)</sup>. Local brokers mainly target teenagers and young men, promising a job paying a lakh of taka with little outlay; people aged 18 to 35 with money are offered onward travel through Malaysia and Indonesia to Australia, the United States or New Zealand, and women are promised good jobs in hotels, motels, parlours or shopping malls, Prothom Alo reported in October 2024 <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>. The eight Bangladeshis rescued in a Teknaf raid on 15 October 2024 came from Patenga in Chattogram and from Teknaf, Cox's Bazar municipality, Maheshkhali, Ramu and Cox's Bazar Sadar, and the police officer-in-charge said an organised ring had gathered them for Malaysia in exchange for large sums of money <sup>[N428 (2024)](#n428)</sup>.

**"Go free, pay after you arrive":**
- Brokers target unemployed, needy young men of poor families and offer to take them to Malaysia by boat for free, saying they can pay after reaching Malaysia and promising very high wages, Parvez Siddiqui, executive director of the NGO Films for Peace Foundation, said in April 2026; the wife of one missing man, Belal Uddin, said he went because brokers told him he could go without money <sup>[N429](#n429)</sup>.
- Nur Kamal (21) of Daganbhuiyan, Feni, told Prothom Alo in November 2026 that someone added him to a WhatsApp group offering to take him to Malaysia free of cost, with payment due only after arrival; he left home on 2 November and on reaching Cox's Bazar was taken to a hut in the Teknaf hills, held under 10 to 12 armed guards and beaten <sup>[N430](#n430)</sup>.
- Kamal (a pseudonym) of Teknaf made a Tk 1 lakh deal to go to Malaysia, to be repaid after he found a good job <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>.

**A first payment, then extortion:** police said a house in Baharchhara, Teknaf, raided in December 2024 was a ring's den: people were gathered there through brokers with promises of a better life, jobs and, for unmarried women, marriage; those brought from the Rohingya camps were first charged Tk 20,000 each, and the ring intended to hold them and extort more money by force on the pretext of getting them to Malaysia <sup>[N431 (2024)](#n431)</sup>.

**Other lures:**

- A social-media friendship and an invitation to visit, which ends in abduction or trafficking, according to the Cox's Bazar police chief in June 2026; he said some fishermen think trafficking people pays more than fishing <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.
- A day out: in September 2026 traffickers took five teenagers and young men from Mithachhari, Ramu, by CNG auto-rickshaw on the pretext of visiting Teknaf's Marine Drive <sup>[N432](#n432)</sup>.
- Australia by way of Malaysia: brokers offered one college student this for Tk 12 lakh <sup>[N426](#n426)</sup>. People already in Malaysia are being drawn into fraud with assurances that they will be sent on to Australia, according to BRAC's Shariful Islam Hasan in July 2026 <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

**The season:** traffickers are active when the Bay of Bengal is calm, from October or November to March, police and local representatives said in October 2024 <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>. The Deputy Commissioner of Cox's Bazar said in June 2026 that the risk of trafficking by sea rises in winter, when the sea is calm <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-4"></a>

#### 4.8.2 The path

**By land:**

1. <a id="the-path-4-item-1"></a>**Fly to Cambodia** <sup>[N353](#n353), [N354](#n354)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-4-item-2"></a>**Cross into Thailand on foot**, then be moved south in stages, kept in hotels and resorts <sup>[N353](#n353)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-4-item-3"></a>**Cross into Malaysia** through the Kelantan border, then wait in a transit house <sup>[N354](#n354)</sup>.

**By sea:**

1. <a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings-item-1"></a>**Held in the hills:** people brought from around the country are kept in the hills and other places near Cox's Bazar, the Coast Guard's East Zone commander said in June 2026 <sup>[N426](#n426)</sup>. In 2025 rings worked in stages, bringing Rohingya out of the camps and driving them to the Kachchhapia area of Teknaf, police said <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>.
2. <a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings-item-2"></a>**Out to the boat at night:** small boats take them in the dark to a large trawler or ship; the coastline is 710 km long and a boat can be launched from any point on it <sup>[N426](#n426), [N99](#n99)</sup>. Departures were rising in October 2024 from the coasts of Teknaf, Ukhiya, Ramu, Cox's Bazar Sadar and Maheshkhali, the Anwara and Banshkhali coasts of Chattogram, and the Karnaphuli river <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>.
3. <a id="malaysia-boats-and-land-crossings-item-3"></a>**Past St Martin's island through Myanmar waters to Thailand**, where passengers were promised they would be handed on and delivered to Malaysia <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>. Centre for Migration Studies researchers wrote in January 2024 that traffickers first take Bangladeshis and Rohingya to Teknaf, then by boat to the Myanmar-Bangladesh sea border and on in small boats to Myanmar's Samila coast, where migrants pay traffickers in advance for the leg to Malaysia or Australia, in some cases more than was agreed in Teknaf; returnees put the cost of reaching Malaysia this way at Tk 4 to 5 lakh <sup>[N425 (2024)](#n425)</sup>.

Some journeys sold as going through Malaysia end in Myanmar instead: one man promised Australia by way of Malaysia later realised he had been taken into Myanmar, walked for several days over hills and was detained there <sup>[N426](#n426), [N99](#n99)</sup>. Eight Bangladeshis rescued off Teknaf in September 2026 told police they had been taken to Myanmar about a year earlier on the promise of Malaysia and kept there for a year <sup>[N433](#n433)</sup>.

The boat route is older: in 2012 to 2015 thousands tried boat journeys, and trafficking camps and mass graves were found on the Thailand-Malaysia border <sup>[N434](#n434)</sup>. BRAC's Shariful Islam Hasan, who reported on it then as a journalist, puts the number trafficked in that period at about one lakh, and said rescued Bangladeshis, many of them teenagers, were sent home in 2015 with their hands and feet in chains <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>. The first mass grave, with over 30 bodies, was found in April 2015 near Wang Kelian <sup>[N435 (2023)](#n435)</sup>. The dead had no names and their graves were marked only with numbers <sup>[N426](#n426)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-5"></a>

#### 4.8.3 What happens

- **On the boat:** there is no food, clean water or toilet, people die and the bodies are thrown into the sea, the Coast Guard's East Zone commander said in June 2026; the torture begins once people are on board <sup>[N99](#n99), [N426](#n426)</sup>. Kamal of Teknaf said that during three months at sea passengers got plain rice, chilli and a glass of water once a day, those who died of hunger were thrown into the sea, anyone who asked for food or water was beaten, and the brokers collected money from families while torturing passengers <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>. The state minister for expatriates' welfare said in June 2026, from victims' and families' accounts, that brokers hold people hostage and take large sums from their families, many die on the way, and those who reach a shore are detained by police there <sup>[N426](#n426)</sup>. Reports of about 500 Rohingya deaths had come in during one week of July 2026, BRAC said <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Ransom on the way:** before reaching Malaysia, Rohingya are held at the Thai or Myanmar border while ransom is demanded from relatives in the camps, and if that fails there are attempts to abduct family members, a Balukhali camp leader said in January 2025 <sup>[N436](#n436)</sup>.
- **Ransom without leaving Bangladesh:** every winter rings hold camp Rohingya for ransom in the name of the sea journey, and sometimes sail a big trawler around for several days, leave the passengers somewhere in Cox's Bazar and run off with the money, Teknaf's police chief said in December 2024 <sup>[N437 (2024)](#n437)</sup>. People held in a Teknaf hill den in November 2024 said they were told to call relatives for money and say they were in Myanmar, were tortured and not fed properly, and were held for eight to more than 13 days <sup>[N438 (2024)](#n438)</sup>. Organised rings bring Bangladeshis and Rohingya to the Teknaf coast with promises of high-paid jobs abroad and a better life, then lock them in secret hideouts and extract ransom through torture, the Coast Guard's Teknaf station in-charge said on 6 June 2026 <sup>[N439](#n439)</sup>.
- **Sold into the dens:** victims freed from a Teknaf den on 5 November 2026 said people were handed to the traffickers for money: S M Asaduzzaman (50) of Saltha, Faridpur, said a man he met in Kutupalong camp lured him with work and handed him over for Tk 10,000, after which he agreed a Tk 2 lakh release deal; Md Saiful Islam of Ukhiya said a broker named Nur Ali handed him over and the traffickers said they had bought him and another man for Tk 1 lakh, and that captives who tried to flee were beaten and chained <sup>[N430](#n430)</sup>.
- **Abduction for ransom:** at least 193 people, 86 of them Rohingya, were abducted in Teknaf in the year to January 2025 and at least 98 freed on ransom; police found links to trafficking and to some local residents <sup>[N436](#n436)</sup>.
- **Caught in Thailand:** 21 Bangladeshis found in a resort in Chana in April 2026 were charged with illegal entry and stay <sup>[N353](#n353)</sup>.
- **Jailed in Myanmar:** Mohammad Jamal of Cox's Bazar, who spent 11 months 19 days in a Myanmar jail after his trawler broke up in 2023, said Bangladeshi prisoners there did all the work, from growing vegetables to cooking, and were fed rice-water at about 7 am, some rice with dried chillies at 11 am and rice once more at 4 pm; the government brought 173 Bangladeshis home from Myanmar's prisons in April 2024 <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>.
- **Caught in Malaysia:** 15 smuggled Bangladeshis were caught in a Cheras transit house in July 2026 and taken to the Putrajaya immigration depot <sup>[N354](#n354)</sup>. Malaysia's Home Minister reported 465 deaths in immigration depots in 2021 to 2025 <sup>[N440](#n440)</sup>.
- **Survivors charged at home:** the nine survivors of a sinking in April 2026 were taken to Teknaf police; the three Rohingya were sent back to the camps after giving statements in court, and the six Bangladeshis were charged under the trafficking prevention law and sent to jail, with 10 to 15 unnamed accused added, police inspector Sukanta Chowdhury said <sup>[N429](#n429)</sup>.
- **Going home:** Malaysia's PRM 2.0 (Migrant Repatriation Programme) lets undocumented migrants leave; Bangladeshis were the largest group in it <sup>[N441](#n441), [N442](#n442)</sup>.
- **Families of the missing:** Manik Molla of Teknaf said in October 2024 that he had been looking for seven and a half years for his youngest brother Shakil, who left by sea for Malaysia at 17; about two years earlier a broker told him Shakil was in a Malaysian prison, took money from the family, and then disappeared <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>. Sohel, son of the widow Hasina Akter of Karachi Para village, Teknaf, boarded a boat for Malaysia and was not heard from again; the government told her he was held in a Malaysian prison, and she said the government would bring him back once all papers were submitted, Prothom Alo reported in October 2024 <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>.
- **Silence at home:** families who lose someone at sea or are cheated often don't want to admit it, which makes the crime hard to investigate, the Deputy Commissioner of Cox's Bazar said in June 2026 <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.

<a id="cases-1"></a>

#### 4.8.4 Cases

- 20 June 2014: Nurul Islam of Lohagara, Chattogram, lured with a well-paid job in Malaysia, said he and another man were taken to the Dariyanagar coast in Cox's Bazar and put on a ship by trawler; on the Thai coast brokers tortured them and took Tk 4 lakh ransom before handing them to a broker in Malaysia <sup>[N443 (2024)](#n443)</sup>. Malaysian police arrested him as an illegal immigrant in June 2017, and after a year in prison the government brought him home in October 2018 <sup>[N443 (2024)](#n443)</sup>. He filed a trafficking case against six people in October 2018; in 2019 the 12-year-old brother of an accused was produced in the High Court in his place and given bail, and in May 2024 the Chattogram tribunal sent the man claiming to be that accused to jail and ordered police to find out who the real accused is <sup>[N443 (2024)](#n443)</sup>.
- July 2023: Mohammad Jamal of Cox's Bazar, persuaded by a broker named Ayub to go to Malaysia on a Tk 4 lakh deal, was taken with nine others by small boat from Gorjonia in Ramu upazila to a small fishing trawler carrying 73 people, five of them girls under 18; after three days the trawler's hull split at the Myanmar border, the passengers surrendered to Myanmar's navy, and he spent 11 months 19 days in a Myanmar jail before coming home with government help, Prothom Alo reported in October 2024 <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>.
- 5 Jan 2024: Cox's Bazar assistant judge Maitree Bhattacharya saw three Rohingya girls (two aged 12, one 14) from Balukhali camp being put on a wooden boat for Malaysia at Lombarighat on the Teknaf Marine Drive; police with her rescued them and arrested a trafficker, and in what was reported as the first Overseas Employment and Migrants Act case against Rohingya fleeing the camps, the girls were sent to jail with the trafficker, bailed on 9 January and were to be returned to the camp, from where they must attend court <sup>[N444 (2024)](#n444)</sup>.
- Told Oct 2024: Kamal (a pseudonym) of Teknaf made a Tk 1 lakh deal with a man named Abu to go to Malaysia, to be repaid after he found a good job; brokers pressed for Tk 3 lakh ransom, Malaysian police chased the boat into Indonesian waters, the Bangladeshis on board sank the trawler after two of them died in a quarrel with Rohingya passengers, the Indonesian navy rescued them, and after 11 months' detention there the Bangladesh government brought them home, at a total cost to him of Tk 2 lakh 30 thousand <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>.
- 12 Oct 2024: the Coast Guard seized a trawler carrying 17 Rohingya from Teknaf's Mochhoni camp at Shikalbaha in Karnaphuli upazila, Chattogram, and arrested three members of a trafficking ring <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>.
- 14 Oct 2024: brokers who had put more than a hundred Rohingya from the Ukhiya camps on a fishing trawler for Malaysia set them down at dawn on Inani beach after 10 days at sea; police and the Coast Guard detained 26 and the brokers hid <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>. Another 80 to 90 Rohingya escaped <sup>[N428 (2024)](#n428)</sup>. One woman said she had paid a broker Tk 30,000 for a good job in Malaysia; another passenger said the trawler left Teknaf, met the Myanmar navy south of St Martin's island and was turned back <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>.
- 15 Oct 2024: late at night Teknaf police raided a house in Dakshin Lambari, Teknaf Sadar union, and rescued 20 people gathered to be trafficked to Malaysia by sea, 12 Rohingya including women and children and 8 Bangladeshis, and detained three brokers, Luna Begum and Md Riduan of Dakshin Lambari and Md Abdullah of Kachubunia, Sabrang union, the Teknaf Model police station's officer-in-charge, Muhammad Gias Uddin, said <sup>[N428 (2024)](#n428)</sup>.
- 12 to 17 Nov 2024: Ekhlas Mia of Ukhiya and his cousin were abducted on the Marine Drive and held in a Teknaf hilltop den with men, women and children tortured for money; unable to pay, he was taken to the beach with 15 to 20 others to be sent by sea, slipped away and asked RAB (Rapid Action Battalion, an elite police force) to rescue the rest <sup>[N438 (2024)](#n438)</sup>. RAB rescued 31 people, 27 Rohingya and 4 Bangladeshis, from the den in the Kachchhapia hills; each had paid Tk 20,000 for the sea journey, the women promised good grooms and the men good jobs <sup>[N438 (2024)](#n438)</sup>. Two men were arrested and a case filed under the trafficking law <sup>[N438 (2024)](#n438)</sup>.
- 14 Dec 2024: Teknaf police rescued 30 Rohingya, 15 men, 3 women and 12 children, held for ransom in a house in Dakshin Lambari Para after being gathered for Malaysia with promises of jobs and marriage; the ring fled and nobody was arrested <sup>[N437 (2024)](#n437)</sup>.
- 29 Dec 2024: at about 4 am police raided Abdul Amin's house on a hilltop in Moddhom Kachchhapia, Baharchhara union, Teknaf, rescued 66 people gathered to be trafficked to Malaysia by sea (18 men, 11 women and 37 children; 5 Bangladeshis and 61 Rohingya from the camps), detained five members of the ring and recovered four rifle bullets, a ramdao and a kirich (machete and blade) <sup>[N431 (2024)](#n431)</sup>.
- 20 Jan 2025: at midnight the Navy and police rescued 19 people, two Bangladeshis and 17 Rohingya from the Ukhiya-Teknaf camps, gathered in huts at the house of Zafar Alam in the hilly Madhyam Kachchhapia area of Baharchhara union, Teknaf, to be trafficked to Malaysia by sea; Zafar Alam (30) was caught after a chase, the others fled, and a case was being prepared, the Teknaf officer-in-charge said <sup>[N445](#n445)</sup>.
- 25 Jan 2025: the Bangladesh Navy raided a traffickers' den in the Kachchhopia hills, Baharchhara union, Teknaf, rescued 17 men, women and children held hostage for trafficking to Malaysia by sea, 3 of them Bangladeshis and the rest Rohingya from the Ukhiya and Teknaf camps, and arrested a trafficker named Ali Abdullah, Lieutenant Commander Afzal Hossain of the Navy's Teknaf contingent said <sup>[N446](#n446)</sup>.
- 2 to 9 Apr 2025: an auto-rickshaw driver from Ukhiya said two passengers held him at gunpoint, took him to the Kachchhapia hills, kept him four days in a hut with 20 to 25 others and forced him onto a trawler on 7 April <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>. On 8 April the navy seized that trawler, FB Kulsuma, 44 nautical miles south-west of St Martin's island with 214 people aboard, 118 men, 68 women and 28 children, and no life-saving equipment or enough food and water; it had left Shaplapur beach in Teknaf <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>. The RRRC said action was being taken against 12 of the Rohingya for various crimes and the rest went back to the camps <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>. A woman married by phone was among them (see Marriage, family-visa and fake-nikahnama schemes) <sup>[N221](#n221)</sup>.
- 26 Mar to 9 Apr 2026: UNHCR (the UN refugee agency) said an overloaded boat that left Bangladesh on 26 March 2026 sank in rough sea with about 250 people missing, and only nine were rescued alive near the Andaman Islands on 9 April <sup>[N447](#n447)</sup>. The nine survivors of the sinking, which Prothom Alo dates to 8 April, were picked up from water drums and pieces of wood by the Bangladesh-flagged merchant ship Meghna Pride and handed to the Coast Guard; the six Bangladeshis among them were then jailed <sup>[N429](#n429)</sup>.
- 30 Mar 2026: the Coast Guard raided a house in Dakshin Lombori, Teknaf, which it called a traffickers' den, and rescued seven people including women and children who had been brought there, with promises of a better life, high-paid jobs and a cheap passage, to be taken to Malaysia by sea; the traffickers fled and the seven were handed to Teknaf Model Police Station <sup>[N448](#n448)</sup>.
- 4 Apr 2026: seven young men from Hajipara, Miapara and Natun Ghona villages of Rajakhali union, Pekua, Cox's Bazar, left the Teknaf coast on one of three trawlers that left for Malaysia at about the same time, phoned home until 8 April and have not been heard from since; the Teknaf brokers stopped answering their phones, and one missing man's uncle said Teknaf police only took photos and addresses and sent the family away <sup>[N429](#n429)</sup>.
- 5 Jun 2026: the Coast Guard chased and seized a fishing trawler off Baharchhara on the Teknaf coast that was taking people to Malaysia by sea, rescued 50 people including women and children (39 Bangladeshis and 11 Rohingya) and arrested nine traffickers, who were handed to Teknaf Model Police Station with a case filed, station in-charge Lt Cdr Md Muttakin Siddiqui said <sup>[N439](#n439)</sup>.
- Told June 2026: Tarekur Rahman of Maheshkhali said a broker promising an easy Malaysian visa took him to Myanmar, where he was beaten and threatened with death and Tk 1 lakh was taken from his family <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>. In the first report of his account, a further Tk 7 lakh was taken in Thailand and he was caught at the border on the way to Malaysia, jailed and sent home <sup>[N426](#n426)</sup>. In the fuller account, his family sold their house under torture, food sometimes came only after three or four days, and after another large payment for the onward leg he was caught near the border and held about seven months in a Myanmar prison <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.
- Told June 2026: Aung Aung U Jewel, a college student who could not afford the language tests needed to go legally, set off when brokers promised a cheap route to Australia by way of Malaysia for Tk 12 lakh; they took him into Myanmar, where after days of walking over hills he was detained and spent a month on remand and several months in prison <sup>[N426](#n426), [N99](#n99)</sup>. His family had no news of him until he sent his mother a letter through a Myanmar police officer, and the brokers took money from the family as a "rescue cost" <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>. When fighting began while he was held in Thandwe prison the prisoners were moved and made to work, and he later came home with the Bangladesh embassy's help <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.
- 11 to 20 Sep 2026: traffickers took five teenagers and young men (two aged 16, two 18 and one 20) from Mithachhari, Ramu, by CNG auto-rickshaw on the pretext of visiting Teknaf's Marine Drive, blindfolded and held them, beat them and collected ransom from their families in instalments while planning to send them by sea to Indonesia and Malaysia; SRB-15 (Special Response Battalion 15) rescued them on 20 September at Khonkarpara ghat near Sabrang Zero Point, Teknaf, and arrested Md Anas alias Munna (21) <sup>[N432](#n432)</sup>.
- 22 Sep 2026: a boat of irregular migrants from Myanmar's Rakhine state capsized off the Baharchhara coast of Teknaf at dawn, killing a Rohingya woman, Chhura Khatun (28), and her 9-year-old son; of the 16 rescued, eight were Bangladeshis who told police they had been taken to Myanmar about a year earlier on the promise of Malaysia, kept in various parts of Myanmar for a year, and put on the boat for Malaysia the night before <sup>[N433](#n433)</sup>.
- 1 Oct 2026: the navy ship BNS Khalid Bin Walid, alerted by fishermen, rescued 76 people from a wooden boat whose engine had failed and which was sinking about 81 km west of the St Martin's Island lighthouse; the navy said 65 men, women and children aboard, some lured with good jobs and some kidnapped and held in remote parts of Teknaf, were being trafficked to Malaysia by way of the Myanmar and Thai coasts, and it detained 11 alleged traffickers <sup>[N449](#n449)</sup>.
- 5 Nov 2026: a joint army, BGB (Border Guard Bangladesh) and police operation freed 57 people from a traffickers' den in the deep hills of Rangikhali, Teknaf, where they were being tortured for ransom and were to be put on a trawler to Malaysia that night; a soldier, Md Titas Sheikh (27), was shot dead in a gunfight, five ring members were detained, and police said one of the 57 was identified as a trafficker <sup>[N430](#n430)</sup>.
- 7 Nov 2026: BGB rescued nine people, including two aged 16 and 17, gathered at South Lombori ghat, Teknaf Sadar union, to be taken to Malaysia by sea, while 10 to 15 ring members fled by boat; the rescued said men were lured with good jobs and women with marriage to suitable grooms in Malaysia, the Teknaf BGB battalion commander said <sup>[N450](#n450)</sup>.
- 16 to 20 Nov 2026: 72 people, mostly Rohingya, who had paid brokers Tk 10,000 to 15,000 each, were taken by small fishing boats from Mithapanir Chhara ghat, Teknaf, to a large wooden trawler; after rough seas from a cyclone kept it near St Martin's Island and the crew and brokers quarrelled, the crew landed them at Laboni Point on Cox's Bazar beach late on 20 November and fled, and police rescued 28, mostly Rohingya and the rest from Teknaf, Naikhongchhari in Bandarban and other districts, while the rest ran away <sup>[N451](#n451)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Did the boat that sank near the Andaman Islands leave Bangladesh on 26 March 2026, as UNHCR said, or was it one of the trawlers that left Teknaf on 4 April and sank on 8 April <sup>[N447](#n447), [N429](#n429)</sup>?

<a id="the-numbers-6"></a>

#### 4.8.5 The numbers

- **2016 to 2023:** law enforcers rescued 1,134 people being taken to Malaysia by sea, 95% of them Rohingya; 85 cases were filed at Ukhiya and Teknaf police stations naming 1,200 accused, of whom 508 were caught <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>. Police and Rohingya leaders said in January 2024 that in the previous five years law enforcers had rescued at least 5,000 Rohingya being trafficked to Malaysia by trawler from the Teknaf, Ukhiya, Cox's Bazar Sadar and Maheshkhali coasts, and that none of those rescued had been charged or jailed <sup>[N444 (2024)](#n444)</sup>.
- **2018 to 2022:** by UNHCR's figures, cited by researchers of the Centre for Migration Studies in January 2024 and by Prothom Alo in October 2024, 8,312 Rohingya refugees tried to reach Malaysia by sea, and 589 of them died at sea <sup>[N425 (2024)](#n425), [N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>.
- **2024:** sea trafficking from Teknaf rose again from January 2024 after six or seven years of being largely stopped, and in the year to January 2025 at least 600 people, including 45 traffickers, were caught in eight or nine operations on the Teknaf coast <sup>[N436](#n436)</sup>. From Lambashia camp alone more than 500 young men and women were sent towards Malaysia in that year, and more than 300 were caught and brought back, a camp leader said <sup>[N436](#n436)</sup>.
- **January 2025:** Baharchhara union parishad chairman Amjad Hossain said that trafficking syndicates bring Rohingya from the Ukhiya-Teknaf camps to several points on the Baharchhara coast, lure them with promises of Malaysia, hold them in the hills and demand ransom, and that police, RAB and Navy operations had rescued about 150 such victims in January 2025 alone <sup>[N446](#n446)</sup>.
- **2025:** about 900 Rohingya refugees went missing or died in the Andaman Sea and Bay of Bengal, the deadliest year on record for sea journeys in South and South-East Asia, with one in seven of the more than 6,500 who attempted the crossing dying or going missing, according to UNHCR as reported by Prothom Alo on 17 April 2026 <sup>[N447](#n447)</sup>.
- **2026:** UNHCR said that more than half of the Rohingya crossing by sea in recent years were women and children, that more than 2,800 had taken the journey in 2026 up to 13 April, and that the boats usually leave Cox's Bazar in Bangladesh or Rakhine State in Myanmar for Indonesia or Malaysia <sup>[N447](#n447)</sup>.
- **Deaths and missing:** at least 300 people died in more than 20 trawler sinkings in the years to October 2024, according to Abdus Shukkur, a Cox's Bazar human rights lawyer, who said the rings especially target Rohingya girls <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424)</sup>. About 5,787 people went missing on illegal sea journeys in 2023 alone, about 25% of them women and children and half of them Bangladeshi citizens, according to an assistant representative of UNHCR (the UN refugee agency) in June 2026 <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>. About two lakh people have crossed the Bay of Bengal in the past decade and many have gone missing, according to BRAC's Shariful Islam Hasan <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does UNHCR's own publication give for people missing at sea on the Bay of Bengal route in 2023, and how many were women and children?
> - How many people were rescued from Malaysia-bound boats in 2016 to 2023: 1,134, or at least 5,000 Rohingya in the five years to January 2024 <sup>[N424 (2024)](#n424), [N444 (2024)](#n444)</sup>?

<a id="the-law-5"></a>

#### 4.8.6 The law

- **Malaysia:** anyone who entered unlawfully or holds forged travel documents is a prohibited immigrant <sup>[185 (2006)](#o185)</sup>. Smuggling cases are brought under the Anti-Trafficking in Persons and Anti-Smuggling of Migrants Act 2007, the Immigration Act and the Passports Act <sup>[N354](#n354)</sup>.
- **Thailand:** in 2017 Thailand convicted 62 people, including nine officials, over the deaths and trafficking of Rohingya and Bangladeshis to Malaysia <sup>[N435 (2023)](#n435)</sup>.
- **Bangladesh, against passengers:** in January 2024 police in Cox's Bazar said that Rohingya who flee the Ukhiya camps and try to go to Malaysia or another country by sea would from then on be charged under the Overseas Employment and Migrants Act and sent to jail <sup>[N444 (2024)](#n444)</sup>. Three Rohingya girls rescued that month were jailed with the trafficker, and six Bangladeshi survivors of an April 2026 sinking were charged under the trafficking prevention law and jailed <sup>[N444 (2024)](#n444), [N429](#n429)</sup>.
- **Bangladesh, in practice:** even when cases are filed, traffickers often get out of jail and go back to the same work, the Coast Guard's East Zone commander said in June 2026 <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>. Twenty abduction cases were filed at Teknaf police station in the year to January 2025, and its officer-in-charge said victims released on ransom do not want to complain <sup>[N436](#n436)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-4"></a>

#### 4.8.7 Where to get help

Bangladesh High Commission, Kuala Lumpur: No 8, Lorong Yap Kwan Seng, 50450 Kuala Lumpur; consular +60326040949 <sup>[186](#o186)</sup>. In Thailand: the Bangladesh Embassy in Bangkok (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[187](#o187)</sup>. In Myanmar: Tarekur Rahman said he got no real help from it in prison and came home on money his family sent, while Aung Aung U Jewel came home with its help <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>. Detained migrants have been brought home by the government from Myanmar's prisons (173 in April 2024) and from Indonesian detention <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does the Cambodia-Thailand-Malaysia land route cost in late 2026?
> - Which papers must a family submit, and to which office, for the government to bring home a relative held in a prison in Malaysia, Myanmar or Indonesia after a boat journey <sup>[N427 (2024)](#n427)</sup>?

<a id="india-women-and-girls-taken-across-the-land-border"></a>

### 4.9 India: women and girls taken across the land border

<a id="how-it-is-sold-7"></a>

#### 4.9.1 How it is sold

Traffickers usually target women from low-income families in financial trouble <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.

**Offers in reported cases:**

- a job in India, ending in a brothel <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>;
- a job in Canada <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>;
- TikTok: a ring trafficked women to India "in the name of TikTok" <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>;
- learning dance in India with a salary of Tk 50,000 a month, offered in January 2025 to a class-eight girl from the Facebook account of a girl she knew, whose mother used the same account and helped three girls set off <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>;
- cheaper cancer treatment in India and work there to pay for it, offered by a woman neighbour <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>;
- work through a cousin the relatives believed was a domestic worker in Maharashtra <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>;
- a love affair and marriage (see Marriage, family-visa and fake-nikahnama schemes) <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-5"></a>

#### 4.9.2 The path

1. <a id="the-path-5-item-1"></a>**A relay of handlers:** in February 2025 three girls took a bus from Dhaka to Jhenaidah; the conductor handed them to a man, who took them to a woman's house, and that evening a battery auto-rickshaw driver took them to a man at the Maheshpur border <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>. Each person has a separate task, so victims cannot say who took them from where, a Border Guard Bangladesh (BGB) battalion commander in Jashore said <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-5-item-2"></a>**The crossing:** routes run through Putkhali, Daulatpur, Gatipara, Sadipur and Rudrapur around Benapole in Jashore, through Maheshpur in Jhenaidah and the Satkhira border, with scattered cases through Tamabil in Sylhet and the Cumilla border <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-5-item-3"></a>**Sold:** rescued women have been found in brothels in Hyderabad, Maharashtra and Bengaluru <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.

**The prices:** traffickers take Tk 15,000 to 20,000 a person just to get someone across, far more than before because checks have tightened, and rescued women said they were told in the brothels that they had been bought for Tk 2 to 3 lakh, according to the director of Rights Jashore, a local rights organisation, in early 2025 <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-6"></a>

#### 4.9.3 What happens

- **Rescued in India:** the Indian Border Security Force (BSF) hands the person to the BGB and immigration police at the "zero line" of the Benapole border <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- **Jailed in India:** a 28-year-old Savar woman promised Canada was trafficked to Nadia district, West Bengal, and came home after seven months in jail there <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>.

<a id="cases-2"></a>

#### 4.9.4 Cases

- 2021: a young woman trafficked by the TikTok ring escaped back to Bangladesh on 7 May 2021 and on 1 June filed a case at Hatirjheel police station naming 12 people, among them Rifadul Islam alias Hridoy (26), "TikTok Hridoy" <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>. Three years later the Bangladesh investigation was still unfinished, although accused arrested in India over the same events had been tried and punished there; Salma Ali of BNWLA (Bangladesh National Woman Lawyers' Association) blamed the lack of accountability of investigators <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>.
- 10 Oct 2024: a 22-year-old divorced woman in Dhaka with cancer was trafficked to India after a neighbour's offer; Hyderabad police rescued her from a brothel on 21 November <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- Told Feb 2025: a 22-year-old woman from near the border in Jashore said she was taken to India on the promise of a job and sold to a brothel, where she saw three Bangladeshi girls; Indian police rescued her two years later and BNWLA rehabilitated her <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- 10 Feb 2025: BGB members chased the three Dhaka schoolgirls and the man taking them across the border at Maheshpur, Jhenaidah; one girl fell, all three were rescued, and the man, his face wrapped in a gamcha (cotton towel), escaped <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>. One girl filed a case at Maheshpur police station on 24 February; nobody had been arrested by 8 March <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- 11 Feb 2025: a 15-year-old girl from a poor family was trafficked after her cousin's offer; Maharashtra police rescued her on 23 February, and Justice and Care, an anti-trafficking organisation, said she had been used for sex work <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-7"></a>

#### 4.9.5 The numbers

- **Border Guard Bangladesh:** from August 2024 to January 2025, 145 people, traffickers included, were detained while being taken across illegally, 55 of them women and children, and 162 people were brought back from India <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- **Justice and Care:** in January 2024 to February 2025, 186 women and children were brought back from India and 133 rescued at the border, counting only BGB cases in the Jhenaidah, Chuadanga and Jashore areas <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>. The return of at least 10 women and girls rescued from brothels was in process in February 2025, Justice and Care said <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- **Bangladesh overall:** a UN global report on trafficking published in 2024 counted 452 women (228 of them trafficked abroad) and 63 girls trafficked from Bangladesh from April 2023 to March 2024, more than in earlier years <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-6"></a>

#### 4.9.6 The law

Taking someone across the border by deceit for sexual exploitation is trafficking under the 2026 Act (see [File a trafficking case](#file-a-trafficking-case)) <sup>[126](#o126)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-5"></a>

#### 4.9.7 Where to get help

- **BNWLA** rehabilitated the Jashore woman rescued from an Indian brothel <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- **Justice and Care** was handling the return of at least 10 women and girls rescued from Indian brothels in February 2025 <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.

<a id="cambodia-myanmar-and-laos-scam-compounds"></a>

### 4.10 Cambodia, Myanmar and Laos: scam compounds

<a id="how-it-is-sold-8"></a>

#### 4.10.1 How it is sold

The job is "computer operator", "data entry", "call centre" or "restaurant" work, for about Tk 5.3 to 6 lakh <sup>[N363](#n363), [N405](#n405), [N165](#n165), [N452](#n452)</sup>. A data-entry job was promised at USD 800 a month <sup>[N165](#n165)</sup>. CID says traffickers advertise these jobs through fake websites, email, Facebook, WhatsApp and Telegram <sup>[N453](#n453)</sup>. Bangladeshis already working in the Gulf, including on a ship in Dubai, are recruited too with promises of higher pay <sup>[N454](#n454), [N453](#n453)</sup>. Many went with BMET clearance <sup>[N362](#n362), [N363](#n363)</sup>. A BRAC study found 66% of cyber-scam victims were recruited through middlemen and 98% never got the promised job <sup>[N27](#n27)</sup>. Of 473 people back from Cambodian scam centres, 80% had gone through a recruiting agency in the regular process, the Bangladesh representative of Winrock International, a development organisation, said in July 2026 <sup>[N234](#n234)</sup>.

People in the sector say Cambodia's economy is no better than Bangladesh's and it has few jobs, yet workers are sent there for the scam centres <sup>[N18](#n18), [N455](#n455)</sup>. With the Middle East market shrinking, people who are a little better off have been going to countries such as Russia and Cambodia, where many are cheated, Prothom Alo reported in October 2026 <sup>[N455](#n455)</sup>. Volunteers of migration organisations said in November 2026 that people are also held in scam compounds in Vietnam <sup>[N182](#n182)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-6"></a>

#### 4.10.2 The path

- **Cambodia:** the worker flies in on a one-month visit visa, not a work visa; Bangladeshi brokers meet him at the airport, keep him briefly in a hotel and sell him to a guarded compound, often Chinese-run <sup>[N456](#n456), [N200](#n200), [N236](#n236)</sup>. Compounds are in Sihanoukville, along the Thai and Lao borders, and in rural areas <sup>[N457](#n457)</sup>. Many of about 16,000 who went in 18 months were shown an employment visa before leaving and later given a tourist visa and sold, although they had followed the government's rules, according to the president of Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation, an organisation led by trafficking survivors <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. One returnee was kept three days in a Phnom Penh restaurant, then taken late at night to a scam centre for an "interview"; the people who sent him said this was the work and he would later be sent on to Europe <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.
- **Myanmar and Laos:** victims are taken across the Thai border into Myanmar, Laos or Cambodia; Myawaddy on the Thai-Myanmar border is a hub <sup>[N458](#n458), [N459](#n459)</sup>. Eight Bangladeshis who went to Cambodia and Laos for work in 2025 worked there for a time, then were taken by road through Thailand and sold to a scam centre in Myanmar, according to Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation <sup>[N460](#n460)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-7"></a>

#### 4.10.3 What happens

- **Sold:** men are sold to compounds for USD 1,500 to 10,000; one man was sold to three compounds in 2022 <sup>[N200](#n200), [N461](#n461), [N458](#n458)</sup>.
- **Forced to scam:** they are held at gunpoint and forced to take part in the scams <sup>[N453](#n453)</sup>. Those who miss daily targets are beaten or given electric shocks <sup>[N351](#n351)</sup>.
- **Ransom:** a compound demanded USD 5,000 to release two youths; another family was pushed to pay Tk 95,000 more <sup>[N461](#n461), [N462](#n462)</sup>.
- **Abandoned:** a man who escaped after three months paid his own ticket and hotel home <sup>[N452](#n452)</sup>.
- **Detained leaving:** on 3 September 2026 Cambodia's immigration police detained 32 Bangladeshis at Phnom Penh airport as they tried to fly home, and held 30 of them in an immigration detention centre <sup>[N463](#n463)</sup>. They had gone under the return process set by the Cambodian government but were held for illegal stay <sup>[N463](#n463)</sup>. They had been taken there by a broker ring with the lure of a good salary, many were forced into cyber scamming, and those who tried to escape were allegedly tortured <sup>[N463](#n463)</sup>. They landed in Dhaka on 20 October 2026 on Thai Airways TG-321, after being held since 3 September <sup>[N464](#n464)</sup>.
- **Escaping into Thailand:** eight Bangladeshis who escaped a Myanmar compound into Thailand had no visa, surrendered to the immigration police and were kept in a safe home on a charge of illegal entry <sup>[N460](#n460)</sup>.
- **Victims turned recruiters:** many who were first victims later join the same ring and traffic and cheat others, a CID additional inspector general said in July 2026 <sup>[N234](#n234)</sup>.

<a id="cases-3"></a>

#### 4.10.4 Cases

- Told July 2026: Mannan Soyal, back from three years in Saudi Arabia, was sent to Cambodia by a relative from his area for BDT 5 lakh on the promise of a restaurant job, and left on 5 December 2025 after training <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. A few days after arriving he was taken to a Chinese-run place with thousands of people at computers and told to call himself "Maria", build relationships with people on Instagram and get money from them <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. When he objected, his passport was already with them, and he was told to pay the money back if he wanted it <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. He was beaten and locked in a small room for missing his target, and later freed in a police raid <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- Told Aug 2026: Tofayel Ahmed came to Dhaka to go to Saudi Arabia, but at a friend's urging went to Cambodia through two brokers, travelling with a BMET smart card <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>. He was held about one month and 27 days before he escaped; he was never given the one-year visa and work permit, and became illegal when his one-month single-entry visa ran out <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.
- Reported Oct 2026: two young men had about BDT 6 lakh each taken, their passports taken on arrival, and were sold to a scam centre for USD 5,000 <sup>[N455](#n455)</sup>. The CID said they were tortured if they refused the fraud work or made even a small mistake, and had no way to leave <sup>[N455](#n455)</sup>. Their families paid a further USD 5,000 ransom, the traffickers kept control of them after release, and money was collected once more for the tickets home <sup>[N455](#n455)</sup>. A case was filed at Paltan police station on 26 October 2026 naming Md Shaon Molla (23) and Md Sakib Sarkar (26) of Pubail, Gazipur, and the CID arrested both the next day <sup>[N455](#n455)</sup>.

<a id="the-numbers-8"></a>

#### 4.10.5 The numbers

- **Clearances:** BMET cleared 15,921 workers for Cambodia in 18 months, and BRAC says more than 1,000 of them were trapped in compounds <sup>[N465](#n465)</sup>; Prothom Alo reported more than 15,000 cleared in the year and a half to July 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. In 2025 alone 12,263 took clearance for Cambodia, the eighth-largest destination that year, against 4,727 for Russia <sup>[N18](#n18), [N455](#n455)</sup>.
- **Returns:** 583 trafficking victims came home from Cambodia in June 2026, including 221 in four days, and another 109 on 1 July <sup>[N363](#n363), [N405](#n405)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported 646 back in June after Cambodian raids <sup>[N18](#n18), [N455](#n455)</sup>. About 1,800 had come home as victims of cyber fraud in 2026 by June, according to BRAC <sup>[N234](#n234), [N190](#n190)</sup>. By April 2026 about 50 people rescued from Myawaddy, Myanmar, had been repatriated through the Embassy in Bangkok <sup>[N459](#n459)</sup>.
- **What returnees went through:** BRAC found 88% of returnees from Cambodia were held captive with no freedom of movement, 38% suffered serious mental trauma and 27% physical torture <sup>[N24](#n24), [N190](#n190)</sup>. One report of BRAC's figures gives 86% who had their passports and papers taken, another 87% who had forged papers <sup>[N24](#n24), [N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Laos:** 7,532 Bangladeshis went to Laos in the 21 months before October 2026 <sup>[N466](#n466)</sup>. About 3,000 took clearance for Laos in the first eight months of 2026 (13th destination), against 3,835 in 2025 (16th), mainly for construction and manufacturing, BMET sources said <sup>[N467](#n467)</sup>.
- **Region:** IOM helped more than 3,500 victims of forced criminality in Southeast Asia in 2022 to 2025, Bangladesh among the largest origin countries <sup>[N468](#n468)</sup>.
- **Gaps in the data:** Bangladesh has no reliable national data on how many people are trafficked to scam centres, come home or get justice, according to UNODC (the UN Office on Drugs and Crime) in July 2026 <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How many people came home from Cambodia in June 2026: 583 or 646?
> - In BRAC's returnee survey, is the 86-87% figure for people whose passports were taken, or for people carrying forged papers?

<a id="the-law-7"></a>

#### 4.10.6 The law

- **Bangladesh:** the 2026 Act counts forcing a person into crimes such as online scamming as exploitation under the trafficking offence <sup>[165](#o165), [N469](#n469)</sup>. Since July 2026 workers for Cambodia, Laos and Brazil need a proper work visa and full checks before BMET clearance <sup>[N363](#n363)</sup>. Most published lists of the 12 countries needing Ministry approval since 4 November 2026 include Cambodia (see [The Balkans and Eastern Europe](#the-balkans-and-eastern-europe)) <sup>[N13](#n13), [N401](#n401), [N27](#n27)</sup>. A Home Ministry joint secretary said in July 2026 that cooperation with Cambodia and others against scam centres has been strengthened, and that quick legal action is taken against traffickers even if they are government officials <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Cambodia:** running a scam centre with violence or detention carries 10 to 20 years in prison <sup>[N470](#n470)</sup>. The 2026 US Trafficking in Persons report says some senior Cambodian officials and police profit from and protect scam centres <sup>[N471](#n471)</sup>. Cambodian law enforcement shut scam centres under international pressure in mid-2026, after which 583 workers came home in June <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
- **Laos:** recruitment, work permits and entry visas for Bangladeshi workers are suspended from 1 September to 31 December 2026 <sup>[N472](#n472)</sup>. The Ministry of Labour and Social Welfare's directive of 9 September 2026 cites illegal entry, trafficking, unauthorised labour suppliers and employers who evade responsibility, without saying why Bangladeshis were singled out <sup>[N467](#n467)</sup>. No person, company, employer or labour supplier may on its own let Bangladeshis enter or work in Laos, and the LA-B2 labour visa stays closed to them until the end of 2026, except for large government-approved priority projects <sup>[N467](#n467)</sup>. A worker found working outside his approved workplace is fined 1 million kip (USD 44) each time and sent home within 30 days at the cost of the employer or whoever broke the rule; employers pay 2.5 million kip (USD 110) per worker for hiring without approval, tripled for a repeat <sup>[N467](#n467)</sup>. Asked in September 2026, the Expatriates' Welfare Minister said nothing was officially known and the matter would be looked into <sup>[N467](#n467)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-6"></a>

#### 4.10.7 Where to get help

- **Bangladesh Embassy, Bangkok** (covers Cambodia; there is no embassy in Phnom Penh): 47/8 Ekamai Soi 30, Sukhumvit 63, Bangkok 10110; +66 (0)2 390 5107 to 8; welfare hotline +66-818-70-84-43 <sup>[187](#o187), [N473](#n473)</sup>. Tofayel Ahmed applied to return through the embassy on Facebook after police raids began and came home on 14 June once it was approved <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.
- **Cambodian police anti-trafficking hotline:** 1288 <sup>[188 (2022)](#o188)</sup>.
- **BRAC:** families who contacted BRAC got 18 men out of a Myanmar compound in March 2025, with the Foreign Ministry, the CID and the Embassy in Thailand <sup>[N453](#n453)</sup>. Mannan Soyal had no passport, and BRAC arranged a travel pass and a ticket home <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. Two young men stuck in a Cambodian hotel for about two months after release reached the BRAC Migration Centre online and came home on 3 September 2026 <sup>[N455](#n455)</sup>.
- **Through the Wage Earners' Welfare Board:** after an application from Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation, the Board's expatriate welfare branch wrote to the labour wing of the Bangkok embassy asking it to free the 30 detained in Cambodia and send them home quickly <sup>[N463](#n463)</sup>. For the eight held in a Mae Sot immigration safe home in Thailand, the Foundation applied to the Board on 24 August 2026 on complaints from victims and families, and the Board's director instructed the embassy's First Secretary (Labour) to free them and send them home <sup>[N460](#n460)</sup>. Three were due home on 22 October 2026, while the other five were still in the legal process <sup>[N460](#n460)</sup>.
- **After return:** returnees need to be treated as survivors, not criminals, with regular follow-up, rehabilitation and mental health care so they are not drawn back into the rings, the Foundation's president said in July 2026 <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

<a id="kyrgyzstan-and-central-asia-as-a-stepping-stone"></a>

### 4.11 Kyrgyzstan and Central Asia as a stepping stone

<a id="how-it-is-sold-9"></a>

#### 4.11.1 How it is sold

The offer is Europe: a Shariatpur man paid Tk 4.8 lakh in 2025 and a Sylhet man Tk 6 lakh in 2023 to be taken to Italy or Poland <sup>[N162](#n162), [N474 (2023)](#n474)</sup>. CID says Kyrgyzstan became a trafficking route because it is seen as close to Europe <sup>[N409 (2024)](#n409)</sup>. Others are sold factory jobs and sent on 59-day tourist visas <sup>[N409 (2024)](#n409)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-7"></a>

#### 4.11.2 The path

1. <a id="the-path-7-item-1"></a>**Dhaka to Dubai** on a visit visa <sup>[N409 (2024)](#n409)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-7-item-2"></a>**Dubai to Bishkek**, Kyrgyzstan's capital, on a tourist visa <sup>[N409 (2024)](#n409), [N162](#n162)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-7-item-3"></a>**The promised next step** (a Ukraine visa, or Italy "after some work") never comes; the Sylhet broker left for Dubai and disappeared <sup>[N225 (2023)](#n225)</sup>.
4. <a id="the-path-7-item-4"></a>**Overland**, some cross into Kazakhstan away from checkpoints to look for work, and have been caught as far as the Russian border <sup>[N475 (2024)](#n475)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-8"></a>

#### 4.11.3 What happens
