- **Abandoned:** about 40 Bangladeshis were found abandoned at one Bishkek address in 2024, and had to go to Uzbekistan and back to get visas <sup>[N476 (2024)](#n476)</sup>.
- **Prison and bans:** Bangladeshis caught crossing Kazakhstan to the Russian border in 2024 got six months in prison, expulsion and a five-year ban <sup>[N475 (2024)](#n475)</sup>.
- **Fines and deportation:** breaking the purpose of entry, such as working on a tourist visa, is fined 10,000 som (Kyrgyz currency) in Kyrgyzstan, and in September 2025, 180 Bangladeshis were sent back <sup>[N477](#n477), [N478](#n478)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-8"></a>

#### 4.11.4 The law

- **Kyrgyzstan:** a foreigner who works without a permit is to be expelled <sup>[N479](#n479)</sup>. Its security chief says foreigners come as tourists and students and work illegally <sup>[N480](#n480)</sup>.
- **Kazakhstan:** prosecutors detained three organisers who smuggled 20 South Asians from Kyrgyzstan in November 2023 <sup>[N481 (2023)](#n481)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-7"></a>

#### 4.11.5 Where to get help

Bangladesh has no embassy in Kyrgyzstan; the Embassy in Tashkent, Uzbekistan, covers it, with a 24-hour emergency number +998930009780 <sup>[N482 (2024)](#n482), [N483 (2024)](#n483)</sup>. BRAC asks stranded migrants to contact its Migration Welfare Centre <sup>[N484](#n484)</sup>.

<a id="russia-recruited-into-the-war"></a>

### 4.12 Russia: recruited into the war

<a id="how-it-is-sold-10"></a>

#### 4.12.1 How it is sold

**The jobs offered:**

- construction or welding, through a recruiting agency, at about 75,000 roubles a month <sup>[N485](#n485), [N410](#n410)</sup>;
- high-paid work in oil, construction or logistics, according to "Promises Written in Blood", a November 2025 report by BRAC's Migration Programme based on survivors' accounts, family testimony and document checks <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>;
- work as a cook or cleaner, according to an Associated Press (AP) investigation summarised in a Prothom Alo editorial of 4 February 2026 <sup>[N486](#n486)</sup>; Jahangir Alam of Lakshmipur was promised work as a cook in a cantonment in 2025 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>;
- security guard, driver, mechanic, electrician, construction worker, welder, repair or transport worker or technician, and sometimes IT, medicine, sport or agriculture, offered through social media adverts or recruiting agents and firms, according to the UN's Independent International Commission of Inquiry on Ukraine; some recruiters said the work was under the military but civilian <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. The commission describes a professional athlete told he could sign a contract after arriving if he liked the work, who was handed to the military on arrival, and a cook who did not want to be a soldier but ended up on the front line <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

**The promises:** the UN commission says the offers spoke of high pay, Russian citizenship, comfortable housing, a pension after leaving the job and other benefits, and some recruits were shown photos or a catalogue of the housing <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. Survivors say brokers repeatedly promised it was not military work <sup>[N410](#n410)</sup>. The commission describes a group of 30 given a letter of assurance that they would not be sent to the battlefield; by the time of its report 17 had been killed on the front line, 5 were missing and 1 had returned wounded; the report does not name the group's country <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. One family was offered medical school in Moscow for their son if he first served four months in the war <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>.

**On Facebook:** Shariful Hasan of BRAC said in November 2025 that a Bangladeshi using the name MD Sohel was campaigning on Facebook about the benefits of joining the Russian army and fighting, and that many people were being newly lured to Russia to fight because of it <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>.

**Going to fight on purpose:** Prothom Alo reported in October 2026 that travel to Russia had not decreased, and that because six months at the war brings a chance of Russian citizenship and pay of Tk 3 to 4 lakh a month, many go to the war of their own accord <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Bangladesh's ambassador to Russia, Md Nazrul Islam, said in October 2026 that more than 90% of the workers go to the war of their own accord, and that Russia says the latest 30 also went voluntarily and holds written proof of it <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.

**The cost:** families paid about Tk 9.5 to 10 lakh, about USD 8,000 <sup>[N410](#n410), [N485](#n485), [N352](#n352)</sup>. BRAC's November 2025 report puts it at Tk 7 to 8 lakh <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. The UN commission found that some recruits paid in advance and others were told the money would be deducted from their pay later <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

<a id="who-sends-them"></a>

#### 4.12.2 Who sends them

- **Agencies in Bangladesh:** a Prothom Alo investigation found that clearances for Russia were taken in the names of 93 recruiting agencies over four years, that only 17 took clearance for more than a hundred workers each, and that Bonna Bijoy Overseas alone took the most, about 2,500 workers or 27% <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in October 2026 that Bonna Bijoy Overseas is accused of trafficking in the name of sending workers to Algeria, has a human trafficking case against it, faces allegations over Russia too, and has had its server locked so it can take no new clearances; its managing director, Barun Debnath, did not answer the paper's call or text message <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. BOESL, the government's recruiting agency, took the second-highest number of clearances, 771, and a responsible BOESL official told Prothom Alo that one of its workers became involved in the war (see When the visa, the agency and the clearance are all real) <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Shariful Hasan, head of BRAC's Migration Programme, said in October 2026 that the agencies accused of trafficking are sending the most workers to Russia <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **The ring:** the February 2026 Prothom Alo editorial, citing the AP investigation, said a Bangladeshi living in Moscow is the trafficking ring's main organiser, that investigators suspect it has links with the Russian government, and named "SP Global" as one of the firms that ran such trafficking openly <sup>[N486](#n486)</sup>. BRAC's report says international facilitators as well as local brokers are involved <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>.
- **A Banani travel agency and a ringleader:** the CID (the Criminal Investigation Department of the police) arrested a woman named in its statements both as Fabiha Jerin and as Tamanna, a partner in Dream Home Travels and Tours Ltd of Banani, Dhaka, at Dhaka airport on 5 February 2025 as she was about to flee to Nepal, in a case filed at Banani police station by the wife of a wounded victim; the CID said she confessed and that her brother Tuhin, in Russia, had encouraged people to go to the war <sup>[N487](#n487), [N488](#n488)</sup>. In June 2025 the CID arrested the alleged ringleader, Muhammad Alamgir Hossain (40) of Lohagara, Chattogram, at Chattogram's Shah Amanat airport; it said Alamgir, who went to Russia on a student visa in 2008 and became a citizen by marriage, had taken 50 people to Russia on three visa categories <sup>[N488](#n488)</sup>.
- **Agents abroad:** the UN commission says recruiting agents in the various countries were local, regional or Russian nationals operating as travel or tourism businesses; some held local licences, some presented themselves as legal or government-approved, and in many cases visas issued by the Russian embassy were handed over through them <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

<a id="the-path-8"></a>

#### 4.12.3 The path

1. <a id="the-path-8-item-1"></a>**A fast departure.** The UN commission found many recruits were pressed to travel within a few days of accepting, with agents arranging the visa, air ticket, housing and the rest of the journey; visas were business visas, short-term tourist visas or none, varying by country <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. It obtained copies of business visas of recruited Bangladeshis that mention an invitation from a Russian civilian company <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-path-8-item-2"></a>**Dhaka to Dubai, then Moscow**, or through other third countries <sup>[N410](#n410)</sup>. Tasneem Siddiqui, executive director of RMMRU (the Refugee and Migratory Movements Research Unit), said in November 2025 that many are first taken to Saudi Arabia on tourist or Umrah visas and later made to sign a contract and forced to the battlefield <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. The CID said in June 2025 that a ring lured 10 people with jobs as cleaners or cooks in a chocolate factory in Russia at Tk 2-2.5 lakh a month, sent them first to Saudi Arabia on Umrah visas, had them perform Umrah, then took them to Russia and sold them to a man called Sultan, who handed them to the Russian army <sup>[N488](#n488)</sup>. Testimony and documents the UN commission obtained name Bahrain, Brazil, Ethiopia, France, Kenya, Mexico, Morocco, Oman, Panama, Spain, Turkey and the UAE as transit countries; agents sometimes travelled with the recruits or met them there, and some recruits were told to go by road to a neighbouring country and fly from there so as not to be caught at the airport <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-path-8-item-3"></a>**At Moscow airport**, brokers take more cash, in one case about USD 1,000 per person <sup>[N410](#n410)</sup>. The UN commission found many recruits had no freedom of movement from the airport on: they were kept in temporary accommodation and handed to the armed forces <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.
4. <a id="the-path-8-item-4"></a>**Passports and phones taken**, and the men are handed to the military <sup>[N352](#n352), [N360](#n360)</sup>.
5. <a id="the-path-8-item-5"></a>**Russian-language contracts** they can't read <sup>[N410](#n410)</sup>. According to the UN commission, within hours or days of arriving recruits were made to sign a one-year military service contract with Russia's Defence Ministry, and could not use translation because their phones were not in their hands <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. Those who refused were pressured, threatened or tortured; one recruit said four people were shot dead in front of him and he was told he would be next, after which he signed <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

Some go legally on six-month work visas; when the visa ends they are offered a military contract to renew it <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>. Workers laid off from Russian jobs have also been recruited there (see [Recruited while abroad or just back](#recruited-while-abroad-or-just-back)) <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-9"></a>

#### 4.12.4 What happens

- **Training:** the UN commission found initial training lasted from 20 minutes to a few days, in some cases up to a month <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. Jahangir Alam of Lakshmipur said he was handed a weapon without training and then taught to use it for one day, that 16 men were together in his war camp, and that he lived in fear after four of them died <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. Khadija Akter said in February 2025 that her husband, Jafar Sardar of Jashore, was kept in a barracks, given an army haircut and war training, and that his last message said "bring me home at any cost" <sup>[N266](#n266)</sup>.
- **Work at the front:** the UN commission says recruits were made to dig trenches, guard areas, deliver food and water and collect bodies, their main tasks being to carry out assaults and advance <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. In the AP investigation, returned workers said they were made to collect bodies and move the wounded, those who refused were tortured, and a Russian commander told one victim he had in fact been "sold" <sup>[N486](#n486)</sup>. The Special Response Battalion (SRB), a police unit, the detective branch and others told Prothom Alo that the men sent from Bangladesh have neither the physical fitness, the training nor the tactics for war, and that when the enemy attacks them with drones Russian soldiers identify the enemy's position <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **Death:** 30 men flew out on 7 May 2026 <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>. In October 2026 the commander of the police's Special Response Battalion said 17 who arrived that May were killed on their first day of deployment after minimal training, 20 Bangladeshis were confirmed dead, and they had been used as bait to locate enemy positions <sup>[N352](#n352)</sup>. On 9 July 2026 the Minister had told Parliament that four of the 30 had died <sup>[N351](#n351)</sup>. A joint investigation by Fortify Rights and Truth Hounds, two rights groups, counted at least 104 Bangladeshis recruited and at least 34 killed <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>. The news agency Agence France-Presse (AFP) reported that Mohammad Yasin Sheikh, 22, was killed on 27 March 2025 fighting for Russian forces <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. According to Jafar Sardar's family, the broker took 10 Bangladeshis to Russia together; one, Akram of Narsingdi, escaped and came home, and the other nine were forced to the battlefield, where one was killed and one was shot <sup>[N266](#n266)</sup>.
- **Abuse:** survivors were sent to fight with little or no training, beaten, unpaid, kept from leaving and threatened when they tried to escape <sup>[N410](#n410)</sup>. Recruits told the UN commission of days without food and water, beatings, detention and death threats <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.
- **Pay:** the UN commission found recruits were taken to open bank accounts, but many were not paid and some got little; money was withdrawn from some accounts without permission, some salaries were withheld or accounts closed, and in some cases recruiting agents or military personnel took the bank cards <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.
- **Missing:** many recruits lost contact with their families after going to the war, the UN commission found <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. BRAC's November 2025 report records that Ayon Mondal of Bagerhat, who went on a valid visa, said in his last message that he was being taken towards the Ukrainian border and has not been traced since, and that Amit Barua of Cumilla has been missing since a photo showed him in Russian military uniform <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. Adnan Hossain, a wounded returnee, said Russia does not publish information on Bangladeshis' deaths and that anyone who does not return from the front line is declared missing <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **Bodies not returned:** many families still cannot get their sons' bodies home <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>. Adnan Hossain said Russia does not bring back the bodies, and those Prothom Alo spoke to said the bodies of foreigners killed are left on the field <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. The ambassador said the bodies cannot be sent home because the Russian authorities are not handing them over <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.

<a id="the-group-of-30"></a>

#### 4.12.5 The group of 30

The 30 men who flew from Dhaka on 7 May 2026 went through Jabale Noor International of Khilkhet and RS International of Ashulia, through brokers, according to 28 letters their families submitted to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry in July 2026; the letters say the men were sold to the Russian army with the lure of high pay (see When the visa, the agency and the clearance are all real) <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Prothom Alo said nearly all the families are low-income <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.

**What they paid:**

- Rajon Hossain (21) of Jhenaidah paid a broker Tk 10 lakh for a high-paid job <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- Pabitra Chandra (30), a carpenter from a very poor family in Gopalpur, Tangail, went with Tk 1.3 lakh from selling his wife's jewellery and Tk 9.5 lakh borrowed from an NGO and local moneylenders <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.
- Robin Mir, an autorickshaw driver from Siddhirganj, Narayanganj, sold his village land and cattle and borrowed, and went with three other men from Narayanganj <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.
- Md Palash Sheikh's family spent Tk 10 lakh <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.

**What happened, in order:**

1. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-1"></a>**The first days.** Robin Mir's nephew, Rahat Chowdhury, said his uncle was given good food and shown around for three or four days, and then there was no news for five or six days <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. Pabitra Chandra told his brother-in-law, Tapan Chandra Sutradhar, that he and several others were kept in a hotel and would be given work once the papers were sorted out; contact then stopped for some time <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. Rajon Hossain got no job and, after four days in a hotel, was sent to an army camp after his signature was taken on a paper <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
2. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-2"></a>**15 to 18 May.** On 15 May Robin Mir said they were being taken to work, and on 17 May he was seen in an army camp in a video <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. On 18 May a worker's family phoned the Bangladesh Embassy in Russia to say that 30 people who had come through Jabale Noor and RS International were being sent to the battlefield; according to the embassy's 25 May letter, it was writing to Russia's Foreign Ministry asking that the workers be returned to work under their appointment letters instead <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
3. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-3"></a>**Training.** After 21 days of training the men were split into groups of 16, 8 and 6 and sent to the war <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
4. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-4"></a>**29 May.** Pabitra Chandra phoned to say "we are being taken to the war by force; the chance of dying there is 95 per cent" <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>, and afterwards asked the Bangladesh embassy to arrange for him and the others to be brought home <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. Robin Mir phoned in tears to say they had been taken through deep forest on the promise of work and sold <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.
5. <a id="the-group-of-30-item-5"></a>**June.** Two wounded returnees and six families said the men pleaded with their families before being sent to war, that the agency promised to take responsibility when the families contacted it, and that from the first week of June the men began to go missing; the families got news of deaths but no proof <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Many died around 12 and 13 June <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. At its 14 October 2026 press conference the SRB said 17 of the 30 died in a drone attack on their first day on the battlefield and three more died later <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. Rajon Hossain said he was sent to the front line with five other Bangladeshis, four were killed in a drone attack, and he was wounded <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Abdul Majid of Lalmonirhat said his son Abdullah Al Mamun phoned his younger brother the day before being sent to fight and was not heard from again; a man who went with him, Al Amin, later said Mamun had died <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.

**Where they were in October 2026:** Prothom Alo reported 20 dead, two wounded and in Russian hospitals (Palash Sheikh and Ali Hasan Sohel), two back in Bangladesh (Rajon Hossain and Adnan Hossain), and six still being made to fight <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.

- **The dead,** according to the families: Pabitra Chandra, Robin Mir, Sohel, Md Mehedi Hasan, Md Al Mamun, Md Abdullah Al Mamun, Md Nazmul Alam, Md Wasim Akram, Md Arif Hossain, Md Sayed Molla, Tarikul Islam Mintu, Sabbir Hossain, Mofiz, Md Arman Ali, Md Nazrul Islam, Md Aminur Islam, Kazi Rezaul Karim, Main Uddin, Ripon Hossain and Arif Hossain Patwari <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. Robin Mir's family learned of his death through the three Narayanganj men he went with <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. Pabitra Chandra's family heard of his death through his companions and, by 21 October 2026, had not had it confirmed by the embassy or any government channel <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. In Abdullah Al Mamun's village a funeral in absentia was arranged, but because his death was not confirmed the people who came prayed instead of performing the funeral prayer; five months had passed by October 2026 <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **The six still fighting:** Roni Sheikh and Sourav Molla of Gopalganj, Al Amin Islam of Lalmonirhat, Kamal Hossain and Helal Sheikh of Madaripur and Abu Hanif of Kishoreganj, some of whom keep in contact with their families <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. Sourav's brother-in-law, Taslim Sardar, who talks to him over the messaging app imo, said Sourav was three to four kilometres behind the front line being made to dig earth for bunkers and had twice tried to escape without success <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>. He said Kamal was digging bunkers with Sourav, Roni Sheikh and Al Amin were carrying food, water and weapons on foot from the camp to the front line, and Hanif and Helal were made sometimes to dig bunkers and sometimes to carry food <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.
- **The wounded:** Md Palash Sheikh was wounded in his first spell at the war, sent back after recovering, and lost a hand; in October 2026 he was in a Moscow hospital, and his cousin Abu Saleh Islam said asking the embassy and the Ministry had brought no help, that he cannot escape because the hospital is under army guard, and that the family has received no money <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **The debts:** Tapan Chandra Sutradhar said in October 2026 that lenders stand behind Pabitra Chandra's family for their money when they sit down to eat, and that the family is thinking of selling its remaining 3 decimals of land, which would still not clear the debt <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Is the "Sohel" the families list among the dead the same man as Ali Hasan Sohel of Goalanda, whom Prothom Alo lists as wounded and in a Russian hospital <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>?
> - Was the group held in the hotel for three days, as the families' complaint says <sup>[N257](#n257)</sup>, or four, as Rajon Hossain says <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>?

<a id="the-numbers-9"></a>

#### 4.12.6 The numbers

- **Clearances:** BMET gave 7,449 clearances for Russia between 1 January 2025 and 27 October 2026 <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>. BMET data show 37 people took clearance for Russia in 2023, 1,395 in 2024, 4,725 in 2025 and 3,105 in 2026 up to 15 October <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. According to BMET, 4,727 Bangladeshis went to Russia in 2025, which made it the 13th largest market for Bangladeshi workers that year, ahead of Malaysia, South Korea and Lebanon <sup>[N18](#n18), [N455](#n455)</sup>. RMMRU says 4,727 went in 2025 against 993 the year before, and that 2,125 took clearance for Russia in the first six months of 2026 <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
- **Families asking for help:** the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow told AFP in April 2025 that about a dozen families had contacted it to get their sons back, all alleging the sons were deceived into the Russian army <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. By November 2025, 10 families had asked BRAC to bring relatives back from Russia, men who had gone on valid visas to work; BRAC's report says there are more, but the number could not be confirmed <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>.
- **Deaths:** a Prothom Alo editorial of 4 February 2026 said at least 40 Bangladeshis were believed to have died in the previous few months after being deceived by recruiting agencies into the war <sup>[N486](#n486)</sup>. Foreign Ministry sources said in October 2026 that over four to five months the Russian government had told the embassy of 40 Bangladeshis missing or killed, without separating the two, and had said nothing yet about the latest 20 deaths <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Prothom Alo had news of at least 54 Bangladeshi deaths in the war over the three years to October 2026 <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Shariful Hasan of BRAC said in October 2026 that nobody has a count of how many found work, where the rest are or how many have died <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **Worldwide:** a UN commission found men from 29 countries recruited into Russia's forces through deception and false job offers <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>. Its draft report, published on the website of the UN human rights office (OHCHR) on 26 October 2026, examined recruitment of nationals of 48 countries and found evidence of deception or coercion for 29, Bangladesh among them, but gives no separate figures, names or outcomes for Bangladeshis <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. Its findings rest on 245 testimonies from recruits detained by Ukraine or back home, their families, lawyers, local authorities, researchers and journalists, plus documents such as invitation letters, visas, air tickets and military contracts; the victims were all men aged 18 to 60, mostly from financially weak and marginal groups <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - BMET's yearly figures (4,725 clearances in 2025 and 3,105 in 2026 to 15 October) add up to more than the 7,449 clearances given for 1 January 2025 to 27 October 2026 <sup>[N260](#n260), [N485](#n485)</sup>: which count is right?
> - For 2024, RMMRU gives 993 Bangladeshis going to Russia and BMET 1,395 clearances <sup>[N24](#n24), [N260](#n260)</sup>: does one count departures and the other clearances?

<a id="the-law-9"></a>

#### 4.12.7 The law

- **Bangladesh:** at a Dhaka briefing on 9 February 2025 the Foreign Ministry spokesperson, Mohammad Rafiqul Alam, said some Bangladeshis had been forced into the war after falling into traffickers' hands, that the Embassy in Moscow had been asked for details, that the relevant ministries had been asked to act against the recruiting and travel agencies involved, and that one member of a ring trafficking people to Russia had been arrested <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. He said the airports had been asked to take special caution over Bangladeshis going to Russia on travel visas and those with a valid Russian travel visa in their passports <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. In June 2026 the government cancelled the licences of three agencies accused of sending the 30 men, and forfeited their deposits <sup>[N490](#n490)</sup>; on 9 July 2026 the Expatriates' Welfare Minister told Parliament the three licences had been suspended <sup>[N351](#n351)</sup>, and later that month told Prothom Alo they had been cancelled <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. In October 2026 police arrested two men accused of the trafficking <sup>[N352](#n352)</sup>. The SRB said Mizanur Rahman, founder of Jabale Noor International and described as one of the ring's leaders, and Abu Taher alias Lentu were arrested on 13 October 2026 over the sending of the 30 men <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Sources in the police's Criminal Investigation Department (CID) said in October 2026 that at least six cases had been filed over trafficking to Russia <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Most published lists of the 12 countries needing Ministry approval since 4 November 2026 include Russia <sup>[N13](#n13), [N400](#n400), [N27](#n27)</sup>.
- **What the Minister says is being done:** Expatriates' Welfare Minister Ariful Haque Chowdhury said in October 2026 that nobody is given clearance without verification and attestation of confirmed job information, that all embassies have been told to keep watch through the expatriate community, that extra caution is being taken over Russia with appointment letters checked much more thoroughly, and that action has begun against the agencies involved <sup>[N260](#n260), [N285](#n285)</sup>. Selim Reza, coordinator of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies, said in October 2026 that the government went on giving clearance for Russia even after learning men were being taken to the war by force, and that demand in Russia's labour market had not been properly assessed <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.
- **International:** the UN commission concluded that the Russian authorities committed human trafficking together with recruiting agents; that trafficked men, having become members of the Russian armed forces by signing the contract, cannot be called mercenaries; and that if they surrender or are captured they are entitled to prisoner-of-war protection until the war ends <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>. It recommended that Russia stop recruiting foreigners through deception or coercion, discharge and assist the trafficked men in its forces, and prosecute those involved; according to international media citing Russia's UN mission, Russia does not recognise the commission's mandate and says the report rests on false information <sup>[N285](#n285)</sup>.

<a id="bodies-and-compensation"></a>

#### 4.12.8 Bodies and compensation

- **From Russia:** Foreign Ministry sources said in October 2026 that compensation can be claimed if someone dies in the war or is missing for more than six months, but that it means a complicated process, including establishing who the heir is and the heir going to Russia and staying at least six months, and that the bodies cannot be recovered <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.
- **From Bangladesh:** the Wage Earners' Welfare Board says it pays to bring home the body of a worker who went with BMET clearance or registered as a member with the embassy abroad, on the family's application, and that a family with neither can still apply, in which case the Board sends a proposal to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry, which pays from a yearly allocation <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. It gives Tk 35,000 in cash for the burial of each body brought home and then a Tk 3 lakh grant, but by October 2026 no application to bring a body home from Russia had reached it, because with no proof of death nobody can apply, and so nobody is getting any compensation (see [If a worker dies abroad](#if-a-worker-dies-abroad-what-does-the-family-get), what does the family get?) <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-8"></a>

#### 4.12.9 Where to get help

**How men have got home:**

- Afzal Hossain Meraj's father applied to the Foreign Ministry on 22 April 2025, alleging his son had been sold to the Russian army; by November 2025 the son had come home, two months earlier, with the help of the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>.
- Jahangir Alam of Lakshmipur fell ill after four men in his war camp died, spent two months in hospital, escaped from there and came home through the Bangladesh embassy, seven months after going <sup>[N18](#n18), [N24](#n24)</sup>.
- A wounded survivor got home in August 2026 with help from a doctor and the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>.
- Adnan Hossain of Narayanganj, admitted to a Moscow hospital with a splinter in his abdomen, had three operations, escaped after two months in hospital, went to the embassy and came home on 15 September 2026 on a travel pass, a valid travel permit <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Rajon Hossain was wounded, admitted to hospital and escaped from there to come home <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.

**Where families have gone:** in February 2025 relatives of nine men sent to the battlefield had formed human chains in Dhaka several times, gone more than once to the Russian embassy and to Bangladesh's Foreign Ministry, and written to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry, and on 16 February 2025 Jafar Sardar's family wrote to the Chief Adviser's office asking for his return and their money; the report of 17 February 2025 records no result <sup>[N266](#n266)</sup>. In July 2026 the families of the group of 30 sent 28 letters to the Expatriates' Welfare Ministry asking for their quick and safe return and for financial help <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>. Taslim Sardar organised a human chain in Dhaka with the affected families in October 2026 <sup>[N489](#n489)</sup>.

**BRAC's Migration Programme** has received many applications from families with almost the same account since late 2024; it submits applications to the Foreign Ministry for the men's rescue, gives case documents to the police's Criminal Investigation Department (CID), and gives families legal and psychological support <sup>[N303](#n303)</sup>. It has asked the government to trace the Bangladeshis still in Russia <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>, and in October 2026 its head, Shariful Hasan, called for a hotline and a list of the missing <sup>[N260](#n260)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What are the Bangladesh Embassy in Moscow's emergency phone and email for families of men sent to the front?

<a id="student-visas-used-as-work-routes"></a>

### 4.13 Student visas used as work routes

<a id="how-it-is-sold-11"></a>

#### 4.13.1 How it is sold

Agents tell students a visa lets them work freely, and charge families Tk 6 to 8 lakh or more, steering them to barely functioning colleges (Malaysia) <sup>[N491](#n491)</sup>. In Jordan, brokers charged students large sums for often fake admissions <sup>[N492](#n492)</sup>. A returnee from the United Kingdom told a roundtable in Sylhet in April 2026 that brokers sent him there to study saying he could work legally alongside his studies <sup>[N334](#n334)</sup>. For Australia, some intermediary agencies in Dhaka and other cities ran flashy campaigns saying "you can go on a travel visa and become a student" or "once you get in, you can enrol in some private college, renew the visa and stay", Kawsar Khan, a registered Australian migration consultant, wrote in September 2026 <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>. He wrote that many Bangladeshi families sell land or break savings to send a child to Australia in the hope that the husband or wife will work there to pay the study costs and that they will somehow become permanent <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-10"></a>

#### 4.13.2 What happens

- **Malaysia:** students have been moved after arrival from a university into a Bangladeshi-owned English language centre, whose students can't work <sup>[N493](#n493)</sup>. A Student Pass allows only part-time work in semester breaks, up to 20 hours a week, in listed jobs <sup>[189](#o189)</sup>.
- **Kyrgyzstan:** working on a study visa is working without a permit, and leads to expulsion <sup>[N479](#n479), [N480](#n480)</sup>.
- **Jordan:** since 1 April 2025 any foreign student found working is deported, and the employer is fined at least JD 800 (Jordanian dinars) per student <sup>[N494](#n494)</sup>.
- **Italy:** a student visa used only to get in is revoked if the student has not enrolled by 31 January <sup>[190](#o190)</sup>.
- **Russia:** a medical-school offer came with four months of war service attached (see [Russia: recruited into the war](#russia-recruited-into-the-war)) <sup>[N485](#n485)</sup>.
- **United Kingdom:** the same returnee said he found no legal work, had to work illegally, and was paid GBP 1 an hour where others got GBP 8 <sup>[N334](#n334)</sup>.
- **Australia:** under reforms announced by minister Tony Burke, as Kawsar Khan described them in September 2026 <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>:
  - a mandatory "No Further Stay" condition is being attached to tourist and visitor visas, so coming on a visit visa and applying inside Australia for a student or other visa is in effect over <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>;
  - "visa hopping" is being stopped: a student can move only to a higher qualification (for example bachelor's to master's), not to a lower-level vocational or diploma course to keep a visa alive <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>;
  - newly arriving students, and graduate visa holders, can no longer bring a husband, wife or children, except doctoral researchers; families already in Australia will not be separated <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>;
  - about 77,000 people who stayed on after their visas expired are being identified and made to leave quickly, with 100 new border force officers and use of Melbourne's old quarantine centre <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>;
  - Kawsar Khan advised dropping the idea, sold by agents in Dhaka, of "reaching Australia any way first and seeing later", because the days of passing years on visa changes or drawn-out appeals are over, and said those whose main aim is simply to work or to live with family would do better not to come under the current rules <sup>[N230](#n230)</sup>.
- **Cyprus:** Shahriar Ahmed Emon, 22, enrolled a few months earlier at a private university in Larnaca, told his flatmates on 12 June 2026 that he was going to his first day at a factory job in Kofinou, left home that evening and disappeared after sending a friend a location pin <sup>[N495](#n495)</sup>. According to police sources, a man he had met on a bus five days earlier, angered by a remark of his, admitted luring him with the job to a deserted area of Kofinou and stabbing him to death that evening <sup>[N495](#n495)</sup>. Unknown people then demanded ransom from his father in Greece, with social-media posts, which police were still verifying, demanding EUR 10,000 one day and EUR 25,000 the next; according to police sources, the accused admitted staging a fake kidnapping to get money from the family <sup>[N495](#n495)</sup>. The family reported the ransom demand and the location pin to the police, who arrested the suspect on 21 June 2026; he led Larnaca CID to the body <sup>[N495](#n495)</sup>.

<a id="forged-documents-and-impersonation"></a>

### 4.14 Forged documents and impersonation

<a id="how-it-is-sold-12"></a>

#### 4.14.1 How it is sold

- **Fake visas:** in February 2024 three men arrested at Dhaka airport had each paid a broker Tk 16 to 18 lakh to fly to Qatar on fake visas and then go on illegally to France, Italy and Greece, a DB (Dhaka detective police) deputy commissioner said <sup>[N496 (2024)](#n496)</sup>. In May 2026 two men agreed Tk 30 lakh each for Italy, paid Tk 20 lakh up front, and were given fake Schengen visas <sup>[N365](#n365), [N355](#n355)</sup>.
- **Swapped pages:** a "page substitution" ring copies a genuine Italian visa page into a look-alike's passport <sup>[N318](#n318)</sup>. In July 2026 the CID arrested members of a ring accused of sending a woman to Italy on another woman's visa, and seized 56 passports and 153 seals <sup>[N288](#n288)</sup>.
- **Fake certificates:** a ring sold fake police clearance certificates with counterfeit QR codes linking to a fake website <sup>[N209](#n209)</sup>. In one study, 22% of workers said agents forged their medical certificates and 16% their pre-departure orientation certificates <sup>[N252](#n252)</sup>.
- **A forged police clearance, Chattogram, 2026:** Zakir Hossain of Banshkhali, unemployed and with a criminal case against him, did not apply to the police because of the case, and went to an acquaintance at a local travel agency, according to his brother <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>. The acquaintance, Abdur Rahman, quoted Tk 1,23,000, the brother said; the family paid Tk 80,000 on 21 July 2026 and the rest on receiving the certificate on 12 August <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>. Police said making the certificate and the fake website cost the ring Tk 3,000, leaving Tk 1,20,000 profit <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>. The certificates carried police officers' signatures and seals and a QR code that showed them on a clone of the Bangladesh Police website, so, police said, they could not be told from real ones by looking <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>. The ring had layers: brokers, and people skilled at making QR codes and websites, Chattogram's Additional Superintendent of Police (Special Branch) said <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>.
- **Changed ages:** girls have been sent abroad with ages raised through altered birth certificates <sup>[N142 (2020)](#n142)</sup>. In May 2025 a dalal sent a Prothom Alo reporter posing as a customer to a computer shop in front of the Gazipur passport office, where a man quoted Tk 32,000, half in advance, to raise the age by one year on both the voter ID card and the passport, delivered within a month or within 15 days for Tk 2,000 more; correcting an NID properly takes several months, the report says <sup>[N87](#n87)</sup>. The Department of Immigration and Passports' additional director general said in August 2026 that because e-passports take all ten fingerprints, the same person can no longer get a passport under a different identity (see [The passport office](#the-passport-office)) <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.
- **Fake players and officials:** some sports federations and associations have for years been alleged to take fake players and officials abroad with their teams as a way of trafficking people, mostly in the smaller sports, Prothom Alo reported in October 2025 <sup>[N497](#n497)</sup>. Until October 2025, federations sent the NSC (National Sports Council) only the players' names, and no one checked whether each was really a player; the chair of the Bangladesh Olympic Association's athletes commission said she often hears that some federations take fake players along <sup>[N497](#n497)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-11"></a>

#### 4.14.2 What happens

- **At Dhaka airport:** in the February 2024 case DB said low-ranking employees of agencies working at the airport and people from private airlines had formed a ring that sent people to the Middle East, Europe and elsewhere on fake visas for lakhs of taka; one of the two alleged ringleaders arrested was a booking assistant of "Qatar Airlines", as the report names it, and the other a security worker at the airport <sup>[N496 (2024)](#n496)</sup>. Police seized three passports, three fake visas, a special card for entering the airport, phones, e-tickets, and work permit and visa papers <sup>[N496 (2024)](#n496)</sup>.
- **At the airport abroad:** the two men with fake Schengen visas were detained at Rome airport for two days and deported <sup>[N365](#n365)</sup>. In June 2026 a Biman ground officer was arrested, accused of helping them board <sup>[N355](#n355)</sup>.
- **At the travel agency:** the forged Chattogram police clearance was detected when Zakir Hossain handed it to a travel agency; he complained in writing to the Chattogram District Superintendent of Police's office, and a case was filed at Banshkhali police station with his brother as plaintiff <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>.
- **At the embassy:** Italy suspended all Bangladeshi work nulla osta in October 2024 because of the number of false documents, and refuses or revokes a nulla osta obtained by fraud <sup>[191 (2024)](#o191), [91](#o91)</sup>. The Italian Embassy says forged documents mean immediate refusal, and the visa centre's rules say applicants caught with fake papers are banned from applying and reported to the police <sup>[N498](#n498), [192 (2019)](#o192)</sup>. In February 2024 the US Embassy in Dhaka filed a case against five Bangladeshis after two of them, Mofizur Rahman and Ashraful Alam Bhuiyan, admitted at their US visa interview on 11 February that they trafficked people from Bangladesh to various countries; they had submitted fake documents describing themselves as garment businessmen <sup>[N339 (2024)](#n339), [N338 (2024)](#n338)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-10"></a>

#### 4.14.3 The law

- **Bangladesh:** making, obtaining, supplying or holding a document to help migrant smuggling carries 1 to 3 years' rigorous imprisonment and a fine of at least Tk 50,000 under the 2026 Act <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>. A trafficking victim cannot be charged for getting or holding a false travel document as a direct result of the trafficking <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **Destinations:** Italy punishes forging or using a forged visa with 1 to 6 years in prison; Malaysia treats a holder of forged travel documents as a prohibited immigrant; Singapore jailed a worker in 2021 who bought a forged degree through an agent <sup>[181](#o181), [185 (2006)](#o185), [N499 (2021)](#n499)</sup>.
- **Travellers are charged too:** in the February 2024 Dhaka airport case, the three men trying to travel on fake visas were arrested with the two alleged ringleaders, a case of fraud and forgery was filed against them, and a court granted one day's remand <sup>[N496 (2024)](#n496)</sup>.
- **The Chattogram police clearance ring:** police arrested four members, Md. Ashraf Ali Khan, Md. Nishan, Mohammad Abdullah Al Mamun and Md. Abdur Rahman, on 16 August 2026 in Chattogram city and Banshkhali; police said the four were mainly brokers and were looking for the others <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>.
- **Sports teams:** since 9 October 2025 the NSC requires a federation's proposal for the government order (GO) letting a team go abroad to reach it at least 10 days before the flight, with proof of the players' fitness and performance; an NSC source said the rule is mainly to stop trafficking, while its director (sports) said only that the question of trafficking in the name of sport does come up <sup>[N497](#n497)</sup>.

<a id="where-to-get-help-9"></a>

#### 4.14.4 Where to get help

CID asks the public to report forged visas and migrant smuggling directly to it (see [Where victims and families get help](#where-victims-and-families-get-help)) <sup>[N365](#n365)</sup>.

<a id="asylum-claims-built-on-a-false-story"></a>

### 4.15 Asylum claims built on a false story

<a id="how-it-is-sold-13"></a>

#### 4.15.1 How it is sold

- **The package.** A BBC investigation published in April 2026 reported that lawyers and advisers in the UK were helping some migrants claim asylum as gay, and secretly recorded a legal adviser, Tanisa Khan, saying she could arrange a "full package" including photos taken at gay clubs and other evidence <sup>[N500](#n500)</sup>.
- **The second claim.** The UK Home Office said many Bangladeshi men first claimed asylum on political grounds and, after refusal, claimed again as gay; a ring is believed to have helped prepare dozens of claims by Bangladeshi men saying they were gay and that their lives would be in danger if returned <sup>[N500](#n500)</sup>.
- **The price and contents.** According to the head of the Home Office's regional criminal investigations, the suspects charged each asylum seeker GBP 1,000 to 4,500 for fake papers, which could include support letters from Apnajon, a Bangladeshi LGBT organisation in Whitechapel, east London, staged photos meant to show the applicant as gay, and letters from someone claiming to be in a same-sex relationship with the applicant <sup>[N500](#n500)</sup>.
- **Canada.** For people who reached Canada on visit visas built on one set of documents and then claimed asylum on a different story, see Visit and tourist visas sold as jobs <sup>[N406 (2024)](#n406)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-12"></a>

#### 4.15.2 What happens

- **Detection.** Apnajon came under suspicion after caseworkers saw the same kind of information repeated across several suspicious claims, followed by months of investigation <sup>[N500](#n500)</sup>.
- **Arrests.** On 6 October 2026 Home Office immigration officers raided several London addresses and arrested two people on suspicion of fraud and immigration offences, accused of making fake documents, including staged photos and forged papers, for up to GBP 4,500 each; phones, jewellery and thousands of pounds were seized, and a third person was arrested on suspicion of staying illegally <sup>[N500](#n500)</sup>.
- **Claims under review.** The Home Office was reviewing more than a hundred asylum claims linked to the ring in October 2026, and Chris Foster, its regional head of criminal investigations, said some fake claims had undoubtedly already been approved <sup>[N500](#n500)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-11"></a>

#### 4.15.3 The law

The UK Home Secretary said in October 2026 that fraudulent asylum claims would be cancelled and the claimants removed from the country, and that those who profited would be prosecuted and their earnings seized; the UK government says conviction for asylum fraud can carry up to 10 years in prison <sup>[N500](#n500)</sup>.

<a id="the-law-in-bangladesh"></a>

### 4.16 The law in Bangladesh

<a id="the-2026-trafficking-and-smuggling-act"></a>

#### 4.16.1 The 2026 trafficking and smuggling Act

The Human Trafficking and Migrant Smuggling Prevention and Suppression Act, 2026 (Act No. 55 of 2026), passed in April 2026, repealed the Prevention and Suppression of Human Trafficking Act 2012 and took effect at once <sup>[N458](#n458), [165](#o165)</sup>. It replaced an ordinance of the same name, first approved by the interim government's Council of Advisers on 27 November 2025 <sup>[N501](#n501), [165](#o165)</sup>. For the first time it separates trafficking and smuggling as two crimes <sup>[N502](#n502)</sup>. It applies to anyone who commits one of its offences against a Bangladeshi abroad <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>. A Home Ministry joint secretary said in July 2026 that it also covers fraud through fake online job adverts, and that the biggest challenge now is enforcing it and preventing fraud inside the regular migration process <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

- **Trafficking:** at least 5 years' rigorous imprisonment, up to life, and a fine of at least Tk 50,000; by an organised gang, death or life imprisonment, or at least 7 years, and a fine of at least Tk 5 lakh <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **What counts as exploitation:** among other things, forced labour, debt bondage, forcing someone into crimes such as online scamming, and holding someone to demand ransom <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **Migrant smuggling** (getting someone into another country illegally for gain): 3 to 10 years' rigorous imprisonment and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh; if the migrant is gravely hurt, dies, is put in danger or treated cruelly, 5 years to life <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **Documents and ads:** making, obtaining, supplying or holding a document to help smuggling, 1 to 3 years; advertising for smuggling online, on social media or in print, 3 to 7 years and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **Victims:** a trafficking victim cannot be charged for illegal entry or stay abroad, or for getting or holding a false travel or identity document, if it was a direct result of the trafficking <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>. Threatening a victim, the family or a witness carries 3 to 7 years, and pressuring a victim or family to settle 2 to 5 years <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **Filing a case:** anyone can complain at a police station or to the tribunal; the police must protect the complainant and keep their identity confidential <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>. All offences are cognisable, non-bailable and can't be settled privately <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **Powers:** on the investigator's application the tribunal can freeze bank accounts, block the sale of property, and bar an accused from leaving the country for up to 60 days; an accused who absconds can be tried in absence <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>.
- **Courts:** the government can set up a Human Trafficking and Migrant Smuggling Suppression Tribunal in each district, and it can try a connected offence in the same case <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>. In 2026 Justice and Care, an NGO, was supporting about 400 cases in 18 tribunals <sup>[N469](#n469)</sup>.

<a id="the-overseas-employment-and-migrants-act-2013"></a>

#### 4.16.2 The Overseas Employment and Migrants Act 2013

- **False job promises (s.31):** taking money on a false promise of a job abroad, or keeping a migrant's passport or papers, is punished by up to 5 years in prison and a fine of at least Tk 1 lakh <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Visa trading (s.33, amended 2023):** getting a demand letter, visa or work permit illegally, or buying or selling one in Bangladesh, is punished by 2 to 7 years' rigorous imprisonment <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Unregistered dalals (s.35, added 2023):** acting as an agency's sub-agent without registration is punished by 1 year in prison or a fine of Tk 50,000 to 2 lakh <sup>[44 (2023)](#o44)</sup>. Agencies may appoint only registered sub-agents <sup>[44 (2023)](#o44)</sup>.
- **Leaving only by air:** BMET's website, as cited by Prothom Alo in July 2025, says a worker must leave with BMET's emigration clearance (smart card) by lawful means, and that going by sea or by any route other than the three international airports, Dhaka, Chattogram and Sylhet, is a punishable offence <sup>[N74](#n74)</sup>. Speakers at a BRAC and Prothom Alo roundtable in Sylhet in April 2026 said there is no lawful way to go abroad by sea, and that no one can lawfully go abroad for work except through the Dhaka, Chattogram and Sylhet airports <sup>[N334](#n334)</sup>.
- **No bail:** offences under ss.31, 33 and 34 are non-bailable and can't be settled privately <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Complaint without a court case (s.41):** anyone cheated can complain to the government against any person, including an agency <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Legal aid (s.27):** migrants and people defrauded in the name of migration have a right to legal aid <sup>[26](#o26)</sup>.
- **Mobile courts** under the Act give immediate remedies to would-be and returned migrants, free, in 7 working days <sup>[49](#o49)</sup>.
- **Licences cancelled or withdrawn:** according to Ministry sources in July 2026, an agency's licence is cancelled when it breaks the licence conditions and withdrawn when it commits a serious offence or is a threat to national security; getting a withdrawn licence back may take a legal fight <sup>[N269](#n269)</sup>.
- **Women:** Farida Yasmin, a Supreme Court lawyer advising the Bangladesh Migrant Women Workers Association, said in January 2024 that the amended Act still does not separately address the protection of women <sup>[N28 (2024)](#n28)</sup>.

<a id="what-happens-to-trafficking-cases"></a>

#### 4.16.3 What happens to trafficking cases

- **2018 to 2025 (CID):** the Trafficking in Human Being (THB) unit of the CID (the police's Criminal Investigation Department) counts 1,657 people charged in 536 cases, of whom only 353 could be arrested <sup>[N18](#n18)</sup>.
- **2020 to 2024 (Home Ministry):** in about 4,400 to 4,500 cases under the 2012 Act, 94 to 95% of the accused were acquitted; a Prothom Alo editorial added that in most incidents no case is filed at all <sup>[N24](#n24), [N313](#n313)</sup>.
- **2021 and 2022 (Home Ministry sources):** no accused was convicted in a single trafficking case <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>.
- **January to September 2023 (Home Ministry):** courts disposed of 332 cases, and in 316 of them (95.18%) all the accused were acquitted; 12,279 accused were acquitted, and in the 16 cases that ended in punishment 56 people were sentenced, two of them to life <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254), [N339 (2024)](#n339)</sup>. In the same nine months 683 new cases were filed, with charge sheets in 31 and final reports in 18; of 30,539 cases in courts up to September 2023, 25,000 were under trial and 5,327 more under investigation <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>. Cases under the Act had been filed against 289,472 people, of whom 132,638 had been arrested <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>.
- **2023 (US State Department's 2024 trafficking report):** the government identified 1,210 trafficking victims (210 of sex trafficking, 795 of forced labour, 205 other), while civil society and international organisations counted at least 10,135, 1,784 of them of sex trafficking; the report says the government does not regularly publish its anti-trafficking data <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- **2024 (Home Ministry):** 1,135 cases were filed at police stations and 4,291 were pending, 1,310 of them under investigation; one third of all the accused had been arrested; trials of earlier cases ended with 5 people sentenced to life, 55 to other terms and 1,250 acquitted <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
- **2025 to May 2026 (Home Ministry):** 1,126 new cases were filed in 2025, and up to May 2026 5,284 cases were pending, 3,306 under trial and 1,978 under investigation <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported that in 2026, 71 new cases were filed in January, 66 in February, 110 in March, 130 in April and 123 in May, while only 17 were disposed of in those five months <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.

<a id="why-cases-fail"></a>

#### 4.16.4 Why cases fail

- **Investigation:** experts told Prothom Alo in January 2024 that accused are acquitted because cases are not investigated properly, witnesses can't be produced in court, and cases are settled with the trafficking rings for money <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>. Salma Ali, president of BNWLA (the Bangladesh National Women Lawyers' Association), said then that trafficking cases are very profitable for the police: investigating officers take money from both the victim and the accused and hold up the investigation for a long time, and ring members, who are very influential, threaten victims into a compromise <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254), [N339 (2024)](#n339)</sup>.
- **No dedicated investigators:** the legal coordinator of RMMRU (the Refugee and Migratory Movements Research Unit) said in July 2026 that the trafficking tribunals have no designated investigation agency of their own, so investigation reports are late and often no proper investigation is done, and that the Dhaka tribunal had had no judge for five months <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Time and money:** he said a case takes two to three years to go from investigation to trial, many victims can't keep it going because they spent lakhs to go abroad and have nothing left, and many lose a day's income every time they go to court <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>. Salma Ali said in July 2026 that cases drag on for lack of skilled public prosecutors and judges <sup>[N234](#n234)</sup>.
- **Confidentiality:** she also said many victims stop pursuing a case in the end over the question of confidentiality, so organised criminals get away, and that trafficking in the name of migration continues through land ports, airports and sea routes <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **Known traffickers left alone:** the chairperson of OKUP (Ovibashi Karmi Unnayan Program, a migrant workers' organisation) said in July 2026 that at a recent meeting in Madaripur the deputy commissioner (the district's top administrator) said he knows the traffickers but has no power to act against them <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- **What investigators are told to establish:** after no convictions in 2021 and 2022 the Home Ministry instructed investigators that trafficking happens in six stages from going abroad to coming back, and to establish who trafficked the victim, who introduced the victim to the ring, whether the victim was threatened, taken by force or trafficked by exploiting a vulnerability, and the route the ring used <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>.

<a id="cases-4"></a>

#### 4.16.5 Cases

- 2019 to 2025: OKUP supported the case of an abused woman who came back from the Middle East in 2019; a long investigation found elements of trafficking, in 2025 the special tribunal's judge proposed an out-of-court settlement, and though she said she wanted justice and dignity, not money, the case was settled for a little over Tk 4 lakh, according to OKUP's chairperson <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.
- January 2024, Savar: the father of a woman trafficked to India on a promise of Canada said that after he filed a trafficking case the accused filed a false case against him at Savar police station, he was arrested and spent four months in jail at a cost of Tk 5 to 6 lakh, and he had heard all the accused were acquitted <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254), [N339 (2024)](#n339)</sup>. He said the investigating officer told him many times he would be taken to court to testify but never took him, and that two of the acquitted were back in the area; the sub-inspector who investigated said the case had long since gone to the CID and he had no update <sup>[N254 (2024)](#n254)</sup>.
- July 2026: Mannan Soyal, back from a scam centre in Cambodia, told a Prothom Alo roundtable in Dhaka that after he filed a trafficking case against the people who cheated him, the accused filed a counter-case, so he is now himself an accused and has no money to fight it, and he alleged the fraudsters were pressing him to take money and withdraw <sup>[N234](#n234), [N190](#n190)</sup>.

<a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help"></a>

### 4.17 Where victims and families get help

1. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-1"></a>**In danger in Bangladesh:** call 999, the police's national emergency line, free, 24 hours <sup>[144](#o144)</sup>.
2. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-2"></a>**File a criminal case with the local police.** CID asks returnees to file cases with their local police so the brokers and agencies can be prosecuted <sup>[N350](#n350)</sup>. Under the 2026 Act a case can be filed at a police station or directly with the tribunal, and the police must protect the complainant and keep their identity confidential <sup>[165](#o165)</sup>. A Chandpur family's case in December 2025 led to an arrest <sup>[N368](#n368)</sup>. Two youths trafficked to Cambodia came home on 3 September 2026, a case naming two men was filed at Paltan police station, Dhaka, on 26 October, and the CID arrested both the next day, according to the CID <sup>[N455](#n455)</sup>. In 2020 to 2024, 94 to 95% of the accused in trafficking cases were acquitted (see [What happens to trafficking cases](#what-happens-to-trafficking-cases)) <sup>[N24](#n24)</sup>.
3. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-3"></a>**Report to the CID.** The CID's Trafficking in Human Being (THB) Unit leads trafficking investigations nationwide and asks the public to report trafficking, forged visas and smuggling to it <sup>[193](#o193), [N365](#n365)</sup>.
4. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-4"></a>**Airport police.** A deputy inspector general of the Airport Armed Police Battalion (APBn) said in August 2026 that it counsels trafficking victims and returnees, tries to get them rehabilitated through BRAC and other organisations, takes and investigates their cases, has brought victims back from Iran, Libya and Greece, and tries to arrest accused people in Bangladesh even when the crime happened abroad <sup>[N107](#n107)</sup>.
5. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-5"></a>**Call the Probash-Bondhu call centre** of the Wage Earners' Welfare Board, 24 hours: 16135 free in Bangladesh, +8809610102030 from abroad <sup>[101](#o101)</sup>.
6. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-6"></a>**Apply to the Welfare Board.**
   - **District office:** the Probashi Kallyan Centre is the Board's district office; in Cumilla it began work in July 2023, taking over services the district manpower office used to give, and an application through it for a migrant in trouble abroad gets legal help with the embassy's assistance <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>.
   - **Membership:** a Board director said in May 2026 that anyone, documented or undocumented, can become a member for Tk 400, and that the Board gives a member who falls ill, is jailed or is in danger abroad legal help through the embassy's labour wing <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>. He also said that with a Tk 300 membership a dead worker's body is brought home from any country, Tk 35,000 is paid for burial and transport, a further Tk 3 lakh goes to the heirs, and up to Tk 10 lakh more is paid if there is insurance (see [If a worker dies abroad](#if-a-worker-dies-abroad-what-does-the-family-get), what does the family get?) <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>.
   - **Money for illness, injury and children:** a worker who applies because of illness within one year of coming home gets a one-time grant of up to Tk 2 lakh (see [What help is there if I'm injured or fall ill abroad?](#what-help-is-there-if-im-injured-or-fall-ill-abroad)), and a family gets Tk 2,000 a month for up to seven years for each disabled child, for at most two children <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>. A worker injured or maimed abroad gets up to Tk 4 lakh against a Tk 500 premium, and a five-year life insurance policy costs Tk 1,000 <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>.
   - **Unpaid money abroad:** the Board helps recover dues owed abroad through 22 law firms in 12 countries, and has given more than 239,000 people training, counselling and financial help under its reintegration policy <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>. The Cumilla centre's assistant director said the Board helps through the embassy to recover unpaid insurance and compensation, that embassies can appoint lawyers, that safe homes for women workers operate abroad, that there is a dormitory for overnight stays near the airport, and that the Board gives education stipends to migrants' children and an allowance for disabled children <sup>[N64](#n64)</sup>.
   - **An example:** on an application by Anirban Survivor Voice Foundation, the Board wrote to the labour wing of the Bangladesh embassy in Bangkok asking it to arrange the release and quick return of 30 Bangladeshis held in Cambodian immigration detention; they flew home on 20 October 2026 <sup>[N464](#n464)</sup>.
7. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-7"></a>**Complain against an agency to BMET** (Bureau of Manpower, Employment and Training): a written application with proof to the Director General <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>. Director (Emigration): +8802222222415, 01711-135901, diremi@bmet.gov.bd <sup>[10](#o10)</sup>. Hotline of OEP (the Overseas Employment Platform, BMET's online system): 09614445271 <sup>[58](#o58)</sup>.
8. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-8"></a>**Complain to the Ministry of Expatriates' Welfare:** enforcement branch, with proof, your address and the agency's licence (RL) number <sup>[49](#o49)</sup>. From abroad, email the complaint cell at minister.expat@gmail.com <sup>[102](#o102)</sup>.
9. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-9"></a>**If the family is threatened at home,** apply to the Welfare Board directly or through the expatriate welfare branch of the Deputy Commissioner's (district administration) office or the DEMO (District Employment and Manpower Office); the Board writes to the local authorities <sup>[194](#o194)</sup>.
10. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-10"></a>**District and union committees:** Cox's Bazar has a District Vigilance Task Force Committee and a District Migration Coordination Committee against unsafe migration, and counter-trafficking committees exist down to union level, though an NGO head there said they need to be made active (see [Local committees and mediation](#local-committees-and-mediation)) <sup>[N99](#n99)</sup>.
11. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-11"></a>**At the airport on return:** the Welfare Board's desk at Dhaka airport, +88 02 8901040, +880 1971 430203 <sup>[76](#o76)</sup>. Returnees from Cambodia and Europe got emergency help, counselling and money to get home there from BRAC's Migration Programme <sup>[N456](#n456), [N398](#n398)</sup>. BRAC's Migration Programme said in March 2025 that 250 to 300 migrant workers a day were coming back empty-handed, without proper clothes or money, and that it gives each Tk 1,000 for the fare home <sup>[N149](#n149)</sup>.
12. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-12"></a>**BRAC Migration Programme:** airport help, counselling, money or materials to restart earning, and legal help, for returnees and trafficking survivors <sup>[166](#o166)</sup>. It said in April 2026 that it had helped about 39,000 people at the airport in the previous eight years, and gives returnees reintegration support and training, and then counselling, and is trying to set them up as entrepreneurs <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>. At Dhaka's Hazrat Shahjalal International Airport, when a mentally ill worker or one with no address is found, the Probashi Kallyan desk or the Airport Armed Police phone Al-Amin (Nayan), manager of BRAC's Migration Welfare Centre, who by the US State Department's account at its June 2024 TIP (Trafficking in Persons) Hero award had helped at least 34,000 returnees and trafficking victims <sup>[N503 (2024)](#n503)</sup>. Al-Amin said he was himself trafficked when he went to work in Malaysia, which Prothom Alo reported was in 2007; he came home the same year, started work in BRAC's migration programme in 2017, and is president of Anirban Survivor Voice, the largest network of trafficking survivors in Bangladesh <sup>[N503 (2024)](#n503)</sup>. Roksana Akter of Bishwanath, Sylhet, abused at her workplace in Saudi Arabia and forced to borrow to come home, said in April 2026 that the EU and BRAC's Prottasha-2 project helped her set up a shop whose income now runs her household <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>. BRAC Information Center: Rahman Villa (2nd floor), 402 Ashkona, opposite the airport, Dakshinkhan, Dhaka; 01700797182, 01700797183 <sup>[N504](#n504)</sup>.
13. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-13"></a>**Women trafficked to India:** BNWLA (the Bangladesh National Women Lawyers' Association) rehabilitated a woman from near the Jashore border rescued from an Indian brothel, BGB (Border Guard Bangladesh) has handed victims it rescued to Justice and Care, an NGO, and Justice and Care said in early 2025 that the return of at least 10 women and girls trafficked across the border since August 2024 and rescued by Indian police was under way <sup>[N220](#n220)</sup>.
14. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-14"></a>**IOM** (International Organization for Migration, the UN migration agency) runs the return flights from Libya with the Embassy and the Ministries <sup>[N366](#n366), [N361](#n361)</sup>.
15. <a id="where-victims-and-families-get-help-item-15"></a>**Embassies:** Bangkok for Cambodia and Thailand, and for people rescued from Myawaddy in Myanmar; Tashkent for Kyrgyzstan; Rome for Italy; Kuala Lumpur for Malaysia; Doha, +974 44671927, hotline +974 33662000 <sup>[187](#o187), [N459](#n459), [N483 (2024)](#n483), [175](#o175), [186](#o186), [195](#o195)</sup>.

The Home Ministry said in July 2026 it was working on a national referral system and a National Plan of Action 2026-30 so that victims get protection, legal aid and services in a coordinated way <sup>[N190](#n190)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What phone or email does the CID's THB Unit give the public for trafficking tips?
> - Is the Welfare Board membership fee Tk 400 or Tk 300, and how does a worker already abroad without clearance pay it?
> - What number does the Airport Armed Police Battalion give victims who want to file a case?
> - What numbers do BNWLA and Justice and Care give families of women trafficked to India?
